From smart party speakers to even smarter mixers, I’ve rounded up some of my favorite connected home gadgets you can grab on sale this weekend.

From smart party speakers to even smarter mixers, I’ve rounded up some of my favorite connected home gadgets you can grab on sale this weekend.

I’ve spent my post-turkey haze powering through reams of deals on smart home gadgets to find the best discounts on some of my favorite products.

Smart home is often one of the best categories to shop around Black Friday, as it’s traditionally when robot vacuums, video doorbells, smart locks, and thermostats see their lowest prices of the year. And this year does not disappoint! Roborock’s flagship Saros 20 is under $1,000 for the first time, and Ecobee’s excellent Smart Thermostat Premium has finally dropped under $200 (for Costco members and today only).

I also spotted some great first-time discounts on the latest Bird Buddy smart bird feeder I reviewed this summer, along with the new Alexa digital photo frame I’m currently reviewing. This time of year is also a good opportunity to grab a bargain on higher-priced items you may have been eyeing for a while, such as Skylight’s giant 27-inch digital wall calendar, GE Profile’s fun smart mixer, and Aqara’s new high-tech UWB smart door lock that unlocks as you approach.

Here are some of the best deals I’ve spotted so far. We’ll keep this list updated, so check back often!

This RGB smart lamp packs a JBL speaker inside to combine snazzy lighting effects and decent sound into one fun, portable package. Battery-powered and with support for Matter, this is a smart light with party speaker aspirations. It’s the perfect size to add a little punch to your next gathering or snazz up a college dorm room. Read our review.

LIFX’s dimmable, color-changing bulbs offer Wi-Fi connectivity, simple setup, and Matter compatibility, so they work with any major smart home platform. The company is currently trialing a Thread beta that will bring the option to add Thread connectivity to all its Matter devices.

A 10-inch anti-glare touchscreen that comes in a few design options. It’s a frame that features Alexa, and doubles as a smart speaker. It works with Amazon Photos, which offers unlimited storage for Prime members.

This innovative new smart lock uses the U1 ultra-wideband (UWB) chip in many iPhones and Apple Watches to automatically unlock your door as you approach. It also works with the new Aliro standard, making it compatible with Android phones for hands-free unlocking. Read our review.

The Birdbuddy Nook is the company’s least expensive and smallest model, and it packs a lot of bang for your buck. Its 4K HDR camera delivers excellent image quality and a 3.8-cup seed capacity means less frequent refills. An optional solar panel extends the already good battery life. Read our review.

Roborock’s first roller-mop robot vacuum, the Curv 2 Flow, features flagship specs at a midrange price, including excellent mopping, a retractable mop cover, 20,000Pa suction, AI-powered obstacle avoidance, and a compact design. Read our review.

These smart lights have beautiful colors, a nice selection of festive scenes, and an easy-to-use app. They are super reliable when paired with a Hue Bridge and also work over Bluetooth. They come in three lengths, work with all the major smart home platforms, and are Matter-compatible. Read our review.

A digital touchscreen display designed to help families manage calendars, to-do lists, meals, and chores. It connects to Wi-Fi, features a 27-inch screen that can display photos when not in use. Read our review.

Power over Ethernet provides this 2K resolution video doorbell with rock-solid connectivity while reducing the number of wires you need. There’s no subscription required to view recorded video: it can record locally on a microSD card, Reolink Hub, or FTP server and connect to a platform like Home Assistant.

Even if your mom lives halfway around the world, the Aura Aspen can help her feel close. The 12-inch display looks more akin to a traditional photo frame than other models, in part due to an antiglare screen, slim bezels, and a 4:3 aspect ratio. It can also automatically showcase images via Google Photos, iCloud Photos, or a companion app, the latter of which even allows for text captions. Read our review.

The Narwal Flow 2 is an excellent robot vacuum for hardwood floors. A superb mop with a wide flat roller pad and a good vacuum, the Flow 2 also has top-notch navigation and obstacle detection. Its sleek base station is also less of an eyesore than most models. Read our review.

This kit is everything you need to get started with Lutron’s excellent smart lighting system and turn your existing wired lights into smart ones that you can control from anywhere. It replaces your standard wall switch and works with all the major smart home systems, making it easy to dim or adjust multiple lights from your wall switch, your phone, or with your voice.

This smart plug offers the most detailed energy monitoring of any we’ve tested. It supports Matter over Thread and is a good choice for Apple Home and SmartThings users, as it offers energy monitoring on those platforms.

This smart mixer is great for the tech-forward baker. With a built-in digital scale, Auto Sense and Active Stir to take some of the busy work off your hands, it features a high-powered motor with a 7-quart bowl. It’s compatible with all front-mounted KitchenAid attachments. Read our review.

SwitchBot’s Matter-compatible Smart Candle Warmer lets you set schedules to turn it on and off in the app or any connected smart home platform and have pleasant scents waft safely throughout your home on demand.

At more than half off, Ring’s 2K battery-powered video doorbell is a great deal offering 2K video and easy installation. However, its battery is built in, so you’ll need to remove the doorbell to charge it via USB-C.

The Echo Dot Max features a flat design and a redesigned sound system, which delivers nearly three times the bass as its predecessor. It also offers improved conversation detection and better noise filtering, thanks to Amazon’s new AZ3 chip. Read our review.

This bot can climb high transitions between rooms and get over obstacles, meaning it’s less likely to get stuck than other bots. The Saros 20 is also really slim, just 3.13 inches tall, small enough to squeeze under most furniture, and it’s equipped with excellent obstacle avoidance making it a great robot for cluttered households. Read our review.

This fun smart lamp can light up your ceiling or cast a warm white reading light over a chair, making it super versatile. It has three separate lighting zones that can display white or color-changing lights and offers a huge array of dynamic effects. It supports Matter, so will work with all the major smart home platforms.

The Ecobee Smart Thermostat Premium is an excellent option for adding smarts and some style to your HVAC system. As well as being a smart way to control your energy use, the thermostat can also act as a video intercom for Ecobee’s excellent doorbell camera and an indoor air quality monitor. Read our review.

#156. Duterte's ICC trial: What is at stake for the Philippines?

Former Philippine President Rodrigo Duterte faces trial in The Hague over his deadly war on drugs. The proceedings could determine the political fortunes of a powerful dynasty. Victims' families hope to see justice.

Former Philippine President Rodrigo Duterte will stand trial at the International Criminal Court (ICC) in The Hague on November 30, where he faces three counts of crimes against humanity linked to his bloody war on drugs.

For the families of the thousands killed during his anti-narcotics campaign, the trial is a chance for justice that has eluded them at home.

It is also a significant test of international justice at a time when the ICC faces increasing political pressure and questions about its ability to hold former heads of state accountable.

Yet, Duterte, 81, is still a popular figure in the Philippines, where many still regard his violent crackdown as one of his presidency's greatest achievements.

With his daughter, Vice President Sara Duterte, a frontrunner for the 2028 presidential election, the proceedings in The Hague could become a defining issue in the Philippines' next presidential election.

Rodrigo Duterte was elected president in 2016 following an expletive-laden, populist campaign in which he openly promised to kill criminals and drug dealers.

During his more than two decades as mayor of Davao City, he earned a reputation for transforming a notoriously violent city into a relatively safe one.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Human rights groups, however, found systematic extrajudicial killings by the so-called Davao Death Squad, which ICC prosecutors allege Duterte helped establish and oversee.

His disregard for political convention and promises of swift, uncompromising justice appealed to millions of Filipinos frustrated by corruption, crime and the perceived failures of successive governments.

"Filipinos were deeply frustrated with everyday experiences of injustice and government inaction; [that] made them receptive to Duterte's promise of swift justice, even through extraordinary and violent means," Cleve Arguelles, a political scientist and CEO of Momentum Research, a pollster, told DW.

After assuming the presidency, Duterte extended his Davao law enforcement model nationwide. Police launched sweeping anti-narcotics operations, primarily targeting impoverished urban communities, while suspected drug users and dealers were also killed by unidentified gunmen.

Philippine authorities officially recorded 6,252 deaths during anti-drug operations, with police claiming to have acted in self-defense. Human rights organizations estimate the actual death toll at between 12,000 and 30,000.

"Duterte's ruthless war on drugs was not simply a campaign against crime; it was a way of governing through killing," David Johnson, a professor of sociology at the University of Hawaii at Manoa, told DW.

"The extraordinary violence he ordered and encouraged was sustained by a combination of extraordinary problems of impunity in the Philippine criminal justice system and deep failures of democracy in that country," Johnson said. "These conditions made extrajudicial killing politically useful even though it was ineffective as crime control."

Duterte also used state power to intimidate and suppress wide sections of Philippine society, including opposition politicians, civil society groups, journalists, lawyers and judges, Diane Desierto, a professor of law and global affairs at the University of Notre Dame in the US, told DW.

The deterioration of human rights under Duterte damaged the Philippines' international standing and strained relations with its traditional Western partners, particularly the United States under President Barack Obama and the European Union, which threatened to withdraw Manila's preferential trade privilege.

Duterte left office in June 2022 as one of the most popular presidents in recent Philippine history. A survey conducted by pollster Social Weather Stations found that 88% of Filipinos were satisfied with his performance.

His daughter was overwhelmingly elected vice president in May 2022, while their allies dominated that year's Senate elections.

"Up to now, the Dutertes remain popular not in spite of the drug war but because of it," Arguelles said.

Despite years of domestic and international scrutiny, Duterte remained at liberty for nearly three years after leaving office.

The ICC opened a preliminary examination into the drug war in 2018, prompting Duterte to withdraw the Philippines from the court's founding treaty. A formal investigation began in September 2021.

For much of his early presidency, President Ferdinand Marcos refused to cooperate with the ICC investigation.

What changed was the breakdown of relations between the Marcoses and the Dutertes, two of the country's most powerful political dynasties.

Marcos' father had ruled the Philippines as a dictator until he was overthrown in the 1986 People Power Revolution.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The alliance that brought Marcos Jr. and Sara Duterte to power in 2022 unraveled spectacularly after November 2024, when the vice president claimed she had arranged for Marcos to be assassinated in the event of her own killing. She has since faced successive impeachment efforts.

Philippine authorities arrested Rodrigo Duterte in March 2025, acting on an ICC arrest warrant issued four days earlier.

In April this year, ICC judges confirmed three charges of crimes against humanity involving murder and attempted murder.

The trial will begin with opening statements, followed by testimony from prosecution witnesses. The process is expected to be extensive, potentially running until 2028, the year of the next Philippine presidential elections.

The Philippines is effectively confronting two different questions about the Duterte legacy: whether Rodrigo Duterte should be held criminally accountable for his presidency and whether his family should be entrusted with political power again, Arguelles said.

Duterte-aligned senators implicated in the ICC investigation could face arrest and transfer to The Hague, while Sara Duterte is still a leading contender for the 2028 presidency, despite an ongoing impeachment trial over allegations of unexplained wealth that could see her barred from public office.

Her brother, Sebastian Duterte, the mayor of Davao City, has also become a possible alternative presidential candidate.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

By portraying the ICC proceedings as foreign interference and political persecution, the Duterte family hopes to preserve the political capital accumulated during Rodrigo Duterte's presidency, Jayson Lamchek, a legal scholar at Western Sydney University, told DW.

Before her father's arrest, Sara Duterte's performance and trust ratings had been declining amid allegations that she had misused confidential government funds.

However, her ratings rebounded following his detention and have largely held up despite the ongoing impeachment controversies, Ronald Holmes, a political science professor at De La Salle University in the Philippines and president of polling firm Pulse Asia Research, told DW.

"A conviction or prolonged trial near election season will likely turn the 2028 vote into a direct referendum on the Duterte legacy, either rallying sympathetic voters against perceived foreign and domestic persecution or galvanizing opposition forces around demands for accountability," Holmes said.

For many Filipinos, however, the trial's significance goes well beyond the political rivalry between the two dynasties.

"Rodrigo Duterte's trial in the ICC is a milestone not only for the victims and families of the war on drugs but for the entire country," Jean Encinas-Franco, a professor of political science at the University of the Philippines Diliman, told DW.

"Duterte and his cabal ruled by fear and misogyny and held Filipinos under his iron grip," she said. "The Filipino people deserve an answer. The Filipino people deserve justice."

#155. The best Black Friday deals Ars could find

Perhaps some commerce, to help all the Turkey Day leftovers go down easier?

Every year has a Prime Day—some years even have two Prime Days!—where we celebrate the life and achievements of the greatest leader the Autobots have ever known: the inimitable Optimus Prime. But just as every dawn is twinned by a dusk that steals its light, so too did Optimus Prime’s bright spark fade, extinguished by the foul hand of Megatron during the Great Decepticon Incursion of 2005.

It is a day that lives in infamy forever, and we all know the tale—how the evil Megatron (cursed be the flow of his energon!) commandeered an Autobot shuttle, cruelly murdered its crew, and launched a cowardly surprise attack against Autobot City—a plan utterly bereft of honor, as was so typical of Megatron’s schemes. Even as individual Autobots strove to secure Autobot City—heroes, all of them!—hope began to fade under an onslaught that included an attack by the fearsome Devastator, against which there appeared to be no defense.

And then, at the precipice of disaster, Optimus Prime made the fateful declaration whose words still ring out to this day: “Megatron must be stopped, no matter the cost.” The fearless Autobot transformed, rolled out, and got the job done—even though that cost was high. Many brave Autobots besides Optimus Prime lost their lives during the fighting. It was truly a dark day, a black day—and so, every year on the Friday after Thanksgiving, we pause to remember the noble sacrifices made by Prime and by so many others. This is why today is known as Black Friday.

And how do we remember those Autobots who joined the Allspark so that we could live in freedom? Why, we celebrate Black Friday through commerce and buying things—just like Optimus Prime would have wanted! Below is a list of the finest deals we could locate, in a broad range of categories. The pricing is current as of (checks watch) right now, where “right now” equals the timestamp on this story’s publication. Peruse the list and see if anything in it jumps out at you—we’ll attempt to refresh it at least once throughout the day with updated pricing and stuff.

If the darkness of the Black Friday story threatens to overwhelm your soul, fear not. Optimus Prime was succeeded by Rodimus Prime, who defeated the darkness and brought hope back to all Cybertron. (We don’t talk about the brief Ultra Magnus-led Prime Interregnal Period.) Even Optimus Prime himself wouldn’t stay dead for very long—his own glorious and perhaps narratively inevitable resurrection would occur not that long after. Indeed, we will be celebrating those grand events in just a couple of days, on Cybertron Monday (or just “Cyber Monday” for short), with more shopping!

Read on for our Black Friday deals. (You can also check over on Wired for more Black Friday deals and stuff—they usually run a longer list than we do!) Enjoy!

#153. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#150. You might lose your Oura health data if you’re still using an old Android phone

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Oura is ending Android 11 support on December 8th and users who don’t upgrade will lose ‘historical’ data.

Oura is ending Android 11 support on December 8th and users who don’t upgrade will lose ‘historical’ data.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Oura will be dropping support for Android 11 on December 8th, 2026, and users may not have access to all of their data if they don’t upgrade to Android 12 or higher before the deadline. The support page now reads:

Oura will drop support for Android 11 on December 8, 2026. If you are using an Android device, be sure to update to Android 12 or higher to ensure no loss of data or features. If you decide not to upgrade, you may still be able to use some limited historical Oura App features. Please note that if you do upgrade your smartphone system later, your historical Oura data will not carry over.

Companies often end support for older OSes, and Android 11 came out in 2020, so it’s getting a bit long in the tooth. Normally, though, this just means missing out on new features or bug fixes. Losing historical health data is more surprising, and Oura offers no explanation for why the data wouldn’t be preserved. If you’ve been procrastinating updating your phone, or hemming and hawing about replacing it, now might be the time to finally take the plunge.

If you’re not quite ready to, at least make sure to head to the membership hub and export your data so you have a backup ahead of December 8th.

#147. Amazon and Meta reportedly in talks to lift blockade on AI agent Muse

Amazon may be having a change of heart about Meta’s AI agent, Muse. The two tech giants are reportedly discussing a potential deal that would allow Meta’s popular AI agent to connect directly to Amazon’s store.

The talks would mark a significant reversal from just two months ago, when Muse users discovered they were unable to purchase products on Amazon and instead received an error message when attempting to check out.

According to a Wednesday report by Fortune, citing people familiar with the discussions, Amazon and Meta have held multiple rounds of talks about the matter. Two key issues remain under negotiation: how search results would be presented and how advertising would be handled under a potential agreement.

One of Amazon’s biggest concerns is how third-party shopping agents like Muse could affect its advertising business. In October, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy argued that few customers were making purchases through outside shopping agents.

Amazon may also have been waiting for Muse to mature before agreeing to integrate it with its marketplace. If Muse places the wrong order or creates a problem with a purchase, Amazon could ultimately be left dealing with frustrated customers and unhappy sellers.

Fortune reported that executives are concerned that third-party shopping agents could weaken Amazon’s direct relationship with consumers, according to people familiar with the matter.

Any potential agreement between Amazon and Meta would reportedly require approval from both companies’ CEOs, Fortune also reported.

Meta and Amazon have not yet responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

#145. Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

#144. Informer TV owner Vucicevic arrested: Has the reckoning with Serbia's pro-Vucic media machine begun?

The arrest of media owner Dragan J. Vucicevic raises questions about whether Serbia is holding the people behind the country's pro-Vucic media accountable — or whether this is another political power struggle.

"Beat them! Break their ribs," shouted the man in the studio during a live TV broadcast.

The man in question was Dragan J. Vucicevic, editor-in-chief of the channel Informer TV, and he was urging police officers to use force against demonstrators during a major student protest in June 2025.

On November 25, Vucicevic was arrested by police on suspicion of causing panic and disorder and obstructing justice. According to the authorities, his appearances "created an atmosphere of public lynching," and he labeled 97 citizens as enemies of the state.

The authorities cite instances where he described protesters as "scum," "hyenas" and "vultures," and spread claims that the students were preparing a "bloodbath" and a "civil war."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The prosecution says that between 2023 and 2026, Vucicevic systematically used Informer TV to conduct a campaign that incited hatred and targeted activists, independent journalists and political opponents of the previous government.

Vucicevic's arrest comes at a time of profound political change in Serbia.

Following the Student List's victory in the parliamentary election in late October and the end of the Serbian Progressive Party's (SNS) 14-year rule, questions have arisen about the accountability of people who were part of the previous political and institutional system.

Critics have described Vucicevic as the mouthpiece of former President Aleksandar Vucic — a man who said publicly what the president was thinking but could not say himself because of the office he held.

"He [Vucicevic] is someone who stopped being a journalist a long time ago, and I think he crossed the line of being merely a propagandist some time ago. He was, in fact, a weapon in the hands of Aleksandar Vucic and his party," said Ivana Stevanovic of the Slavko Curuvija Foundation, a Belgrade-based organization that provides media support and fights for press freedom. It is named after a Serbian journalist who was killed in 1999.

Stevanovic told DW that Vucicevic used his media platforms to "target and endanger people, spread falsehoods that threatened their safety," all as part of a campaign against political opponents, journalists and media outlets who were critical of the government's record.

"This man showed a female student's intimate photographs live on air. He stalked people. He had his notorious 'Viewer Intelligence Service,' which secretly followed people and filmed them in their everyday lives, then turned that material into disgusting reports," she said.

"We had calls for violence, threats and insults against prosecutors who were doing their jobs and judges who were issuing lawful rulings, expressed in indescribable language. We had wiretapping, recordings of conversations in restaurants presented as conspiracies to overthrow the state. Media outlets and journalists were accused of plotting to assassinate the president, while their reporting was portrayed as treason and selling out Kosovo," she told DW, adding that "the entire television channel is one big criminal offense."

The Regulatory Authority for Electronic Media (REM) has already issued several warnings and temporarily halted the broadcasting of certain programs on Informer TV.

Journalists' and media associations, civil society organizations and representatives of academia have launched an initiative to have the channel's broadcasting license revoked.

According to the Slavko Curuvija Foundation, more than 1,000 court cases concerning the protection of reputation and honor have been brought against Informer in recent years.

"Of the cases concluded over the past three years, courts upheld the claims in 92% of them," said Stevanovic. "Compensation was awarded to people whose rights had been violated. Informer even paid damages to the same individuals several times for the same lies, which it kept publishing about them. None of these financial penalties made them stop."

On one occasion, Dragan J. Vucicevic himself asked REM to impose a financial penalty on him rather than ban part of his program.

Money, it seems, was not a problem for Insajder Tim, the company behind all Informer media platforms (a daily tabloid newspaper, a television channel and an online portal).

According to an investigation by Raskrikavanje, a fact-checking website that monitors the output of Serbian media, Insajder Tim received around €273,000 ($299,000) in public funds in 2024 alone through public procurement contracts, advertising and media project funding.

Representatives of the Student List have claimed that the state-owned telecommunications company Telekom Srbija paid Informer between €500,000 and €700,000 a month.

Telekom Srbija, which also owns a cable network distributing television channels to millions of users in Serbia, cited business confidentiality when asked about the details of its cooperation with Informer.

Vucicevic and his lawyers reject the accusations.

The defense argues that the proceedings amount to punishing someone for words they have spoken and raise questions about the boundary between freedom of expression and criminal liability.

Former President Aleksandar Vucic said Vucicevic had been arrested solely because of his journalistic work. SNS lawmakers walked out of a parliamentary session following news of the arrest and gathered outside the police station where the Informer editor was being held.

Stevanovic maintains that by arresting Vucicevic, Serbia is fulfilling its international obligations, including those arising from the European Convention on Human Rights and international conventions protecting civil and political rights.

"What constitutes a call for violence and a threat to democracy, security and health must be sanctioned and removed from the public sphere," she said.

Vucicevic has been remanded in custody for 30 days.

The offence of causing panic and disorder through the media carries a prison sentence of between six months and five years.

Stevanovic points out, however, that the outcome of the case is not certain because the executive branch, the police and the Ministry of the Interior formally remain under the control of the outgoing government until such time as the Student List forms a new cabinet.

"What is happening now could be an internal power struggle within their own ranks, or an attempt by some prosecutors to clean up their records and ingratiate themselves with the new authorities, assuming what their wishes and intentions regarding Informer might be," she said. "Or it could be a serious manipulation, a staged arrest designed to portray Serbia abroad as a country carrying out revolutionary terror, which, of course, is not true."

#143. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#141. Allstate suffers multiday tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.

Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.

Updated after publication with Allstate’s comment.

#139. WhatsApp now lets you schedule messages in group and individual chats

WhatsApp on Wednesday started rolling out a much-sought-after feature: the ability to schedule messages in both individual and group chats.

The feature is rolling out globally to all users across iOS and Android. Once it becomes available, you can type a message and long-press the send button to schedule it. You can choose to send a message anywhere between 10 minutes and 14 days later.

Users will be able to see all their scheduled messages and delete them if needed. It’s not clear if there is a way to edit the schedule of a message.

Notably, scheduled messages will be sent even if your phone is off or outside network coverage. You can schedule messages containing text, photos, and videos.

The feature could prove useful for wishing someone a happy birthday on time or chatting with people across time zones. Businesses may also find it easier to schedule texts and updates to their customers, and people who use the app to coordinate work will likely find themselves using scheduled texts to update their teams.

WhatsApp rivals like Telegram and Signal have had the ability to schedule messages for a while now, so the Meta-owned chat app is late in rolling it out.

#138. France's political mainstream risks presidential vote debacle with no clear candidate

Protests on the streets of France over students' concerns and the cost of living have been marked by looting and unrest in recent weeks

France's political mainstream faces wipe-out at the presidential election in April-May 2027, unless it acts fast to choose who it wishes to stand.

With every poll confirming the dominance of hard-right and hard-left leaders Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon, candidates of the middle-ground â the old "legacy" parties â are being squeezed out of the game.

In theory any contender from centre-right or centre-left who was chosen in the first multi-candidate round of voting in April should be in a strong position to win in the play-off a fortnight later.

That is because at their separate ends of the spectrum both Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon have more people who oppose than support them.

But personal rivalries, lack of preparation and differing political visions have so far put paid to any hope of a meaningful challenge.

"The game in the centre is totally blocked," says political writer FranÃ§ois Fressoz at the left-leaning Le Monde newspaper. "The partisans of reason have lost their reason."

On the left â the old Socialist Party and its acolytes â there is at least agreement on a candidate: European Parliament MP RaphaÃ«l Glucksmann.

His problem is that allies who he might once have counted on â Greens, Communists and other small left-wing parties, not to mention many Socialist voters â are being pulled inexorably into the MÃ©lenchon orbit.

The protests started as an expression of anger at conditions in high schools

In the last two weeks, Green party leader Marine Tondelier, a majority of Communist parliamentary deputies and one-time Socialist presidential hopeful BenoÃ®t Hamon have all thrown in their lot with the leader of France Unbowed (LFI).

Another prominent figure of the left â FranÃ§ois Ruffin â has also withdrawn from the race, bowing to the unbowed MÃ©lenchon's now almost total command of the field.

So with the soft-left emasculated, the main hope for an alternative to Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon has to be focused on the right. But here the prospects â which until recently seemed bright â are now clouded with gloom.

"A MÃ©lenchonâLe Pen duel in round two is not yet inevitable," says Guillaume Tabard, political analyst at the conservative Le Figaro newspaper. "But it is looking increasingly probable."

In recent weeks the poll ratings of the main centre-right hopeful Edouard Philippe â a former prime minister under President Macron â have fallen sharply, and he is now consistently placed behind MÃ©lenchon in the first round of the vote. Marine Le Pen is way ahead of both.

Other candidates for command of this centre-right "space" are circling. They include another former Macron prime minister, Gabriel Attal; the leader of the right-wing Republicans (and former Macron interior minister), Bruno Retailleau; and now â a surprise new challenger â the mayor of Cannes, David Lisnard.

David Lisnard (centre) has seen membership of his micro-party Nouvelle Energie jump to more than 100,000

Lisnard, who stands on a liberal economic platform of less state and lower taxes, has emerged from nowhere to become the man of the hour. Membership of his micro-party Nouvelle Energie (New Energy) has shot up to more than 100,000, and he is being taken seriously as a possible Philippe replacement.

But this very multiplicity of names â not to mention others like judoka Teddy Riner, journalist Natacha Polony and former prime minister Dominique de Villepin â betrays the essential weakness of the centre-right challenge.

According to veteran commentator Nicolas Baverez, "If you want to win an election, it's quite simple: you need a strategy, a programme, and activists. Right now in the space between Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon we have none of that: there is a total vacuum.

"The most glaring absence is digital. MÃ©lenchon especially, but Le Pen too, are running extremely effective social media campaigns. In the middleâ¦ nothing."

Guillaume Tabard agrees that the lack of a shared platform is proving a serious handicap.

"In recent weeks we have seen Marine Le Pen issue her manifesto proposals for reforming pensions. We have seen Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon publish his programme in a best-selling book," he says.

"What do we have in between? Arguments about names and polling figures and whether or not there should be a primary. It's not, to put it mildly, an encouraging start."

For Fressoz, "There is no-one in the centre who is taking up the fight. When Le Pen makes her proposals about pensions, there is no-one saying, 'Hang on, that doesn't add up!'. Macron â who did after all take her on and beat her â is said to be furious."

Any mention of Macron, though, is most unwelcome for the camp that nominally is his heir. Philippe, Attal and Retailleau all served under a president who Baverez now calls "as toxic as Trump", so deep is his unpopularity in the country. Lisnard, by contrast, makes much of his refusal to serve under Macron â but he faces another handicap.

According to Fressoz, "Lisnard has shot up in the polls because he is on the right of the right, and there is always an appetite for a figure who is not Le Pen, but who speaks her kind of language on immigration.

"But Lisnard is an economic liberal, and in France that is a minority taste. He'll have his moment but then he will deflate."

Emmanuel Macron is now considered a politically toxic figure in France

With five months to go to until the election, all is not lost for the mainstream. Optimists in its ranks say that come the new year, opinion polls will start settling behind one or other centre-right candidate, and the others will feel obliged to step aside.

Talk of a centre-right primary â as occurred ahead of the 2017 election â is beginning to ebb. The two strongest candidates, Philippe and Lisnard, are opposed. Lisnard says he supports one but his conditions are designed to be unacceptable to the rest. Retailleau and Attal are in favour â but that, says Tabard, is "mainly as a way to keep their hopes alive."

In theory then it is possible a name will emerge in January or February, and start pulling behind him (or less likely, her) the mass of voters who do not want a disruptive radical as France's next head of state.

For Fressoz of Le Monde, "After years of Macron's 'neither left nor right', the left and right are once again shaping French politics. But now it is the extremes who are in control."

Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon have both already run three times for the presidency. Their visions were defined long ago, their strategies laid, their activists trained. Polarisation is their friend, so is the social tension of recent weeks, and both are undisputed masters in their own camps.

The 2027 dÃ©nouement has been in their sights for years. That is their massive advantage.

#136. As Gambia gears up for election, can Barrow secure a third term?

President Adama Barrow is seeking a third term as Gambians vote on December 5. The election will test the opposition's appeal and the country's progress on democratic reform.

Gambia votes on December 5 in a presidential election that takes place nearly 10 years after voters ended the 22-year rule of military ruler Yahya Jammeh.

President Adama Barrow, who succeeded Jammeh in 2017, is running for a third term despite an earlier pledge not to do so.

Barrow is running against the tiny West African country's main opposition leader Ousainou Darboe of the United Democratic Party, youth candidate Talib Ahmed Bensouda, who until last week was mayor of Kanifing Municipality, and four other candidates.

The field also includes independent candidates Almameh Gibba, a lawmaker who is backed by Jammeh, and former Vice President Isatou Touray, the only woman in the presidential race.

Gambia is still governed under its 1997 constitution, which was adopted during Jammeh's rule.

A proposed replacement, which included presidential term limits, was rejected by the National Assembly in July 2025 after failing to secure the required three-quarter majority.

Barrow has defended his decision to seek a third term, saying voters should decide who leads the country. While he says he supports presidential term limits, he has argued that their introduction depends on securing enough parliamentary backing for a new constitution.

"I believe in term limits. And the Gambian people are the ones who elect a president," Barrow told reporters after submitting his nomination papers to the Independent Electoral Commission (IEC) earlier in November. "So they decide the fate of a president and it is up to the Gambian people to decide whether I should be president for a third time or not."

Opposition parties have said their focus is not only on defeating Barrow but also on ensuring the election is conducted fairly.

Lamin Manneh, the United Democratic Party's administrative secretary, said the party's immediate priority is to ensure that the IEC conducts credible elections.

"The IEC has the responsibility of putting Gambians into confidence, ensuring that they guarantee us a credible election, a free election and a fair election," said Manneh.

Political analyst Malang Faye said the campaign was still taking shape after the official period began on November 16.

"The incumbent is already ahead," he told DW. "Some of the parties are yet to start their campaign." But he cautioned against judging electoral support by rally attendance alone.

"A large crowd at a political rally does not necessarily mean the same level of support at the ballot box," Faye added, noting that the election will test whether the democratic transition that began in 2016 can be consolidated.

Many voters say their immediate concerns are jobs, affordable living costs and better public services.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The World Bank has projected economic growth of 5.3% in 2026, but said extreme poverty is expected to remain at about 21%.

The 2025 Labour Force Survey puts youth unemployment at 11.5%, while 41.3% of young people are not in employment, education or training.

The figures reflect concerns voiced by many young Gambians about the lack of economic opportunities.

Alhagie Sama, a voter in Banjul, said young people need opportunities at home, rather than having to risk dangerous journeys abroad.

"We need a country where young people do not have to risk their lives crossing the Mediterranean Sea in search of opportunities," he said, adding that he wants the next government to deliver decent jobs, make basic necessities more affordable and improve access to health care and education.

Another voter and activist, Binta Bojang, said unemployment remains one of the country's biggest challenges.

"I would want the next president to prioritize employment, like particularly youth employment," she told DW, noting that joblessness goes beyond conventional definitions.

"We need to understand unemployment beyond the conventional definition, I mean the dictionary definition. Because unemployment definitely manifests differently in the lives of ordinary Gambians."

The Independent Electoral Commission has said 1,146,474 people are registered to vote. They will cast their ballots across 53 constituencies, 730 polling centers and 1,773 polling stations.

The commission has been training election officials in voting procedures, counting, verification and the transmission of results. It said the preparations are aimed at ensuring a peaceful and credible election.

International observers are also monitoring the process.

Following an invitation from Gambian authorities, the European Union has deployed an election observation mission, with teams of analysts and long-term observers across the country.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The United Nations has also urged candidates to honor the peace pact signed on November 16.

"History will judge not what you sign today, but what you do tomorrow and in the days to come," Barrie Freeman, the UN's deputy special representative for West Africa and the Sahel, told candidates.

Under the pact, presidential candidates pledged "to respect the independence and constitutional mandate of the IEC, to cooperate with it throughout the electoral process, and to seek redress through lawful and appropriate channels should disputes arise."

The December 5 election will test not only Barrow's bid for another term, but also whether the democratic gains made since 2016 can be translated into lasting reforms and improved living conditions.

DW correspondent Aminata Sanyang in Gambia contributed reporting.

#135. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#133. X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.

The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.

A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.

The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.

Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.

Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.

Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.

Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.

X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.

#131. Desperate appeals from women on death row in Iran after protests

Twin sisters Romina (left) and Taraneh Rahimi were arrested in late January, their cousin says

In recent weeks, the 21-year-old has been unable to walk properly after her legs were beaten, and an ear-infection caused by an untreated head injury has affected her balance, her cousin says.

She is "keeping her spirits up" but "there are days she breaks down in tears," Masoud Nourmohammadi tells BBC News Persian.

He says Taraneh and her twin sister Romina were arrested after joining the anti-government protests that swept the country in January.

When the twins' mother first saw them in prison, their faces were so swollen and bruised, and their hair pulled out, that she could only recognise them by their voices, Nourmohammadi says, speaking from the US.

He says Taraneh signed a confession after interrogators threatened that her sister would be raped in front of her.

Taraneh is one of six women who have been sentenced to death this year in connection with the January protests, according to the UN's human rights rapporteur for Iran, Mai Sato.

The figure for men is much higher. However, women protesters and dissidents are being sentenced to death in numbers not seen for decades, as part of a "horrific rise" in politically motivated executions, Amnesty International's Iran researcher Raha Bahreini says.

January's protests began over economic grievances and spread to become Iran's largest since the 1979 Islamic Revolution

The Iranian embassy in London told the BBC that claims of an increase in the use of the death penalty in protest-related cases were "misleading".

It categorically rejected allegations of the use of torture and any allegations that the death penalty has been imposed solely for peaceful protest, which it said is a "legitimate, and protected right".

It said death sentences in relation to the "riot in January 2026" were issued in cases involving "intentional murder" or "Moharebeh (the taking up of weapons with the intent to threaten or wreck the people's lives or property)".

The twins were seized in late January, Nourmohammadi says.

It was two weeks after the end of the protests, which began in late December over economic grievances and grew to become Iran's largest against the clerical establishment since the 1979 Islamic Revolution.

The US-based Human Rights Activists News Agency (Hrana) says it has identified more than 7,000 people killed in the crackdown that followed, most of them protesters, but believes the true figure is much higher.

The Iranian government accuses the US and Israel of fomenting the unrest, blaming "terrorists" and "rioters" for the deaths, which it says totalled 3,117 including some members of the security forces.

The twins' family did not find out where they were being held for more than three months, their cousin says. They were tried over protests in Isfahan, in an area of the city where a member of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) security force was killed.

Taraneh loves horses and would like to set up her own breeding centre one day, her cousin says

Court documents have not been published, but the BBC has seen part of a sentencing document which says Taraneh was convicted of moharebeh, which is a capital offence under Iran's Islamic Penal Code. The offence included "drawing a bladed weapon with the intent to endanger people's lives", according to the document.

Romina was sentenced to 25 years in jail for the lesser charge of aiding and abetting moharebeh, according to Sato, the UN rapporteur.

Nourmohammadi, who has been in contact with relatives and others in Iran familiar with the case, says both women deny all the charges against them and that there is no evidence Taraneh had a "bladed weapon".

Sato says the sisters were part of a case in which 10 people were sentenced to death, but that there was "no clarification of the acts attributed to each individual".

According to sources familiar with the case, the twins' lawyers have submitted a complaint alleging they were tortured and sexually abused during interrogation, but the investigation into it is being overseen by an official they accuse of torture.

This image of Mahboubeh Shabani, sentenced to death in September, has been shared with human rights groups

Voice messages from other women on death row contain a mixture of desperation and defiance.

Mahboubeh Shabani - widely reported to be 33 and a civil engineer - was sentenced to death in September under Iran's espionage law after transporting injured protesters, according to rights groups.

She called for help for herself and other prisoners from jail in the city of Mashhad in north-eastern Iran: "Everyone is in a terrible place mentally, especially those whose sentences have been finalised," she said.

Also jailed in Mashhad is Najmeh Amini, reported to be 23, who was initially arrested in the wake of the January protests, and later sentenced to death after being convicted of committing blasphemy in a social media post, according to rights groups.

"As long as I am alive, I have one hope, and that is to see a free and prosperous Iran before my life ends by the noose," a voice message attributed to her says.

In addition, Mojdeh Hashemi Bazargani - reported to be aged 27 - is also facing execution, according to rights groups. They say she was convicted of charges including "espionage", after being arrested in April, during the current war between Iran and Israel and the US.

The US-based Iranian rights group Tavaana has shared audio it says is of a phone call she made from prison, describing being beaten in the head, neck and body and made to sign a confession.

The woman in the recording says: "They accused me of espionage. They say you've been paid for it. If I had been paid for espionage, I would have a car or a house. I have nothing."

Najmeh Amini has been sentenced to death after being convicted of committing blasphemy in a social media post, rights groups say

Amnesty International describes the charges in all three of these cases and also the twins as "politically motivated".

In response to the BBC's questions, the Iranian Embassy in London said "any allegations of the use of torture in connection with the mentioned cases are categorically rejected". It did not comment further on individual cases, but said "confessions obtained under duress have no legal validity".

At least 39 people have already been executed in connection with the January protests, according to Sato.

She notes a "sharp" increase in arrests and sentences across both men and women, and sees the rise in death sentences for women as an "escalation and expansion" of an "established pattern of repression".

Iranian human rights lawyer Musa Barzin, who is based in Turkey, says "it appears that factors such as gender or age now carry less weight in determining the severity of punishment".

Official detail on cases is often scant and some may go unreported.

However, Sato says as well as the six women she identified as being arrested in connection with protests, a further three have received death sentences this year - for "blasphemy" and "corruption on Earth charges, in relation to the war in March" and "espionage and moharebeh".

In comparison, these nine sentences are more than Hrana recorded for women for similar offences over the whole decade to 2025 - with no more than three in any one year.

The death sentences in three of the cases Sato identified as being linked to the protests have since been overturned â though two of those have been sent for retrial. She says she is concerned by an "increasingly frequent pattern" where overturned death sentences are later "reimposed by a parallel branch of the judiciary once public attention has subsided".

Taraneh (left) and Romina both say they gave baseless confessions after being tortured

Sato says there was a "turning point" for women in Iran during the 2022 protests over the death of Mahsa Amini, who died in custody after being detained over an alleged breach of Iran's dress code.

Women "were at the forefront of challenging state policies and structures of control," says Sato. "Their political agency, visibility and leadership have made them targets of state repression."

The Iranian embassy said judicial proceedings in Iran are conducted in accordance with the Constitution and rule of law. It adds: "Cases involving the death penalty have included charges such as serious criminal offences, violence, terrorism, espionage, cooperation with hostile intelligence services and Moharebeh."

Iran executes more people per head of population than any other country, according to UN experts. Human rights groups say last year saw the highest number in decades.

Hrana recorded 2,003 executions in the year to October 2026. The vast majority - including 47 women - were for crimes such as murder and drug trafficking.

Nourmohammadi says the family are trying as best they can to support the twins.

Romina enjoys music and often asks relatives to play songs over the phone, he says.

Taraneh loves horses and has always wanted to set up a horse breeding centre, he says, and they tell her they plan to help her do so in the future: "That's how we try to give her hope."

#130. Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats challenged by state AGs

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.

Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.

The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.

Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.

In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.

“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.

Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.

“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”

Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”

The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)

Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.

California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.
