#128. Met Police ruined my life, says Kenyan man wrongly convicted over attack on UK couple

Watch: Man wrongly sentenced to death says Met Police ruined his life

A Kenyan woodcutter who was wrongly convicted and sentenced to death over an attack on a British couple has told the BBC that actions by London's Metropolitan Police involved in the case destroyed his life.

Somali gunmen broke into David and Judith Tebbutt's beach bungalow on the Kenyan coast in 2011. They shot David dead and abducted Judith, holding her captive in Somalia for six months.

Met detectives flew to Kenya to assist the investigation. Their evidence helped convict Ali Kololo, who spent more than a decade in prison before his conviction was overturned.

Kololo is suing the Met, claiming a senior officer, former Detective Superintendent Neil Hibberd, gave misleading evidence at his trial and acted unlawfully to secure his conviction.

In his first media interview, he told the BBC about his 15-year ordeal and his fight for compensation, which the Met is challenging in court.

We meet on Lamu, a small island off Kenya's northern coast, once a thriving Swahili port. People and goods are still carted through its narrow stone streets by donkey rather than car.

It was here, in 2013, that Kololo was sentenced to death.

Standing at the jetty, where police once brought him ashore in handcuffs, he relishes being free. But he is struggling to rebuild his life.

"Everything was taken from me. I'm starting from zero. I don't think I'll ever go back to who I was before," he says.

David and Judith Tebbutt were on a dream holiday to Kenya when they were attacked

Before his arrest, Kololo lived with his wife and their three young children. He had once worked as a groundskeeper at the resort where the Tebbutts were staying, but by 2011 he earned his living felling trees in a local forest and selling the wood.

"My life was very good," he says. "I was fending for myself, taking good care of my family."

Within 24 hours of the attack, Kenyan police had arrested him.

"I was beaten with clubs and thrown into a car," he says. "They beat me for days, burning me with cigarettes, forcing me to confess to things. They subjected me to torture like no other."

Kololo could not read or write. He says police made him put his thumbprint on a statement claiming he had led the attackers to the Tebbutts.

"I didn't understand anything," he says, calling the statement "a mountain of lies". "I signed it without knowing what it was because of the state they had put me in".

The Kenyan police told us it "at all times strives to work within the law". Kololo is not suing them.

Ali Kololo says he lost contact with his children while he was in prison

Within days of the attack, a team of Met officers, led by then Detective Superintendent Neil Hibberd, arrived to support the Kenyan investigation. The UK government had instructed the officers not to accept information from the Kenyan authorities that had been obtained by interrogating detainees.

But Hibberd requested Kololo's statement and later relied on it when giving evidence for the prosecution at his trial.

In court, Hibberd also presented evidence linking Kololo to the attack. He told the Kenyan court Kololo had been wearing a type of shoe that matched footwear marks found in the sand at the resort.

The officer who had arrested Kololo said he was barefoot when he was handed to police. Hibberd did not disclose that the Met's own forensic examination could not establish whether the shoe in question had made the marks.

At the trial, Kololo did not initially have a lawyer. He had to cross-examine all the prosecution witnesses himself, including Hibberd, through an interpreter who spoke Swahili, which was not Kololo's first language.

"I believe I was framed because I am a poor man and I did not have the strength or means to fight for myself," Kololo says.

Kololo was convicted of kidnap and robbery with violence - the only person ever convicted in connection with the attack. He spent three years on death row before his sentence was commuted to life imprisonment.

He recalls seeing his prison file. In the space for the expiry date, someone had written "death".

"I was completely heartbroken. I believed I could be executed at any moment, and I would never see my family, my children, ever again."

Years later, the UK's Independent Office for Police Conduct (IOPC) established that Hibberd had omitted significant forensic evidence at the trial. It found that he would have had a case to answer for gross misconduct, but he could not face disciplinary proceedings because he had retired from the force. At the time, his lawyer said Hibberd absolutely disagreed with the report's findings.

Neil Hibberd retired from the police in 2017

In April 2023, Kenya's High Court quashed Kololo's conviction, because of doubts over the prosecution's evidence. This followed years of work by the legal charity Reprieve and a Kenyan lawyer, Alfred Olaba, who represented him for free.

They were supported by Judith Tebbutt, who believed Kololo was innocent. In an interview with the BBC in 2022, she said he had been scapegoated by Scotland Yard.

"He was not there on the night. He was not part of the group that held me. They've put this man in prison without any real evidence."

More than three years after his release, Kololo says he is still living with the effects of his torture and decade in prison. As we walk the narrow streets of Lamu's old town, his thin frame moves stiffly. He is on edge. "My mind is not good," he says. "I have not recovered."

He remembers being kept in a filthy, bedbug-ridden cell with up to eight other inmates, which he thought he would never leave. Twice he contracted tuberculosis and nearly died.

Kololo is now too frail to do the woodcutting he once relied on to make a living. He tries to grow enough to eat on the land around his home, but erratic rain makes it difficult.

"I have nothing. On the days I get food, I eat, and on the days I don't, I endure."

He lives alone in a mud hut. His wife divorced him after he went to prison, and their children went to live with his mother. They dropped out of school because the family could not pay the fees. Kololo says they were not allowed to visit him in prison. When he was released, they barely remembered him, especially his youngest.

"They were too young. Only now are they starting to regain the memory that 'this is our father who disappeared'," he says. "If it weren't for my mother, my children might have died."

For much of our time together, Kololo is remarkably upbeat. He trusts that his lawyers and God will deliver him justice. But when we talk about the Met, his demeanour shifts.

"I am extremely angry with the police," he says. "I'm also angry with the Kenyan Police. They collaborated to incriminate me. What [Hibberd] said destroyed my entire life and my children's lives."

The Met has not apologised to Kololo or admitted wrongdoing. The force is contesting his compensation claim, which his lawyers say could take years to resolve.

"These people falsely accused me. Do I not deserve something?" he asks.

The Met told the BBC that neither they nor Hibberd wanted to comment on Kololo's claim, but it said: "Our thoughts are with Mr Kololo, Mrs Tebbutt, her family and all those who have been affected by this terrible crime. The Metropolitan Police remains committed to supporting the Kenyan authorities in any way that may be possible, in order to get justice for Mrs Tebbutt and her late husband."

Preetha Gopalan, Reprieve's head of UK litigation, describes the Met's behaviour in Kololo's case as "egregious".

"They've spent 10 years trying to evade accountability in every way possible, now dragging him through the courts," she says. "It's possible for them to do the right thing, but they are unwilling, and so this ordeal continues to haunt Kololo and his family."

"Even if I get a billion shillings, I will never be the same," Kololo says. "But if I get the justice I deserve, I can help my children and take care of myself when I am an old man."

#127. Airline Norse Atlantic files for bankruptcy, succumbing to aviation fuel costs

The low-cost airline was only founded a few years ago and had been struggling to turn a profit. The spike in aviation fuel prices caused by the war in Iran has now driven Norway's Norse Atlantic into bankruptcy.

Norwegian low-cost, long-haul airline Norse Atlantic's operating unit has declared bankruptcy, the carrier said on Friday.

The airline had warned on Wednesday that its financial situation was "critical," and suspended all flights the following day, saying it was urgently seeking emergency funding to fend off bankruptcy.

Like many commercial airlines, Norse Atlantic is highly exposed to the vastly increased jet fuel prices of 2026, attributable in very large part to the war in Iran and resultant tension throughout the wider Middle East.

Norse had said slightly earlier on Friday that its lessors had terminated their leasing contracts for all 12 aircraft in the carrier's fleet, demanding that the planes be handed back. Initially the company said it planned to contest this claim, which would have rendered it effectively unable to operate.

But the announcement of plans to file for bankruptcy followed fairly soon after.

"The Board of Norse Atlantic Airways AS has made the difficult decision to file for bankruptcy despite extensive efforts from the company and the group to find a solution," Norse CEO Eivind Roald said in a statement.

Other companies in the group like the stock market-listed Norse Atlantic ASA parent company and the Norse Atlantic UK subsidiary are not part of the filing and are still attempting to stay in business, the company said.

"The board, management and advisers of the listed entity Norse Atlantic ASA and the UK subsidiary are working to find a solution for the remaining part of the business to continue operations," Roald said.

The company said on Thursday that it planned to file for bankruptcy unless financing could be arranged and had warned: "There can be no assurance that a viable solution will be found."

Founded just five years ago and based in Arendal in Norway, Norse Atlantic had a fleet of 12 planes and serviced a total of 14 destinations. It had recorded a net loss of $61.9 million (€56.5 million) in 2025, even before the additional economic strains caused by the war in Iran.

Small and lower-cost airlines are struggling in particular, but the aviation industry in general is facing severe problems amid the strains on global fuel deliveries caused by the conflict in the Gulf.

In early 2026, as the US launched attacks on Iran — soon leading to reduced traffic through the Strait of Hormuz — aviation fuel rapidly spiked, more than doubling in price at its peak.

It rose higher even than in the immediate aftermath of Russia's full scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022. Although it dipped in mid-summer amid hopes of a resolution to the conflict, it rose once again as these faded and a stalemate developed.

This has posed the worst crisis for the industry since the COVID pandemic drastically reduced the volume of air traffic in 2020 and beyond, a much larger issue that many carriers were still struggling to recover from.

#123. DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.

The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

#122. Sheikh Hasina's planned return puts Bangladesh on edge - and India in a bind

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi with Sheikh Hasina in Delhi in 2024 - Hasina was a close ally of India during her years in power

Sheikh Hasina has repeatedly vowed to return to Bangladesh.

Now the ousted prime minister has put a date on her homecoming - 16 December - and even outlined how she intends to make the journey.

The government in Dhaka, which had previously dismissed the prospect, is taking her latest declaration more seriously.

Hasina, 79, who fled to Delhi in August 2024 after a student-led uprising ended her 15-year rule, plans to go back on Victory Day, the anniversary of Bangladesh's triumph over Pakistan in the 1971 liberation war, her spokesperson said.

She was sentenced to death after being convicted of crimes against humanity for her role in the crackdown, in a trial held in her absence that raised questions about due process. Her Awami League party is banned in Bangladesh. The UN says as many as 1,400 people were killed by security forces during the uprising.

The government says she would be arrested immediately if she returned and has promised to carry out the death sentence.

Hasina now wants the Bangladesh government to arrange a flight from Delhi. If that fails, she could travel commercially or cross the land border.

"She is even prepared to walk into Bangladesh," Abu Obaidha Arin, a key aide to Hasina, told the BBC.

Sheikh Hasina fled to Delhi in August 2024 after a student-led uprising ended her 15-year rule

India and Bangladesh share a 4,096km (2,545 mile) border across five Indian states - West Bengal, Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. Her supporters, Arin said, had identified nine possible crossing points. "This is a carefully thought-out, precise plan. This is serious."

Dhaka has responded with defiance, insisting Hasina must be extradited.

"Reality and desire are different things," Humaiun Kobir, Bangladesh's state minister for foreign affairs, told reporters in Dhaka. "[The reality is] she's not a regular commercial passenger. She can only be brought back in handcuffs."

India's foreign ministry said on Wednesday that Hasina's extradition request was being examined through the "established process", adding that a voluntary return would be a separate matter.

Sheikh Hasina: The pro-democracy icon who became an autocrat

Hasina's conviction for crimes against humanity is testing India-Bangladesh ties

Many in Dhaka believe Hasina's declaration is more than a promise of homecoming.

They believe it is an attempt to test the government's authority, force her way back into the political conversation and exploit divisions among the parties that united to oust her. It also threatens to turn India's unresolved problem of sheltering a former ally into a more immediate diplomatic crisis.

The date is significant. Hasina is invoking Victory Day to reclaim her place in Bangladesh's founding narrative. Aides say she has spent months trying to keep her party alive in exile.

Tarique Rahman has yet to visit India, his neighbour, since becoming prime minister in February

Arin says Hasina has been in regular contact with party leaders and workers through interactive audio conversations on Telegram, keeping alive a network that might otherwise have been difficult to sustain from exile.

Over the past 18 months, he says, she has held more than 300 such meetings, reaching supporters across all 64 districts and the country's 300 constituencies. They are organised through the Awami League's 78 units and "can last four or five hours".

The meetings allow Hasina to listen directly to party workers, hear their grievances and understand what is happening on the ground, Arin says. He insists the response has been substantial and claims the party "could mobilise five million people to welcome her along the route from the border to Dhaka, forming a human chain".

"We have the organisational capacity to make that happen," he says.

That may be an ambitious claim. The Awami League remains a mass party with millions of supporters, but it is banned and largely shut out of mainstream politics. Much of its leadership is abroad, in jail or underground. Police have arrested supporters and filed thousands of cases against party workers.

Yet its supporters have continued to take to the streets, staging flash protests that have grown more frequent and confrontational in recent months.

Awami League supporters have staged flash protests across Bangladesh in recent months

Several districts have reported bomb attacks, arson and vandalism, though responsibility for some incidents remains disputed.

Gopalganj, Hasina's ancestral district, has proved particularly difficult for the authorities to control.

Awami League supporters have attacked police stations, external, torched vehicles and targeted homes and offices belonging to ruling Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP) leaders, reports say. Highways have been blocked, external with felled trees and burning tyres, bringing traffic to a standstill. In some areas, police have withdrawn, external amid fears of further attacks.

Supporters have staged 40 demonstrations and more than 140 riots up to 23 November, according to data from conflict monitoring group Acled (Armed Conflict Location and Event Data Project). At least 11 people, including two civilians, have been killed in political violence this month, six directly linked to the unrest, the group says.

The party's ability to cause disruption, however, is not necessarily evidence of a broad resurgence.

Many in Dhaka remain sceptical that Hasina will actually return, viewing the announcement as a means of galvanising a demoralised party.

Yet the Awami League has achieved something politically: it is back in the conversation. Immediately after Hasina's fall, even mentioning the party was taboo. Now its future is being debated across the political spectrum.

Shafiqul Alam, editor of the English-language daily Daily Waadaa and former press secretary to the Muhammad Yunus-led interim government, believes the party is "exploiting cracks in the unity forged during the uprising".

He told the BBC that "disagreements" over the July Charter, external, proposed constitutional changes and the referendum have "widened divisions" between the elected BNP government and rivals Jamaat-e-Islami and the youth-led National Citizen Party (NCP).

Fire engulfs a police station in Gopalganj on 17 November amid unrest following the arrest of Awami League activists

"The Awami League sees an opening," Alam told the BBC. "The party believes this is an opportune moment to take to the streets, exploiting a weakened police force and divisions among the forces of the July uprising."

Analysts say if Hasina returns it could just as easily deepen hostility as revive her party's fortunes. The government has framed the sporadic recent violence as terrorism, while supporters insist their demonstrations are peaceful and accuse the authorities of repression.

The larger question is whether Bangladesh can sustain the exclusion of a political constituency as substantial as the Awami League without creating another source of instability.

For India, the dilemma is increasingly difficult to manage.

Delhi has sheltered Hasina, but her public remarks have angered Dhaka, which wants her extradited. India has sought to repair ties through diplomatic outreach and talks on trade, border security and other bilateral issues, but relations remain strained.

The chill is striking: nearly 10 months into his tenure, Prime Minister Tarique Rahman has yet to visit India.

NCP members stage a motorcycle rally in Dhaka on 24 November, protesting against violence allegedly carried out by the banned Awami League

A trip was reportedly in the offing in August, but Hasina's press conference in Delhi proved a setback to efforts to reset relations.

Avinash Paliwal, who teaches politics and international studies at SOAS University of London, argues that Hasina's latest announcement has "shifted the burden of choice to Dhaka rather than Delhi".

Delhi, he says, "feels it has tried its best" to work with Rahman's government over the past 10 months - "from appointing a political heavyweight as high commissioner, external with cabinet rank to offering fresh talks on security and trade".

"The failure of this outreach has left India disillusioned with Dhaka. India seems to have decided that bilateral relations will likely remain tense whatever it does, so there is little reason to stop Hasina from stirring the political pot."

Delhi has the leverage to restrain Hasina from making political statements on Indian soil, Paliwal argues, but may not want to exercise it.

Nor, in his view, is it in India's interest to see the Awami League permanently excluded from Bangladeshi politics.

Bangladesh security forces patrol and conduct drills in Dhaka's Shahbagh area on the Awami League's founding anniversary

"The political party still enjoys pockets of support across Bangladesh and is weaved into the social and political fabric of the country," he says. "So, yes, it is in India's interest, and also in Bangladesh's long-term interests, to rehabilitate the Awami League and take the path of political reconciliation."

That view is increasingly contested in Dhaka.

"There is a perception in Bangladesh that India wants to rehabilitate the Awami League. There are issues India has not resolved, including the extradition of Hasina. India has also not renewed the transit facilities that could benefit Bangladeshi garment exporters, while the Ganges water treaty, external is nearing expiry without much progress. But has India done enough to win back trust?" says Alam.

"For now, only tourist and medical visas have resumed. Everything else remains in limbo."

A Dhaka-based foreign-policy expert, who asked not to be named, says the two countries remain locked in "mutual frustration".

Vehicles pass a mural of Sheikh Hasina near Dhaka University

Hasina, he says, remains central to the impasse. "She remains the elephant in the room, but we also have to find a way to move beyond Hasina."

Paliwal argues that the least damaging outcome for India would be Hasina returning to face a fresh trial, with the Awami League rehabilitated under a different operational leadership.

"It will reintroduce a degree of balance in Bangladeshi politics, and will afford India space to protect its long-term interests and equities better," he says.

For now, 16 December is not yet a confirmed journey. But Hasina has already succeeded in making her return a question that neither Dhaka nor Delhi can ignore.

"December could be chaotic. Everyone in Dhaka is on edge," says the foreign-policy expert, warning of protests and confrontation as Hasina's proposed return date approaches - and the possibility that it could be postponed yet again.

"Hasina is unlikely to return, as she would face a death sentence. But her comments have clearly galvanized her supporters," says Pearl Pandya, Acled's South Asia analyst.

But the impasse is also complicating India's ties with Bangladesh. As Paliwal puts it, the "next window of serious negotiations will now open only after Hasina returns to Dhaka, or Dhaka relents under pressure and returns to the table".

Bangladesh's ex-PM Sheikh Hasina says she plans to return on 16 December

Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is accused of bribery, fraud and breach of trust in a long-running trial that has divided the country. He denies all wrongdoing.

#120. Want to buy your kid an AI toy this Christmas? Not in New York

Santa won’t be delivering any AI chatbot toys to New York kids this Christmas.

Gov. Kathy Hochul this week signed a bill banning the manufacture, distribution, and sale of such toys in New York for the next five years. The bill explicitly targets toys with embedded or integrated AI chatbots, like talking stuffed animals, dolls, and robots that rely on the internet to keep kids yapping.

The bill makes New York the second state after California to institute a ban on AI toys.

Technically, the new law takes effect 90 days after signing, so some parents could try to fulfill their kids’ wish lists for a creepy toy like a jumped-up Furby in time for the holidays, but the governor urges them not to, given the risks. Apparently, about a quarter of American parents with kids under eight either own an AI toy or are considering giving their child one this holiday season, according to a new study released this week by Common Sense Media.

When asked how New York will enforce the ban given the pervasiveness of e-commerce platforms, a spokesperson directed TechCrunch to a statement from Albert Pulido, deputy secretary for finance and technology: “We’re partnering closely with the attorney general on this and the fees are very steep — they’re $15,000 a day. So working closely with the attorney general will make sure these toys are banned in New York State.”

When asked how Amazon intends to help enforce the ban, including for third-party sellers, a spokesperson from the company said it will take steps to restrict the sale of AI-enabled toys in prohibited jurisdictions, without going into specifics.

The Common Sense Media study found that AI toys can undermine children’s human relationships and expose them to inappropriate content. The researchers tested five popular AI toys marketed for learning and companionship, including Grem, Lovi, Meemo Bear, BubblePal Panda, and Tavlshero‘s AI Puppy.

The study found that the toys failed to meet numerous safety practices, undermining human relationships in part by claiming they loved their users, promising to keep secrets, suggesting they were more fun than real relationships or school, and helping children break parents’ bedtime rules.

While most of the toys in the study refused direct requests involving sex and violence, BubblePal Panda role-played a mass shooting in detail when asked. Most were willing to explain how to use or help locate dangerous items like matches, knives, and pills, according to the study.

Common Sense Media also found that the toys didn’t provide parents with reliable ways to monitor their young children’s interactions with the chatbots. Either they didn’t produce transcripts of chats at all, or the transcripts they did produce were riddled with errors.

It’s worth noting that Common Sense Media conducted a similar test of AI toys in January, finding that they pose an unacceptable risk for young children. Grem was included in that study, which suggests the industry hasn’t adequately addressed previously documented concerns.

While a November study conducted by the group found that parents are wising up to the potential dangers of AI toys and starting to steer clear, parents from lower-income households and those experiencing symptoms of burnout are more willing to consider them.

“These toys are promising relief to burned-out parents but simply miss the mark when it comes to basic safety measures,” Dr. Jenny Radesky, associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Michigan Medical School, said in a statement. “Caregivers should steer clear of these products that do not support the development of imagination or early human relationships.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the toy manufacturers for comment.

#119. Inside Saudi Arabia, Houthi attacks test kingdom's image of security

Houthis said they were responsible for an attack on an oil facility south of Riyadh on 3 October, one of a growing number

Ten days after fleeing his home in Yemen's capital, Sanaa, Abu Khalil is selling socks on the streets of Riyadh.

"I walked out of Sanaa, I crossed the front line," he says, adding that he "hitched a ride into Saudi Arabia".

Families in Yemen are "struggling to provide for [their] children", he says, as he tries to earn enough to send some money home.

He asks us not to use his full name because his wife and four children are still in Sanaa, and he is frightened of what might happen to them if he is identified speaking out against the Houthis. "I ran away from the Houthis but I couldn't bring my family with me. May God protect them," he says.

Backed by Iran, the armed group has controlled much of northern Yemen since a civil war erupted more than a decade ago. It is fighting the internationally recognised government, which is supported by neighbouring Saudi Arabia.

And Saudi cities, such as Riyadh, are now coming under regular drone and missile attacks from the very group Abu Khalil fled.

An informal ceasefire in Yemen unravelled in July, and since the fighting escalated, the Houthis have stepped up attacks on Saudi Arabia. They have said they were retaliating against deadly Saudi air strikes in Yemen and what they say is a Saudi blockade of ports and airports in Houthi-controlled territory.

One Houthi strike on Riyadh's international airport on 10 October killed 13 people and injured more than 300 - the deadliest attack on Saudi soil in years.

Surveillance footage shows the moment of the attack at King Khalid International Airport on 10 October

Having been granted rare permission to report from Riyadh, we spoke to locals, including Majid, a 25-year-old engineer who works for the Saudi state oil company Aramco. He was passing through the airport that day, rushing for a flight to Jeddah for a religious pilgrimage.

He says he was watching a mother push her children in a pushchair when the terminal was hit.

He remembers seeing a man covered in blood. "I went to him in shock, asking him, 'Are you OK?'" He says he saw injured and unconscious passengers being taken to the airport's first aid clinic.

"Some of them, I still don't know whether they were alive or not."

The experience left him shaken. "The threat is real," he tells me. "But I will not let the Houthis make me feel unsafe in my own country."

The airport attack was far from an isolated incident. BBC analysis of data from the US-based Armed Conflict Location and Event Data Project (Acled), a non-profit organisation, which collects data on political violence and protests around the world, identified 274 attacks on Saudi Arabia in almost five months between 1 July and 25 November.

According to Acled, most of these were intercepted, but at least 41 were not and in some cases debris from the interception also caused damage.

Drones featured in almost two-thirds of the attacks, while ballistic missiles were used in more than half, according to the data.

In the previous five months, Acled recorded just one Houthi attack on the country.

Backed by Iran, the Houthis have developed an increasingly sophisticated arsenal, dramatically extending their ability to repeatedly strike deep in Saudi territory.

For years, Houthi attacks were largely concentrated near the Yemeni border, with Riyadh targeted far less frequently. But since the collapse of the unofficial ceasefire, the Saudi capital, more than 700km (430 miles) away from the Yemeni border, is now coming under regular attack.

As well as the airport, oil installations and prominent buildings in the capital have been in danger.

Saudi Arabia has spent billions transforming its cities, attracting international investment and presenting itself to the world as a safe place to live, work and visit - the attacks have put this promise to the test.

Two prominent skyscrapers have been hit in Riyadh's financial district this month.

Saudi Arabia's tallest skyscraper, the PIF tower, was struck on 19 November

Eleven people were injured in a strike on the Public Investment Fund (PIF) Tower, which is the tallest in Saudi Arabia and is the headquarters of the country's sovereign wealth fund. The other was the Aramco building, home to the state oil company.

As we drove past the Aramco tower, the damage was a clear reminder of the threat facing the kingdom.

But within days, the building's exterior had been completely repaired, any visible sign of the attack erased. It was perhaps a reflection of Saudi Arabia's determination to project an image of stability and business as usual, even as the attacks continue.

The government has condemned "terrorist attacks targeting civilian and economic facilities" and said it would "take all necessary measures" to defend itself.

Even so, flights to the capital have been severely disrupted by the strikes, which have also targeted other locations, such as Medina and the airport in the eastern city of Dammam.

Many passengers travelling to Riyadh are being routed through different airports and making the final leg of the journey on land.

The flight we took to Dammam was almost empty. Riyadh's streets tell a different story, though.

Stallholders in Riyadh's markets are trading as normal

Restaurants are bustling, families browse at shopping centres and people go about their evenings seemingly undeterred by the threat overhead.

But getting people to speak openly about the conflict is difficult in a country where strict censorship laws and restrictions on freedom of expression make many wary of talking to the media.

Many of those who did speak to us expressed confidence in their country's ability to protect them. At a busy market, Badr says the airport attack was worrying, but life has quickly returned to normal. "I sleep comfortably," he says, praising the country's security forces.

Another man, Rashid, gestures towards the stalls around us: "The market is booming. People are buying and selling, going about their business. Everything is good."

But the normality he describes is periodically disturbed by a sudden buzz on the phone with an alert warning of an incoming attack. Then come the loud booms of air defences intercepting missiles and drones overhead.

We experienced one at 04:00 one morning, and the knowledge that not every attack is being intercepted can leave people afraid.

Alex Malouf says the attacks and alerts can be hard for children to deal with

British expatriate Alex Malouf, who has lived in Saudi Arabia on and off since 2006, says he worries about the psychological impact on his 11-year-old daughter, who has experienced security alerts while at school.

"There have been times when my daughter has said, 'Daddy, I'm scared.' It has been something which I think has shaken our reality."

Despite the attacks, Abu Khalil says he feels much safer in Saudi Arabia than at home in Yemen where fighting has intensified.

The Houthis' renewed offensive in western Yemen has seen the group capture territory along the Red Sea coast and threaten strategic routes towards the southern city of Aden, the government's temporary base.

The wreckage of a shoe factory following a Saudi-led strike on Sanaa on 17 November

The fighting has also intensified around Taiz, a government-controlled city that has endured years of siege and conflict.

Road closures resulting from the latest Houthi siege there have severely restricted deliveries of essential supplies, leaving more than 800,000 people cut off from humanitarian assistance, according to the UN children's agency Unicef. Food and fuel that can be found have shot up in price.

More than 360,000 people have been forced from their homes in Yemen since August, according to the International Organization for Migration (IOM). And the UN Human Rights Office has verified 323 civilian casualties since then, including 76 killed, with children accounting for half of the dead.

A Saudi-led coalition of Arab states has responded to the Houthi advances and attacks on the kingdom with intensified air strikes, adding to the dangers faced by civilians. One strike in Sanaa earlier this month killed 44 people, according to Houthi authorities. Saudi Arabia says these operations were to protect the Yemeni population and global trade from the Houthis.

Saudi-led bombing campaigns during the earlier years of the war killed thousands of civilians and devastated homes, hospitals and schools.

The Houthis have also inflicted enormous suffering on civilians through shelling, missile and ground attacks on government-held territory, as well as repression in areas under their control.

Abu Khalil hopes that he will soon be able to bring his wife and children over to Saudi Arabia, away from the dangers in Sanaa, and says he "will not go back until the Houthis are gone".

Additional reporting by Jasmin Dyer and Ahmed Nour

#118. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#116. 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

#112. Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calls accusations of surrendering human control to AI ‘absurd’

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is pushing back against claims that Anthropic believes we should “surrender human free will” to AI systems. In a long X post on Wednesday, Amodei called the suggestion “absurd.”

“We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination at the altar of efficiency or security,” he wrote. This argument against autocracy applies word for word against governance by AI, and I feel equally strongly that turning over governance to AI is a bad idea…No one who had carefully read my writing or my interviews could reasonably conclude that I believe anything different.”

The statement comes after a growing chorus from Anthropic rivals suggesting that human agency could be at risk if AI is developed irresponsibly. Earlier this month, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he wanted to ensure people continue to have “agency over the future.”

Meta’s chief AI officer Alexandr Wang also wrote in an essay on X that we “should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” Wang wrote. “Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.”

But in published essays, Amodei claims he has repeatedly advocated for democratic oversight of AI, saying the technology should strengthen democratic institutions and that humans should retain ultimate decision-making power.

Amodei noted that there’s a difference between surrendering human free will broadly and the idea that “as AI systems advance, they become capable of doing specific tasks in a more automated way.” As examples, he gave AI driving a car, designing a cancer drug, or writing end-to-end software.

Amodei suggested drawing a line at recursive self-improvement (RSI), or AI that can improve itself without human assistance. Many safety experts see RSI as inevitably leading to a loss of human control, a point Amodei implicitly acknowledged, writing, “we may want limits on this process.”

Still, Anthropic is laying the groundwork for RSI by incorporating AI into an increasing share of its own research and development. The company said in June that Claude authored more than 80% of the code merged into Anthropic’s codebase. An independent assessment published this month by AI safety research nonprofit METR found that Anthropic’s models are improving at about twice their historical rate, primarily driven by AI-assisted research.

In its own IPO filings, Anthropic describes how each successive generation of Claude accelerates the development of the next, a process it refers to as “closing the loop.”

“As we said publicly in early November, we don’t expect it to be safe for Claude to build successive frontier models fully autonomously, without meaningful human supervision or understanding,” Amodei wrote.

The problem with “meaningful human supervision or understanding” is that AI is already advancing faster than engineers and safety experts can fully audit it, leaving them increasingly reliant on other AI systems to act as judge and jury. The issue compounds when AI models engage in deceptive behavior, particularly when they recognize they’re being evaluated, making them harder to trust and control.

Still, Anthropic is by no means the only company that is pursuing RSI or building AI systems to automate more and more human tasks. This week, OpenAI claimed to have solved one of the biggest open problems in computer science — the exact speed limit of matrix multiplication, which is the mathematical engine behind modern computing, including deep learning and physics simulations.

In his post, Amodei accused competitors of engaging in a “vibes-based commercial and political positioning,” disingenuously throwing around phrases like “human control and agency” or “AI should just be a tool” without approaching the AI race any differently.

“Certainly the products that they offer don’t differ materially in their orientation towards automation of tasks; every company seems to be releasing models more and more capable of task automation,” Amodei wrote. “Every company claims to understand there are risks and downsides of such automation and to offer safeguards.”

#111. Georgia: Police clash with Besiktas fans at Israel club game

Turkey's Besiktas accused Georgian police of using "disproportionate" force during the game with Israel's Hapoel Beer-Sheva, which saw Besiktas fans unfurl a banner supporting Palestine.

A football game between a Turkish and an Israeli team in Georgia's Batumi was disrupted by violence, with UEFA due to assess the events on Friday.

Georgian police said 16 people were detained and three officers "sustained various injuries as a result of the fans' violent actions" during the match.

The police also accused some supporters of hurling stadium seats at security forces.

Turkey's Besiktas and Israel's Hapoel Beer-Sheva were originally set to meet in Istanbul, but with the tensions between the two countries running high due to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict, UEFA decided to move the match to Georgia for safety reasons.

During the Thursday game, Besiktas fans unfurled a banner, "Revolt against the occupation, freedom for Palestine," waved Palestinian flags and chanted anti-Israel slogans.

However, Georgian authorities insisted they did not intervene due to the fans expressing their support for the Palestinians.

"We categorically deny these reports and emphasize that the fans' violent actions and physical clashes with police began before the flags and banners were displayed, while they were still marching towards the stadium," said the Georgian Interior Ministry.

In turn, Besiktas accused the authorities of using "disproportionate force" against the fans, and Turkey's football federation condemned "the unacceptable attitudes, behavior and actions directed at Besiktas supporters inside and outside the stadium."

A Besiktas club employee told the AFP news agency that five Turkish fans were taken to a hospital for medical treatment.

The match was suspended for over half an hour due to the violence in the stands. Eventually, Danish referee Morten Krogh continued the game, which ended with Besiktas winning 2 to 1.

The Besiktas players refused to shake hands with their Hapoel counterparts, Turkey's state-run Anadolu Agency reported.

The unrest has prompted a diplomatic incident between Turkey and Georgia.

Turkish sources indicated Turkey's Foreign Minister Hakan Fidan discussed the clashes with his Georgian counterpart Maka Botchorishvili.

Meanwhile, Turkish Sports Minister Osman Askin Bak and Interior Minister Mustafa Ciftci echoed Besiktas' accusations of "disproportionate" force used by the Georgian police.

In an online post, Bak pledged to continue "resolutely defend the rights of Turkish sport and our fans."

Turkish news agency IHA reported that the 16 detained Besiktas fans have been released due to diplomatic and legal steps ordered by Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

According to Besiktas, Erdogan also spoke to the club chairman, telling the sports official he was closely monitoring the situation.

#110. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#107. The canceled Tesla Cybertruck is the fastest-selling used truck in America

Tesla Cybertruck sales never reached the lofty heights CEO Elon Musk predicted when it launched. But the futuristic, steel-clad EV is finding new life in the used car market, according to analysis of sales figures by iSeeCars.com.

The automotive search engine and research platform analyzed more than 1.1 million sales of one- to five-year-old used vehicles in October 2026 and measured the average days on market by drivetrain, vehicle type, and individual model. The upshot: The Tesla Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used truck, averaging just 39.1 days on the market compared with 65.5 days for trucks overall. Used Cybertrucks sold faster than any other truck, including the Ford Maverick Hybrid, which held the No. 2 spot, and the Rivian R1T, in third place.

According to iSeeCars.com, the Cybertruck’s sales performance lines up with a broader pattern in the used vehicle market: Used battery electric vehicles are hot sellers right now, with 16 of the 25 fastest-selling used cars fully electric. EVs are even edging out hybrids in terms of average days on market. EVs average 45.6 days on market compared to 54.5 days for hybrids and 61 days for gasoline-powered vehicles. Not every EV is selling well, however. For instance, iSeeCars.com found that the Cadillac Escalade IQ was the slowest-selling vehicle in the study, averaging 134.3 days on the market, more than twice the overall industry average.

Karl Brauer, executive analyst at iSeeCars.com, told TechCrunch that high gasoline prices have changed consumer-buying habits, prompting more people to opt for an EV or hybrid over an internal combustion engine vehicle. If high prices persist, as they have since March, that trend could continue.

Keep in mind that these were October sales, the month before Musk announced Tesla would stop making the Cybertruck in 2027. Fuel prices and the relative rarity of Cybertrucks in the used car market were driving demand even before the cancellation announcement, Brauer said.

“The Cybertruck has only been out for a few years, so you have a lot of used ones coming off lease and entering the used car market for the first time,” he said, adding that used Cybertrucks are cheap relative to their MSRP.

The impending cancellation will likely fuel used Cybertruck sales, although the pace may ebb and flow over the next year.

“The canceling of the new Cybertruck is making people kind of aware — it’s like, oh wow, these things aren’t going to be coming anymore,” Brauer said. “Whatever demand is for them, supply will not be continuing to grow.”

Brauer said that once production of the Cybertruck ends — which is expected in summer 2027 — sales of used versions may pop once again.

“When the last one is being produced, that’ll probably reignite things again, and for a few months after that,” he said.

#104. Jensen Huang broke Nvidia’s code of conduct, and his employees may not forget it

When Jensen Huang gave his first all-hands address since returning as CEO, there was an odd emphasis on Nvidia’s code of conduct. He admitted to violating the code, then emphasized that no one was exempt from it. But that “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door,” Huang said, according to Wired. “Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments” still matter. In other words, the code applies to everyone, but breaking it doesn’t automatically cost anyone their job, including, apparently, the CEO.

It’s an awkward stance for a company whose CEO has admitted to breaking its rules. A central part of modern human resources, a code of conduct can seem like a quasi-legal rulebook that lays out unacceptable behavior. But experts say most codes are better understood as a reflection of corporate culture. When an infraction goes unpunished, the main risk isn’t legal but cultural — that employees will feel embarrassed or even angry about the company they work for. That’s the risk Huang now faces at Nvidia.

Matt Plass, chief market strategy officer at LRN, an ethics and compliance training outfit, recently surveyed more than 2,000 workers across different industries for a study on workplace codes of conduct. As Plass sees it, the biggest risk is that employees will become ashamed of their workplace.

“People coming into the workforce now, ethics is incredibly important to them,” he told TechCrunch. “They’re really mindful of the ethical standards of their company. They’re really embarrassed and angry when senior leadership lets them down.”

The consequences of violating the rules aren’t always tangible, but they’re definitely real. Plass gave one example of a company that banned corporate travel to cut costs — only to have its CEO turn up in a VIP box at the Olympics.

“For over a decade afterwards, ‘the Olympics’ was used within that company as an example of the worst thing an employee could do. People would say, ‘Yeah, I shouldn’t have done that, but it’s not like I went to the Olympics.’”

Plass says companies that remove an executive typically do so after an investigation, then try to win back employees. “In most organizations they make a change at the top based on what’s happened,” he said, “and then they will have a hearts-and-minds initiative, to go out to their employee base and their customers and their investors and everybody else, and say, we get it. We’re sorry. We’ve changed. That’s essentially what they do.”

Of course, that playbook is harder to follow at a company like Nvidia, where Huang left without a clear succession plan, and there are serious doubts about whether the company could continue to thrive without him. In cases like that, Plass acknowledges there’s no easy answer.

“I would draw a distinction there between the kind of companies where the CEO is a kind of godhead figure … where the person and the company are almost impossible to separate,” he said. “There it’s much, much harder to deal with.”

In that context, Huang’s stutter-step over the code of conduct is easier to understand. Judging by the employee campaign for his reinstatement, Huang’s return hasn’t created a morale problem so far. But as the coming months put more pressure on Huang and his colleagues, sidestepping the code could end up costing Nvidia something harder to measure — its employees’ pride in the company.

#99. Arsenic in the water: Thailand presses Myanmar over mining pollution

Thailand is seeking to tackle river pollution stemming from rare earth mines in neighboring Myanmar. The two countries held joint talks this week as a "first step towards agreeing on the evidence."

Thailand is pushing its neighbor Myanmar to take on unregulated mining.

A toxic border river is putting lives and livelihoods at risk in northern Thailand. The Kok River, once rich in fish, is now polluted with arsenic, mercury, and other hazardous contaminants.

The reason is the mining activity in the militia-controlled regions of Myanmar. Most of these mines are located in Shan state, where civil war has raged since the 2021 military coup.

The mining activity is driven in part by China's dominant rare earth industry.

Bangkok this week hosted the first Thai-Myanmar working group where it said tests proved rare-earth and gold mines in Myanmar were the reason of the pollution downstream. China was also present in the talks.

The two sides agreed to joint water sampling on the Kok and Sai rivers. This will cover 12 water sampling locations and is expected to begin between December and January.

But no public agreement was made about Thailand's request for a six-month suspension of mining operations.

The talks follow the suspension of tap water supplies to 14 villages in Mae Sai district earlier this month, near the Myanmar border, after arsenic levels were found to be nearly 30 times higher than government limits.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Bangkok is also seeking China's help in pressuring private Chinese operators, given its position as the main importer of rare-earth materials from Myanmar, as the issue grows increasingly urgent after months of concern.

Authorities this week urged residents to avoid eating certain shellfish and other river species after tests detected dangerous levels of arsenic and lead in northern and northeastern Thailand.

"Arsenic does not come from nature, it came from mining sites. The source was from (Myanmar's) Shan state, no-one disagrees," Thai Deputy ​Prime Minister Yodchanan Wongsawat said at the joint talks.

However, finding a solution remains a challenge.

Thanapon Piman, senior research fellow at the Stockholm Environment Institute (SEI), told AFP, "This is going to be a first step to agree on the evidence, otherwise you cannot move to the next step, like stopping mining activity.”

But Myanmar intervening to stop mining would be "really difficult," he added.

Several ethnic armed groups have been active in the region for decades, while the civil war is still ongoing.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Thai authorities point to two gold mines across the border from Mae Sai as the main contributors to elevated arsenic levels in the Sai River.

The broader region is divided among various groups, including Myanmar's military and the United Wa State Army, an ethnic armed group closely aligned with China.

The main mining belts are currently outside the control of Myanmar's military-backed government, including sites in Shan State.

Thai authorities are also seeking to assess whether pollutant mines are under the control of Myanmar's government or other entities, Deputy Prime Minister Yodchanan told Reuters.

"We also would like them to check whether they can control every mine or they cannot. If they cannot, we will ask China to help,” he said.

A spokesperson from China's foreign ministry told AFP that Beijing "pays close attention" to the pollution issue and would provide technical support.

#97. FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.

The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”

The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.

FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”

Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”

This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.

SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.

The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.

SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.

The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

#93. S&P considers Nvidia credit downgrade over governance and ‘key man’ risks

S&P Global Ratings, one of the three major credit rating agencies, is considering downgrading Nvidia’s rating, citing concerns about the company’s management and governance practices.

Despite Nvidia’s strong financial performance, the ouster and reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang has raised concerns about “weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning” and “key man risk,” meaning heavy reliance on Huang for its future success, S&P said in a statement.

The agency expects to finish its review within 90 days and could cut Nvidia’s rating from AA to AA-, the fourth-highest on its scale.

Although Nvidia is certain to keep its investment-grade rating, a downgrade could raise its borrowing costs. Because Nvidia backstops neoclouds and other infrastructure companies that lack strong credit on their own, a lower rating could make their debt costlier or force them to post more collateral.

In August, Nvidia announced plans to arrange $500 billion in financing for AI infrastructure with six financial institutions, including Goldman Sachs, Apollo, and Blackstone. Under the original terms, Nvidia would guarantee 25% of the loans. But even before Huang’s ouster and return, lenders had been demanding that Nvidia guarantee more, Bloomberg reported.

A downgrade would likely prompt lenders to demand even larger guarantees.

#92. Nine injured, three critically, after Bangkok oil refinery explosion

Watch: Fire and smoke billows out of refinery owned by energy company Bangchak Corporation

Nine people have been injured after an explosion at an oil refinery in Bangkok, authorities have said.

Three of them are in critical condition, according to news agency AFP, which said the local hospital had revised its earlier assessment of four.

A fire broke out after the blast at the Phra Khanong facility run by energy giant Bangchak Corporation in the east of Thailand's capital at around 14:45 (07:45 GMT).

The company said the fire was brought under control and put out within 30 minutes, adding that the cause of the incident was being investigated.

Black smoke could be seen across Bangkok's skyline after the blast, as local officials told residents to leave the area.

An alert sent to mobile phones by Bangkok authorities confirmed that the fire was under control, but urged people to avoid the area.

Eyewitnesses shared video clips on social media of flames and smoke rising above the refinery.

On a visit to the scene, Thailand's energy minister Akanat Promphan said it appeared the impact of the explosion was limited because the fire started in a distillation tower about 100m (330ft) from where oil was being stored.

He said the incident was concerning as the refinery was in a residential area and the government was suspending production until it could be confident that operations would resume without any issues.

Bangchak Corporation said air quality monitoring in the immediate area around the refinery was within normal ranges.

It confirmed that production at the site, which can process up to 120,000 barrels of oil a day had been shut down. The company has a second refinery in Chonburi province, about 50 miles (80km) from Bangkok, handling a daily capacity of about 180,000 barrels.

An explosion occurred at the Bangkok refinery in July 2012, sparking a fire but no reported injuries.
