#91. Dutch authorities seize 26 critically endangered turtles from luggage of passenger bound for Hong Kong

The tiny Vallarta mud turtle was only discovered in 2018. It's critically endangered with around 300 thought to live in the wild. Almost one tenth of that number were stashed in a traveler's luggage, bound in duct tape.

The Netherlands Food and Consumer Product Safety Authority (NVWA) said on Friday that it had seized more than two dozen critically endangered turtles that customs officers found in a passenger's luggage at Amsterdam's Schiphol Airport.

The Vallarta mud turtles discovered in the luggage of a man flying to Hong Kong via Schiphol are endemic to Mexico. They were only formally discovered and catalogued in 2018 and are one of the smallest of all known species of turtle.

"These highly rare and protected turtles were presumably taken from the wild in Mexico, where an estimated population of only about 300 of these animals remain," the NVWA said in a press release.

Dutch customs agents discovered the animals on November 21 during a regular inspection and then alerted the NVWA, as the responsible agency for such cases, on realizing that they had uncovered live fauna.

Police then arrested the man at the NVWA's request, and the NVWA said in Friday's statement that he was still in custody, with federal prosecutors continuing their investigations.

"Most of the turtles were packed in a box alongside a toy car, while others were placed inside a sealed sock within a suitcase," the NVWA wrote. "The animals had been wrapped in duct tape, which prevented them from moving. All of the turtles survived the journey."

Authorities said the turtles were being housed at a specialized rescue facility, "where the duct tape was carefully removed," and where they were "receiving the care they require."

Vallarta mud turtles are exceptionally small freshwater turtles that only entered the taxonomical record in 2018, endemic to an area in western Mexico.

Relatively little is known about the creature owing both to its recent discovery and the damage to its habitat caused by urbanization and a growing tourist trade in the Mexican state of Jalisco.

The Turtle Island organization in Graz, Austria — which recently started breeding the turtles in Austria as a "genetic reservoir for eventual rewilding" and is working to help preserve them in Mexico — considers it "the world's smallest turtle species," and estimates that "over 99%" of its natural habitat has been lost to urbanization.

Roads and traffic are a particular risk for the slow-moving creatures.

"Despite strict protections, their small size and striking appearance make them prime targets for illegal collectors," Turtle Island says when detailing other risks to the tiny animals, that are not known to grow larger than 10 centimeters (less than 4 inches) in length.

The IUCN Red List classifies it as "Critically Endangered" and it is subject to the highest level of protection under the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES).

"The suspected poaching of 26 of these turtles from the wild may have reduced the population by nearly 10%," the NVWA wrote, basing its figure on the estimated population of around 300 of the turtles in the wild.

The agency urged people to report any suspected instances of illegal trade of protected animal or plant species, whether "via online forums, marketplaces, bird fairs, or antique and curiosity markets."

#89. Grand Theft Auto VI proves why efficiency isn’t a virtue

It should have been impossible for “Grand Theft Auto VI” (GTA VI) to live up to the hype. Fans waited 13 years for the newest installment in the crime-adventure franchise, and Rockstar Games exceeded even those inconceivable expectations when the game finally came out last week.

GTA VI launched as the highest-rated game of all time on Metacritic and made $3.79 billion on release day to mark what could be the biggest entertainment event ever – more massive than “Avengers: Endgame” ($2.9 billion) or Taylor Swift’s Eras tour ($2 billion).

The 13-year wait certainly fueled the frenzy, and sales, around GTA VI, but some fans craved GTA VI because of what it represents in the emerging era of AI.

Even the biggest workaholics among us – startup founders – scheduled time off to play the game, including Spiry co-founder Dima Beseda, who told TechCrunch, “I reshuffled my workload to free up time around the weekends and even booked vitamin IV drips to keep my energy up.”

Yet some players felt a sense of anticipatory nostalgia as they wiped away a layer of dust on their Xbox to play the game they’d been looking forward to since they were kids.

“We do have a view that GTA is likely to be the last game of its kind to be made this way,” Ben Feder, managing partner at TIRTA Ventures and former CEO of Rockstar parent company Take-Two Interactive, told TechCrunch.

That’s because GTA VI was created entirely without generative AI.

“We’re kind of at this technological moment where this way of developing has sort of reached its natural frontier,” Feder said. “I don’t think you can say that games like this will always be around.”

As millions of people immersed themselves in Vice City, the tech industry witnessed another long-awaited moment: Anthropic became the first major AI lab to go public. At Nasdaq HQ, Anthropic executives gathered to celebrate the Claude maker’s $81.3 billion IPO. At virtually the same moment that Anthropic broke the record for the largest IPO ever, GTA VI was also breaking records with the highest-rated and best-selling game launch of all time.

But when the Anthropic team rang the bell on Thursday, the sound also signaled the end of an era in video game development, and perhaps even in the arts at large.

The AI industry operates with efficiency as its north star. The promise of this technology is that we will be able to automate so much work that we can expand the capabilities of humanity, avoiding needless time spent tinkering with ultimately fruitless ideas. Instead, a prompt goes into an LLM, and a response is spat out.

This technological revolution exploded into the world with some soul-crushing side effects: children are inundated with mind-numbing slop on YouTube, lawyers cite nonexistent cases in court, and tech companies like Meta lay off tens of thousands of people as part of a corporate-branded “Year of Efficiency.” But against the backdrop of the AI industry’s first major IPO, Rockstar has unveiled a game that about 5,000 people labored over for more than a decade.

The final product is breathtaking, looking so lifelike that it can be hard to distinguish gameplay footage from real life. These sprawling open-world games are usually made possible by procedurally generating new landscapes and objects, but Rockstar has taken the time to painstakingly craft every detail of the world, even developing 30,000 different nonplayable characters with unique appearances and personalities.

The video game industry is already grappling with the threat of AI. Google’s Project Genie, which launched earlier this year, allows users to create and wander around 3D worlds generated from prompts to an LLM. The stock prices of several video game companies faltered around the time of Project Genie’s release, perhaps because of investors’ uncertainty about the future relevance of video game developers.

“The notion that somehow new tools would allow an individual to push a button and generate a hit, market a hit, and bring it to many millions of consumers around the world, it’s a laughable notion,” Take-Two Interactive CEO Strauss Zelnick said at the time. “It’s just never been the case with entertainment.”

Zelnick’s fellow tech CEOs see different future, though. On a Meta earnings call this year, CEO Mark Zuckerberg promised that, “soon, you’ll have an AI that understands you and [can] even generate great personalized content for you.”

Elon Musk even went as far as proposing that a game like GTA VI could be made with AI.

“You won’t even have to ask,” Musk wrote on X. “AI will figure what video game you’d like best.”

This is the paradox of “Grandthropic” week. GTA VI is so impressively vast and captivating because its developers could make mistakes, experiment, and rework ideas, even if that meant delaying the game (twice). GTA VI was incubated with the exact inefficiencies that investors hope AI companies like Anthropic can help eradicate.

“Plenty of fantastic art — video games and elsewhere — almost by definition has to be created slowly, and there’s something to having that kind of inefficiency around it,” Feder said. “Creative output requires deletion, right? Getting to kind of a masterpiece oftentimes requires an enormous amount of deletion. And when you’re talking about a game of the kind of complexity we’re talking about, that kind of deletion ends up being very expensive, and it is by definition not efficient.”

GTA VI reeks of its own humanity – every small detail is loaded with intention, but it’s this impressive precision that keeps the world abuzz, triggering the kind of monocultural moment that has become increasingly rare in a media landscape that revolves around highly personalized social media algorithms.

The game forces the player to slow down, which is either frustrating or genius, depending on your sense of humor. People waited more than three presidential terms for the next installment in a high-octane action franchise, only to download a 212 GB behemoth of a game in which your earliest missions involve driving around doing errands and putting up drywall to make your girlfriend happy. Don’t worry — there are still car chases and shootouts — but in a moment when AI feels suffocating, GTA VI is such an artistic achievement that it is reaching audiences that don’t even like violent video games.

The ultimate fantasy of GTA VI is no longer to role-play as a criminal — the fantasy is just taking your time to relish the mundane and explore an expansive world that developers were given the time and space to perfect.

Amanda Silberling is a senior writer at TechCrunch covering the intersection of technology and culture. She has also written for publications like Polygon, MTV, the Kenyon Review, NPR, and Business Insider. She is the co-host of Wow If True, a podcast about internet culture, with science fiction author Isabel J. Kim. Prior to joining TechCrunch, she worked as a grassroots organizer, museum educator, and film festival coordinator. She holds a B.A. in English from the University of Pennsylvania and served as a Princeton in Asia Fellow in Laos.

#88. Tributes for Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor after his death aged 65

Watch: Taylor on the 'overwhelming reaction' to Duran Duran's music

Simon Le Bon, Sir Rod Stewart and Gary Kemp are among the pop stars who have paid tribute to Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor following his death at the age of 65.

Taylor co-wrote some of the group's biggest hits including Save A Prayer, Rio and Girls On Film.

Le Bon and his bandmates paid tribute, saying "he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music", while Sir Rod remembered his "dear pal" and Spandau Ballet's Kemp called him "a great player who set the tone for the 80s".

Taylor's wife Tracey called him "the rock of our family", adding: "We will miss him every single day." He died after "a long and courageous battle with prostate cancer", Duran Duran said in a statement on Wednesday.

Andy Taylor: Duran Duran's livewire guitarist who was a Wild Boy on stage and off

He died "peacefully at home, surrounded by his family, on Monday 23rd November", a statement said.

Paying tribute, his Duran Duran bandmates said: "From the moment he arrived in Birmingham from the North East with a guitar and an unshakeable belief in what we could become, he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music.

"His playing drove the songs that took us around the world, and with a dry wit that was matched only by his wonderfully unsentimental, no-nonsense outlook on life he kept us grounded and gave the band its edge through the extraordinary years that followed."

They said their thoughts were with his wife, children and grandson.

Le Bon said there was a "a massive Andy Taylor shaped hole in my heart".

"Andy never took the easy musical route. His overdrive overdrove us into being the only funk/rock/punk band in the New Romantic movement, with songs that still survive, decades later."

Watch: Andy Taylor tells the BBC about his cancer diagnosis in 2023

Sir Rod, who wrote five songs with Taylor for the 1988 album Out of Order, recalled them making "some good music together" and having "some outrageous fun".

Kemp said Taylor "influenced many of us along the way with his sparse staccato style", adding: "He brought shameless rock to the mix."

In a band tribute, Spandau Ballet called him "a hugely talented musician and an important part of the British music scene".

US guitarist Nile Rodgers, the co-founder of Chic, recalled "countless great days and nights" with his "dear friend".

Belinda Carlisle said she was "blessed" to have worked with Taylor, who played the guitar solo and was in the video for her song Mad About You.

Former Led Zeppelin frontman Robert Plant called Taylor "a tough fighter", while Then Jerico's Mark Shaw remembered him as "one of the greatest musicians and songwriters I've ever heard and had the privilege to work with".

Taylor (second left) married his wife Tracey at the Chateau Marmont hotel in Los Angeles in 1982

Taylor's wife Tracey, a hairstylist he met during the early days of Duran Duran, and whom he married in 1982, said: "For all the incredible experiences music brought him, family always came first for Taylor, adding: "Nothing made him happier than having us all together."

She said he faced the last few years "with so much courage and determination" and said they were "grateful" for the "kindness and support shown to him by fans throughout his illness", saying it brought him "immense comfort and strength".

Andy Taylor's guitar riffs powered some of Duran Duran's best-known songs

The son of a fisherman, Taylor taught himself guitar, bass, and drums and began playing professionally at age 13.

He played with several bands around the UK before answering a music paper advertisement for a "live-wire guitarist", which led to his audition for Duran Duran in Birmingham in April 1980.

The group went on to achieve global fame through the New Romantic movement, which combined slick, melodic synth-pop with aspirational music videos and chiselled good looks.

Taylor's guitar lines - full of glassy, powerful riffs - were often a highlight of their songs, from the choppy opening of Girls on Film to the sleazy scuzz of Hungry Like The Wolf.

Duran Duran bridged the sounds of pop, funk, disco, punk and new wave with a sound that remains indelibly tied to the 1980s

Duran Duran were one of the biggest pop acts of the 1980s, and its five members became global hearththrobs.

Their indelible hooks and airbrushed music videos found a natural home on the newly launched MTV, giving their career a huge boost in the US.

They became so popular in the US that the press labeled them the leaders of a "second British invasion" of the charts - alongside groups like Spandau Ballet, Human League and Eurythmics.

During a hiatus in 1984, Andy and John Taylor enlisted Robert Palmer for their successful but short-lived side project the Power Station.

The following year, the guitarist decided to quit Duran Duran after a miserable experience performing at Live Aid.

"We'd reached rock bottom and we had absolutely nothing to say to each other," he wrote in his autobiography, Wild Boy.

"We drove to the venue through streets packed with excited rock fans, but inside the bus the atmosphere was as if we were on a way to a funeral."

He went on to play on Palmer's Addicted To Love, and released a solo album, Thunder, in 1987.

Taylor quit Duran Duran at the height of their fame in 1985, but rejoined the band several times in the 2000s and contributed to their most recent album, Danse Macabre

After a period writing and producing albums for other artists, he rejoined Duran Duran in 2000, lasting for just one album (2004's Astronaut) before parting ways again.

In 2022, when the band were inducted to the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame, Taylor was notably absent from the ceremony, despite public plans for a reunion.

When the group took the stage, Le Bon read a letter from the guitarist, in which he revealed his diagnosis with stage 4 metastatic prostate cancer.

Nonetheless, he contributed to the following year's Halloween-themed album Danse Macabre, marking his final collaboration with the band.

The following BBC programmes will pay tribute to Taylor:

Duran Duran at the BBC, on BBC Four on Friday 27 November at 21:30 GMT

Sounds of the 80s, on Radio 2 on Saturday 28 November at at 18:00 GMT

#86. A month with the iPhone Duo has changed how I use my phone

For years, the definition of a premium smartphone depended on its cameras, processors, and how light it felt when you stowed it in your pocket. The iPhone Duo challenges that concept by asking consumers to pay more for a fundamentally different smartphone experience, even if it means sacrificing what they’ve long thought of as high-end features.

In a little under a month of using the Duo, I’ve found that the trade-offs I was initially concerned about matter far less to me than I had expected. What’s been more interesting is how the Duo’s foldable form factor has changed my everyday habits, including how I interact with apps, which apps I use, and how I organize and personalize the phone itself.

I also easily forgot a number of my initial concerns about switching to the Duo. The apps, for the most part, are now optimized for the Duo; I’ve reacquainted myself with Touch ID; and I don’t miss the Pro Max camera on most days.

That’s not to say the Duo doesn’t have its shortcomings. I still miss that telephoto lens sometimes, and I’m still longing for Face ID — though not as much since Apple shipped a face unlock feature in the latest update. But after a month of using the Duo, I’ve found these compromises have had less of an impact on my day-to-day experience compared with the benefits that the new form factor brings.

I was surprised by how much I’ve grown to appreciate the Duo’s multitasking capabilities. Initially, I didn’t think I’d regularly use it to view two apps side by side, given the still fairly limited screen space even while the phone is unfolded, especially compared with an iPad. But I’ve found myself snapping apps to both halves of the screen more often than I had imagined.

For starters, Apple has made this side-by-side mode incredibly intuitive and easy to use — you simply drag the app over to the left or right side of the screen and drop it into the target box that appears.

I have regularly taken advantage of this feature to do all sorts of things.

I really enjoy keeping X open as I read through the news, which allows me to switch back and forth between the article I’m reading and real-time reactions to it. Sometimes I’ll keep email or Slack open on the side while I’m working on something else so I don’t miss an important message or notification.

And much like the overstimulated members of Gen Z (which I am not), I sometimes like scrolling social media while engaging with some other media off to the side.

Case in point: I passively watched Netflix’s “Love is Blind” reunion while reading the gossipy tweets and Reddit posts about the main characters on the other side of the screen. This sort of TV watching behavior used to be called second-screen viewing, but now it’s morphing into something new. Everything is happening on one screen, so should we call it “Duo screen viewing,” perhaps? It makes me long for even more interactive TV experiences where commentary and content are combined.

I’ve noticed another shift in my behavior since adopting the Duo as my main device. When I’m out and about, the Duo feels more like an (arguably heavier) iPhone mini. For those who still miss smaller iPhones, the Duo is the next best thing. Despite its weight, the phone feels small. You can cover it with one hand; you can type with a thumb; you can reach the top and bottom easily; and you can even fit it into your front and back pockets. The Pro Max now feels unbelievably strange in my hand — how was I ever comfortable wrangling this giant rectangle?

The Duo’s small screen works well when you’re on the go, especially for short interactions like responding to a quick text or pulling up directions to a destination. It’s not as great for mindless scrolling, which I appreciate, as it forces me to engage with the real world instead of killing every moment of boredom or unease by opening up apps.

But when you’re home or somewhere with time to kill, you can unfold the screen and enjoy your apps super-sized. It’s the best of both worlds.

I find that I instinctively unfold the phone when I’m preparing to watch videos, because the larger screen is best for that. It’s also unfolded if I’m shopping, as it’s more convenient for looking at products, zooming in to examine the details, or complete checkout forms.

Generally, I also unfold the Duo when I’m actively using apps. This setup has also allowed me to rethink my Home Screen layout: With the Duo closed, I prefer to see just widgets on the small screen with key information like weather, time, battery life, and so on. Meanwhile, the only app icons on the Home Screen are those in the dock.

In the weeks since its launch, most of the apps that weren’t yet optimized for the Duo have been upgraded to support its foldable form factor. In mid-November, Apple said there were over 200,000 Duo-ready apps, and in my experience, that certainly feels true. A majority of my so-called “daily drivers” — with the glaring exception of key Google apps like Gmail and Drive — have dropped the black band on the side of the screen that was frustrating early testers of the phone.

Most apps now take advantage of the available space, whether that’s on the Duo’s small front screen or the wider, unfolded one.

I’ve also found it interesting to see the different interpretations developers have had for how to best optimize their app for Duo.

Some have used the form factor as a way to bring in some sort of novelty: there’s a note-taking app that opens and closes like a notebook, with pages you can write on with the Apple Pencil; a retro Walkman-like music player; a tiny typewriter; a gaming handheld; a game where you close the phone to make grilled cheese.

As much as I love the whimsy of this group of apps, I appreciate how my everyday apps feel better to use on the unfolded screen. Spotify, at last, feels less cluttered when it has more screen real estate. A number of note-taking, list-making, and calendar apps also feel somehow more usable when spread out.

The Netflix app is actually fantastic with its support for multiple viewing modes, which include playing video on the closed outer display, the “laptop” orientation with its big player controls, and the “tent” mode.

Other developers have similarly reimagined how their app would work if it had originally been designed for the Duo’s small and larger screens.

One good example of this is Sofa, a handy utility for organizing the books, movies, TV shows, podcasts, albums, and games you want to check out. When the Duo is closed, its navigation is tucked off to the right side in a vertical bar, while the main screen focuses on the items you’ve saved. But when the Duo is unfolded, the number of items displayed on the screen also expands into a two-column layout, with more of your items featured on the screen and your lists on the left side. It doesn’t feel like two different apps, but like you’ve opened up the app itself and given it more space to spread out.

In terms of your daily social media scroll, apps like TikTok and Instagram have been updated to be perfectly usable on the Duo, whether the phone is open or closed. When closed, TikTok now stretches the video to cover the screen, instead of showing the ugly black bar. Scrolling short-form videos on the Duo, while unfolded like a slightly smaller iPad mini, has become my preferred way to watch.

Here’s another surprise: I’ve spent less time with my Kindle, and am instead reading books on my Duo. Unfolded, the Duo is a decent enough replacement for an e-book reader, albeit it can be a bit more stimulating as it’s not an e-ink device. However, the matte inner screen isn’t quite as bright and distracting as your typical glossy OLED display. There’s less glare and softer reflections, but there’s also something about the display that just feels calmer than a smartphone, allowing you to more fully engage with a book’s text.

Unfolded and held vertically, reading on the Duo feels a lot like reading on the standard Kindle, at least in terms of hand-feel. And putting your phone into the “Do Not Disturb” focus mode can help you not be distracted by incoming notifications and messages while you read.

You can also unfold the device while keeping it in landscape orientation, with the screens bent slightly inward like a real book. For even more fun, you can turn on Kindle’s page-turn animation, which makes it seem as if you’re turning the page in a physical book. And I love how you can “performatively read” using an app like Book2Life or Actually Read — they display the book’s cover on the Duo’s outer screen as you read the text on the inner screen.

E-book readers will likely find they can carry one less device when they’re out and about, because the Duo can stand in for whatever Kindle or other reader you typically keep in your purse or bag. You can save the Kindle for other times, whether that’s at home, on long travel days, or when you don’t want to worry about sand getting into your $2,000 phone.

On the big-screened iPhone 17 Pro Max I used to carry, I tended to keep a PopSocket of some sort on the back because the phone was both heavy and large, which made it hard to hold for longer periods of time. The Duo is similarly heavy, but its shape makes it far easier to hold in one hand without the extra help.

Folded, you can do the usual one-handed hold where the bottom edge of the phone rests on your pinky, your other three fingers cradle the back, and your thumb is free to navigate the screen.

Unfolded, the Duo’s like using a small Kindle or iPad mini, where two hands are required. You also don’t need a PopSocket for balancing the phone on the side, because it can be propped up like a tent or opened like a tiny laptop. Or, if you prefer, Apple and others offer Duo cases with a built-in stand. (Apple’s case comes in navy and taupe, though, so if you’re not boring, you’ll probably want to shop from third-party vendors).

If you think you still might need a PopSocket at times, or you just aren’t ready to give it up, the brand now makes a slimmer version: its Low-Pro Grip. There are other competitors, like the OhSnap grips, which aren’t a bad alternative either.

There’s something about the Duo that seems to have kicked off a new iPhone personalization trend, at least judging by what I’ve been seeing on social media.

Unlike a regular iPhone, where every phone case is simply a shell that offers a degree of protection based on the materials used, the Duo case lineup provides far more variety.

You can buy cases with and without built-in screen protectors, and some use adhesive to better stick to the outer screen, while others don’t. Some cases let you customize the band around the outer screen with a different color than the case that protects the rest of the phone.

Then you have cases that cover the hinge, while others leave it exposed. There are cases that offer holes where you can attach straps, wristlets, and charms. Some of those have attachment points at the bottom and others on the side.

Essentially, there are a lot of options for customizing how you want to carry the Duo and how you want to express your own style.

I’m also now starting to see whimsical cases that come with attached pens. (Thank goodness — most of the pen cases so far have been kind of plain!)

I also believe there’s something about the Duo that particularly appeals to women. Maybe because it’s small, or maybe it’s because you can attach a strap to its side and carry it like a little purse. Or maybe because, like a purse, you can deck it out with chains and charms — a choice that’s grown increasingly popular as young people have embraced whimsical, retro tech as a way to express their individuality and sense of fashion.

The Duo easily lends itself to this trend, perhaps even more than a regular iPhone. It becomes an accessory you want on display, not one buried in a bag.

Before switching to the Duo, I spent a lot of time thinking about what I’d be giving up by moving away from the Pro Max.

I’m surprised to find that, a month later, I only think about what I’d have to give up if I switched back to a traditional iPhone. It’s not just the extra space of the unfolded screen I’d miss — it’s all the little habits I’ve developed around having different screen sizes for different purposes.

The Duo makes me pause and think about how I want to use an app. That small moment of deliberation, before I even open the phone, has helped me break some of my more mindless smartphone habits, particularly around social media.

That’s not a benefit I could have anticipated if I’d judged the device by its specifications alone.

#85. Guinea-Bissau's military leaders delay return to civilian rule until 2028

Transitional President Gen Horta N'Tam said the postponement was driven by factors including final reasons

Guinea-Bissau's military rulers have postponed elections scheduled for next week by two years, delaying the return to civilian rule.

Transitional President Gen Horta N'Tam announced in a decree that the presidential and parliamentary elections, originally due on 6 December, would now take place on 26 November 2028.

The announcement came exactly a year after the military seized power, promising to return the country to civilian rule within 12 months.

In a statement, the presidency said an assessment of electoral preparations had found that there were insufficient "technical, material and financial conditions" to hold the polls as planned.

It said the delay would provide more time to ensure the elections were credible, transparent and inclusive, with all candidates given equal opportunities.

The presidency also reiterated its commitment to holding the country's first-ever local government elections but gave no date for the vote.

Gen N'Tam was sworn in as transitional president a day after the military overthrew President Umaro Sissoco EmbalÃ³ in November 2025.

The coup took place three days after general elections and a day before provisional results were due to be announced.

Both EmbalÃ³ and his main challenger, Fernando Dias, had claimed victory.

EmbalÃ³, who has since been living in exile in Morocco, was barred from returning to Guinea-Bissau last month, despite expressing an interest in contesting the elections.

Guinea-Bissau has experienced several coups and attempted coups since gaining independence from Portugal in 1974.

#83. Serbia's Student List runs chemistry professor Radomir Saicic for president

After ousting strongman Aleksandar Vucic, Serbia's student movement has now nominated academic Radomir Saicic for the upcoming presidential vote. Saicic accused the "outgoing regime" of trying to collapse the state.

A long-time supporter of the Serbian student movement, chemistry professor and researcher Radomir Saicic, is set to run for president on behalf of the Student List in late December.

Saicic accepted the nomination on Friday, a day after Serbia's new parliament scheduled the ballot for December 27.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In his acceptance speech, the Belgrade University lecturer said state institutions, including its president, needed to "win back public trust."

"It's time to restore dignity to the office of Serbian president," he said.

"Once we can agree with each other about things that matter to all of us despite our differences, Serbia will have the strength to decide its future on its own, and to have its voice heard and respected in the world," he added.

The 65-year-old is one of the lawmakers in the new assembly, which was formed in the wake of the Student List ousting the regime of Serbia's longtime strongman ruler Aleksandar Vucic at the parliamentary polls last month.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Vucic is banned by the constitution from running for president after winning two terms. He has hinted at his party boycotting the presidential vote.

While the office of the Serbian president is officially far less powerful that the office of Serbian prime minister, Vucic managed to organize his rule around himself during his time as prime minister and then hold on to levers of power while serving as president.

On Friday, Saicic accused the "outgoing regime" of "still trying to collapse our state."

"This past decade was marred by the breakdown of the rule of law, corruption, and hate speech," he said.

Saicic, a member of the Serbian Academy of Sciences and Art, is seen by many as a moderate nationalist and a traditionalist.

He has notably described the neigboring, Albanian-dominated Kosovo, as "temporarily occupied territory," keeping in line with Belgrade's long-running policy of treating Kosovo as a breakaway province.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The presidential candidate has also urged a "productive relationship" with Russia, pointing to Serbia's energy needs.

While seemingly reticent on the topic of the European Union, Saicic told reporters on Friday that he has a "fundamentally positive stance toward Europe and European values," pointing to examples including environment protection, academic education, the rule of law, science and technological progress.

"All of these things we should develop as much as possible, not for the sake of meeting some formal criteria, but to actually be what we are supposed to be," he said.

Saicic has sought to present himself as a devout Orthodox Christian while simultaneously criticizing the Serbian church leaders for aligning with the Vucic regime.

In his Friday speech, Saicic quoted the Bible, saying that "whoever wants to become great among you must be your servant."

#82. X finally gets parental controls after 20 years

After 20 years of existence, X, the social network formerly known as Twitter, has finally added parental controls for teenagers, giving parents and guardians a way to manage what their kids see and interact with on the platform. But unlike most platforms’ implementation of such controls, X is putting its own spin on the system by giving teens a say in the matter.

The company said accounts belonging to 13-to-17-year-olds will be set to “protected mode” by default, meaning they won’t be able to see sensitive content, and won’t be served targeted ads. However, X is allowing teenagers to retain a measure of control over their accounts, letting them see what restrictions were made to their accounts. Parents and guardians will also not be able to read their teens’ private messages, see search or viewing history, or post as their teen.

The controls let parents see how much time their teen spends on X per week, set daily limits, require their approval for new followers, limit feed recommendations and videos to already followed accounts, filter specific words, turn off sensitive media, specify who can send their teen DMs, and see who they are messaging.

The controls arrive months after X came under fire for a lack of controls preventing the platform’s AI chatbot from being used to generate sexualized images of children and women. The social network has also long been rife with child sexual abuse material, misinformation, AI bots, and openly lets users post porn.

X has generally not been a platform aimed at teenagers, but that doesn’t mean they don’t use the platform. A 2024 study found that nearly a third of Gen Alpha kids have an X account. The platform itself requires users to be at least 13 years old, though that’s not a high bar given the kind of content on the platform.

“Parents want practical tools, and teens deserve room to grow. With Parental Controls on X, families can set the guardrails that fit their home, while teens keep ownership of their accounts and can see every change. Striking that balance is vital and what we set out to build.” Michael O’Herlihy, head of safety at X, wrote in a blog post.

#81. Uganda to withdraw troops from DR Congo town amid diplomatic row

The planned withdrawal will end Uganda's nearly two-year campaign against ADF militants in Lubero

Uganda has announced plans to withdraw its troops from a town in eastern Democratic Republic of Congo within two weeks, as diplomatic tensions escalate between the two neighbours.

The troops will hand over security in Lubero, in North Kivu province, to Congolese forces after nearly two years of military operations, the Ugandan army said in a statement on Friday.

The announcement comes days after DR Congo summoned Uganda's top diplomat in Kinshasa over controversial social media posts by Uganda's military chief, Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba, who is the son of President Yoweri Museveni.

The Ugandan military did not give a reason for the withdrawal or say whether it was linked to the diplomatic row.

What are Ugandan forces doing in DR Congo?

The Uganda People's Defence Forces (UPDF) were deployed to Lubero to pursue fighters from the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) Islamist group fleeing joint military operations elsewhere in eastern DR Congo.

The ADF, which originated in Uganda in the 1990s, is affiliated to the Islamic State and has been blamed for numerous deadly attacks on civilians in both countries.

Uganda first deployed troops to eastern DR Congo under the current joint military campaign in November 2021, following a series of bomb attacks in the Ugandan capital, Kampala.

Ugandan army spokesperson Col Chris Magezi said that the joint military campaign, known as Operation Shujaa, would continue elsewhere in DR Congo.

The planned withdrawal applies only to Lubero town.

Five years into the joint offensive, the ADF remains DR Congo's deadliest armed group, despite a significant decline in its activities in Uganda, according to the conflict monitoring organisation Armed Conflict Location & Event Data (Acled).

John Ngono, Acled's DR Congo analyst, said the group was killing an average of four civilians a day and had expanded into previously unaffected parts of eastern DR Congo.

He said the findings suggested that "Operation Shujaa may have improved security in Uganda while shifting the geographical impact of violence within the DRC".

Acled says the group has not carried out an attack in Uganda since June 2025, but its growing interest in mineral resources in DR Congo could help finance its operations and sustain its ability to threaten both countries.

The planned withdrawal comes amid strained relations between Uganda and DR Congo, despite years of close military co-operation.

Earlier this week, Gen Kainerugaba appeared to endorse a rebel takeover of Walikale, a mineral-rich territory in eastern DR Congo, threatening that Ugandan troops would seize it themselves if the rebels failed to do so.

The Congolese foreign ministry described his remarks as "particularly serious" and demanded that Uganda clarify its official position and take steps to prevent similar incidents.

Gen Kainerugaba also defended Uganda's military presence in eastern DR Congo, insisting it was driven by security concerns.

The planned withdrawal comes amid renewed fighting between Congolese forces and rebels in the wider Lubero territory, raising fresh concerns about the safety of civilians.

Eastern DR Congo has endured decades of conflict involving dozens of armed groups, with millions of people forced to flee their homes.

#79. Is Alice Huang a spy or not?

The central, still-unproven claim in the Nvidia scandal is that Alice Huang, a former executive at the data-center company Megaspeed, is a Chinese spy. She had an affair with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who abruptly resigned on Nov. 12 after acknowledging it and was just as abruptly reinstated on Monday of this week.

What’s publicly known is murkier than the headlines suggest. U.S. intelligence has reportedly tracked Alice Huang’s contact with an officer of China’s spy agency, but officials haven’t offered public evidence that she was commissioned to target Nvidia’s chief. In the past week, after President Trump successfully pushed to bring Jensen Huang back, the administration has played down the allegations it once circulated internally. Whether that reflects new intelligence or political pressure is the big question.

Undisputed reporting shows the claim emerged from the White House itself. It first appeared in a highly classified memo written in March under the oversight of Secretary of State Marco Rubio and circulated to White House officials ahead of Trump’s trip to China in May. The memo formed the partial basis for the New York Times’ initial reporting on the scandal, published Oct. 29 and its existence was first reported on Nov. 8.

The memo did not definitively say, according to the Wall Street Journal, that Alice Huang was a Chinese spy. CNN, by contrast, has reported that the memo concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset.

What’s better established is that Alice Huang was in touch with Chinese intelligence. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) is Beijing’s civilian spy agency, responsible for both foreign intelligence and domestic counterintelligence. Last weekend, the Times, citing people with knowledge of U.S. intelligence reports, reported that Huang was in contact with an MSS officer from the spring of 2025 until this fall, a period that overlaps her relationship with Jensen Huang. (The Chinese government has called the spy claims a “smear,” and Megaspeed says Huang left the company in August 2025 and that its business is legitimate.)

Attorney General Todd Blanche, who was Trump’s personal defense lawyer before taking over the Justice Department, told journalists on Tuesday: “I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.” On the same day, Trump said a pardon was an option for Jensen Huang but that he did not think one would be needed. Senior intelligence officials reportedly told Congress that the government apparently has no actual evidence that the MSS directed Alice Huang to pursue an affair with Jensen Huang.

None of those statements necessarily exonerates Alice Huang as an intelligence agent. Instead, they support the alternate theory that the Chinese government didn’t tell her to begin the affair and that Jensen Huang broke no law by having it.

From a certain angle, the circumstantial evidence surrounding Alice Huang looks quite suspicious. She was a top executive at a company investigated for illicitly channeling Nvidia chips to China, and her husband, Kang Wei, is said to have even closer ties to the MSS, with some sources saying he worked for the agency.

The timing of the government’s reversal is at the heart of the matter. Trump has said he lobbied the board “directly and indirectly” to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO, so it wouldn’t be surprising if officials who serve at his pleasure were recasting the case in terms that satisfy his agenda. That wouldn’t necessarily make their assessments wrong. But unless new intelligence has actually resolved the question, and none has been publicly identified as yet, that backdrop is critical to keep in mind. CBS has also reported that Rubio has privately voiced concerns about aspects of Jensen Huang’s return.

Earlier this month, CNN reported that the intelligence community was still working to understand Alice Huang’s relationship with Jensen Huang and that the intelligence collected on her to date does not prove she was acting as a spy for Beijing. After a briefing by top intelligence officials earlier this month, Rep. Jim Himes (D-Conn.), the incoming chair of the House Intelligence Committee, said he was unsure whether she was a spy or a “useful idiot.” And this week, the Wall Street Journal pointed out that under Chinese leader Xi Jinping, top corporate executives routinely maintain ties to the country’s intelligence service without being formally deputized as assets.

The crush of conflicting claims, most from anonymous government sources, obviously leaves large questions unanswered as to what actually transpired. Some of those questions that naturally spring to mind:

There are answers to all of these questions, of course, but when the public will get them is anybody’s guess. Jensen Huang is due to appear before the Senate Banking Committee next week, and by the look from the statements of some of its members, he’s likely in for a rough ride. Unfortunately, congressional hearings, particularly public ones, rarely unearth secrets that haven’t already been disclosed.

#78. EU leaders fail to bridge budget splits as talks stall

Emmanuel Macron said it would be a "strategic mistake" to reduce EU spending

European Union (EU) leaders have deferred negotiations on its long-term budget after tensions surfaced over the bloc's spending priorities and aid for Ukraine.

Tense talks on the 2028-34 budget ended after 12 hours on Thursday at a summit in Brussels, with some members walking out of negotiations that will resume in December.

Divisions were largely split along the lines of countries, including France and Italy, wanting to approve a higher-spending proposal against those advocating for financial restraint, with Germany chief among them.

Many members want the budget locked in ahead of several high-stakes elections held across Europe in 2027, including in France.

Countries remain divided over a proposal to provide Ukraine with an additional loan of â¬45bn (Â£37.6bn), split between non-military and military funding.

Slovakia, the Czech Republic and Hungary have objected to the loan, but German Chancellor Frederich Merz said uniform EU agreement was a condition for approving the broader budget.

"I stand by my view that aid for Ukraine must be supported by the European Union as a whole," Merz told reporters.

"The European Union comprises of 27 member states, not 23 or 24."

Bulgaria, which previously opposed the plan, said it would agree to fund part of the loan that commits non-military financial support to Kyiv, marking a small sign of progress emerging from the special summit of the European Council.

EU members have been wrangling over the long-term budget after the European Commission submitted a â¬2tn proposal earlier this year, with some countries objecting to high levels of spending.

Those advocating higher spending want the funds to future-proof industries such as farming and invest in regional development. Fiscally cautious countries want to avoid amassing ballooning debt.

Thursday's talks were set up by the European Council to unblock the impasse, with a proposal to reduce spending by approximately â¬220bn to â¬230bn.

Under the current proposal, the budget totals â¬1.582tn, which includes â¬140bn to repay pandemic-era borrowing.

Four countries wanting to rein in the budget, including Denmark and Austria, walked out of negotiations after several hours of meetings.

Merz said there was "new movement" towards reducing the size of the EU budget, though he added "we are not there yet" when he confirmed that a new round of negotiations would not take place in Brussels on Friday.

European budgets requires approval from all 27 countries, with each country effectively holding veto power.

French President Emmanuel Macron said it would be a "strategic mistake" to lower long-term spending.

France's forthcoming presidential election came into focus during the EU budget talks, with frontrunner Marine Le Pen calling the talks an "underhanded political manoeuvring to thwart the democratic will of the French people".

Last month, Le Pen announced she would significantly reduce French aid to Ukraine and EU contributions if she were to win the presidency.

Macron addressed Le Pen's remarks on the sidelines of the Brussels summit, saying: "It is an odd kind of arrogance to behave as if she is already in charge of France's destiny because, as things stand, she has not been elected."

Once the talks concluded, the European Council announced a two-day summit on 17 and 18 December would revive budget negotiations.

Spanish economist Pablo HernÃ¡ndez de Cos was also named as the next President of the European Central Bank, replacing Christine Lagarde from February 2027.

#76. Nvidia hits pause on chip shipments to Southeast Asia

Nvidia is under pressure to do things differently, and a new WSJ report shows how far that’s going.

According to the Journal, Nvidia has told manufacturing partners, including Dell and Super Micro, to hit pause on server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia for an unspecified period as it assesses whether its regional partners are meeting U.S. export requirements.

As for why those countries, Chinese companies have been tapping Nvidia chips remotely from data centers there, a loophole the US Commerce Department closed — reportedly grudgingly — earlier this month. The Journal notes that the think tank Institute for Progress estimates remote data centers like these supply about 24% of China’s AI computing power.

#74. Venezuela to scrap law that bars opposition from public office

Jorge RodrÃ­guez said no politician had been subjected to the law's provisions

Venezuela will repeal a law that bars opposition figures from assuming public office, the speaker of the National Assembly Jorge RodrÃ­guez has said.

The legislation, which was introduced two years ago, has been criticised for enabling political persecution, but RodrÃ­guez said no politician had been subjected to its provisions.

The older brother of Venezuela's interim president Delcy RodrÃ­guez said he expected the reforms to be approved next week.

Delcy RodrÃ­guez's interim government has been negotiating with opposition parties since taking over at the start of the year, when former President NicolÃ¡s Maduro was seized by the United States.

The legislation, known as the SimÃ³n BolÃ­var Law, was passed shortly after the election in July 2024 in which then-incumbent Maduro was declared the winner amid widespread allegations of fraud.

Under the legislation, individuals who requested or supported foreign sanctions against Venezuela were permanently banned from political office and had their assets confiscated.

"Legislative processes are dynamic processes. That law responded to a very specific moment in a particular situation that Venezuela was going through," Jorge RodrÃ­guez said.

During subsequent talks, which began in August, the interim government and parts of the opposition agreed to review several other laws as part of a "route map towards democracy", according to an opposition statement.

Delcy RodrÃ­guez has promised that the South American country will hold free and fair elections, without setting a date.

Venezuelan opposition leader MarÃ­a Corina Machado is widely expected to run for office in the event of an election despite being excluded from transition talks.

Machado returned to the country in October after more than 10 months in exile.

In the 2024 election, Machado's ally Edmundo GonzÃ¡lez ran in her place after she was barred from running for office by Maduro's government.

The electoral authority, which was closely aligned with the government, declared Maduro the winner without making detailed voting tallies public.

The opposition argued that GonzÃ¡lez won the election, not Maduro, uploading copies of the voting tallies as evidence.

#73. Mushroom murderer Erin Patterson launches last-ditch High Court appeal

Patterson was last year found guilty of murdering three relatives

Australia's mushroom killer Erin Patterson has launched a final legal bid to overturn her murder convictions and avoid dying in jail.

Patterson, 52, was last year found guilty of murdering three relatives and attempting to kill another when she served them a toxic mushroom-laced meal in 2023.

Last month, in Victoria's Court of Appeal three judges dismissed an appeal against her convictions and threw out the possibility she could be released on parole â saying her crimes were "extraordinarily cold and calculated" and had to be "denounced in the strongest terms".

She has now made a last-ditch application to the High Court to look at her case.

Patterson had 28 days to file an application for special leave to appeal to the High Court, a deadline which expired on Friday.

She wants the country's top judges to examine both her conviction and sentence - but the court does not have to agree to look at the case. It only hears appeals in about 10% of cases.

After a high-profile trial last year, a jury found Patterson guilty of murdering her in-laws, Don and Gail Patterson, and Gail's sister Heather Wilkinson, by serving them individual portions of beef Wellington containing death cap mushrooms which she had tracked down, foraged and prepared.

Patterson was also found guilty of the attempted murder of Heather's husband Ian Wilkinson, a local pastor, who also ate the toxic lunch at Patterson's home in rural Victoria.

He survived after spending weeks in hospital and has ongoing health issues due to the poisoning.

Simon Patterson, the host's estranged husband, was meant to attend the lunch but cancelled at the last minute, in part due to his belief that his wife had repeatedly tried to poison him over several years.

Patterson was sentenced last September, with the trial judge allowing a non-parole period of 33 years due to the "harsh prison conditions" she would likely face. This glimmer of hope of freedom was thrown out by the appeal judges, who ruled she should never be released.

She had argued her conviction was unsafe on seven grounds - including a hotel booking mix up which saw the jury stay in the same place as the prosecution, a key police witness and the media.

Her lawyers said this was "catastrophic" and undermined the integrity of her trial, but the appeal court judges dismissed all the grounds for appeal.

#71. Kanal-Centre Pompidou, Brussels' most anticipated new art museum, opens its doors

A major addition to Europe's cultural scene: Kanal-Centre Pompidou, the new contemporary art museum in a former Citroen factory complex, is celebrating its opening with a free, nonstop weekend program.

Brussels is best known as the de facto capital of the European Union. But beyond its role as a political hub, it also boasts a thriving contemporary art scene.

Yet until now, the Belgian city didn't have a major museum of modern and contemporary art on the scale of Paris' Pompidou or London's Tate Modern. But this will now change with the opening of Kanal-Centre Pompidou on November 28.

The museum aims to "make Brussels a world-class cultural destination," on par with the Guggenheim in Bilbao or the MoMA in New York, Boris Dillies, the head of the regional government, told press agency AFP.

Behind this new museum is an ambitious architectural project involving the transformation of a vast industrial landmark into a multidisciplinary cultural institution.

Instead of building the new museum from scratch, Kanal was designed to occupy the former Citroen garage and factory complex, built in 1933-34 beside the Brussels canal.

Originally standing as a symbol of modernity and industrial progress, the factory was a space where cars were serviced and assembled, as well as displayed. The former showroom has been transformed into as a glass-and-steel space that rises 21 meters (some 70 feet) high.

Rather than erasing the building's industrial past, the project's architects — noAarchitecten, EM2N and Sergison Bates, working together as Atelier Kanal — decided to celebrate its original structures. Vast concrete volumes and industrial details remain visible, creating a dialogue between the site's history and its new cultural purpose.

Like London's Tate Modern, which transformed a power station into a museum, Kanal shows how industrial architecture can gain a second life without losing its identity.

"Kanal reveals how architecture can reinvent the museum typology, not only as a place for art, but as a stage for the city and its citizens," Atelier Kanal said in a statement, adding that their concept for the former Citroen factory ensured that the space would be "renewed without being erased, made precise without being overdetermined and open to continued transformation."

Covering around 40,000 square meters (430,550 square feet), the complex is one of the largest cultural buildings in Belgium.

Kanal has established a partnership agreement with the Centre Pompidou in Paris that has guided both the international outlook of the new museum, as well as its intention to remain rooted in Brussels' local culture.

"A place for all people" was the founding vision of the Centre Pompidou, articulated by its architects Richard Rogers and Renzo Piano as they designed the iconic institution in the 1970s.

Kanal's collaboration with the Centre Pompidou, which runs until 2031, gives the Brussels museum access to one of the world's greatest collections of modern and contemporary art. Visitors will be able to see works that normally reside in Paris, while also encountering pieces from Belgian collections and newly commissioned projects.

The Brussels museum has already announced some of the upcoming exhibitions that will be drawing from the Pompidou's collection during their five-year partnership: two photography exhibitions focusing on Robert Frank and Brassaï, as well as art exhibitions focusing on iconic figures of modernism: Robert and Sonia Delaunay, Georges Braque, Pablo Picasso and Henri Matisse.

The new institution, however, emphasizes that it's not a branch of the Pompidou. The partnership is rather envisioned as a dialogue between two museums with different perspectives and histories.

The Centre Pompidou itself has been closed for major renovations since September 2025, and is scheduled to reopen in 2030.

The headline exhibition, "A truly immense journey," brings together more than 350 works from the collections of Centre Pompidou, Kanal and numerous Belgian lenders, and will feature modern icons including Lygia Clark, Sonia Delaunay, Alberto Giacometti, Natalia Goncharova, Wifredo Lam and Henri Matisse.

Along with this show, nine other exhibitions fill the building.

One of them, "An infinite woman," focuses on how mass-produced image of a Mangbetu woman long served as a colonial symbol and how artists of African descent have reappropriated and subverted this imagery.

Another one, "Right to the City — Right to the Future," explores the architectural transformation of the Brussels' Quartier Nord, which was historically a working-class neighborhood that was largely cleared in the 1960s to become a high-rise business district — displacing over 15,000 residents.

Alongside modern and contemporary art, Kanal is dedicated to architecture, landscape and the performing arts.

This reflects a broader shift in the role of museums, which are increasingly expected to be places where people gather, exchange ideas and engage with contemporary issues rather than simply admire objects behind glass.

The opening program alone demonstrates the museum's ambitions, offering a 36-hour nonstop program of live performances, opening rituals, concerts, DJ sets, films, installations and workshops for children and families, with events open to everyone.

The exhibitions can also be visited for free during the inaugural weekend. Visitors, however, need to have previously booked a time slot; all 30,000 tickets were snapped up within 24 hours of their release.

#69. Ex-Kenyan governor jailed for 25 years over pregnant girlfriend's murder

It is rare for such a high-ranking official to face public criminal trials in Kenya

A former Kenyan governor has been jailed for 25 years for the murder of a university student who was seven months pregnant with his child.

The High Court on Friday sentenced Zacharia Okoth Obado, the former governor of Migori County, along with two former aides, over the 2018 killing of Sharon Belyne Otieno.

The investigation established that an "intimate relationship" had existed between Obado and Otieno, which "subsequently led to a pregnancy with the deceased that the governor was unhappy about".

Both Obado and Otieno's legal teams said they would appeal against the sentence.

His former personal assistant, Michael Oyamo, was also sentenced to 25 years in prison, while former county clerk Caspal Ojwang' Obiero received an 18-year sentence.

The court found that Obado had provided the motive and financial resources for the murder, while Oyamo coordinated its execution and Obiero provided logistical support and helped conceal the crime.

Judge Cecilia Githua said Obado's absence from Migori at the time of the killing did not absolve him of responsibility.

She rejected the prosecution's request for the death penalty, as well as Obado's plea for a non-custodial sentence.

Otieno, 26, was killed between 3 and 4 September 2018 after leaving a hotel in Rongo, south-western Kenya, with journalist Barrack Oduor.

She had contacted Oduor to discuss her relationship with the then-governor. The two were abducted shortly afterwards.

Oduor managed to escape by jumping out of a moving vehicle. His account of the abduction became a crucial lead in the investigation.

Otieno's body was later found near Kodera Forest.

A post-mortem examination found that she had died from strangulation and excessive bleeding caused by multiple stab wounds.

The court also established that Obado was the biological father of her unborn child.

Obado and his co-accused were convicted of murder on 23 July 2026, following a lengthy trial in which prosecutors called 42 witnesses and presented 81 exhibits.

On Friday, the court declined to award compensation to Otieno's family, saying there was insufficient evidence to establish the financial losses they had claimed.

Before sentencing, Otieno's mother had appealed to the judge not to send Obado to prison, saying his imprisonment would amount to "another death in the family".

She urged the court to consider a non-custodial sentence, arguing that Otieno's three children, from different relationships, needed his support.

But the judge said the family's circumstances could not override the court's responsibility to impose an appropriate punishment.

Otieno's family, through their lawyer Antony Opondo, told Kenya's Daily Nation that they would file an appeal seeking Obado's acquittal.

Otieno's murder sparked protests and renewed public debate about violence against women in Kenya.

A national survey found that at least 34% of women had experienced physical violence in 2022.

The case has also drawn attention because it is rare for such senior political figures to face public criminal trials in Kenya.

Obado served two terms as governor of Migori County in western Kenya, switching allegiance between a smaller political party and the region's dominant opposition movement.

He later backed President William Ruto and formally joined his ruling party in 2023, becoming a regional organiser.

#65. Millions of wives in South Asia unprotected from marital rape - study

Millions of women and girls remain unprotected in South Asia because of inadequate laws to deal with marital rape

Failure to fully criminalise marital rape leaves millions of women and girls unprotected in eight South Asian countries, where intimate partner violence is 35% higher than the global average, says a new study.

More than 100 countries have outlawed marital rape, but a large part of South Asia - India, Afghanistan, Sri Lanka and Bangladesh - remain among the three dozen countries which haven't.

The only South Asian country where rape within marriage attracts the same penalty as any other rape is The Maldives.

Nepal and Bhutan have criminalised marital rape, but apply lesser penalties, says the study by Samaj, a coalition of women's rights groups in the region.

India is among the three dozen countries where marital rape is still not outlawed

South Asia's marital rape laws largely stem from British colonial rule, says Ranjeeta Silwal of Equality Now, a global women's rights group part of Samaj.

The law, dating to 1860 in India and Bangladesh and 1885 in Sri Lanka, lists exceptions to rape - including sex by a man with his adult wife.

Campaigners say the law is outdated and forced sex is rape, regardless of the perpetrator. Experts warn marital sexual violence harms women's physical, psychological, emotional and sexual well-being.

More than 100 countries have criminalised marital rape, including the UK, which changed its law in 1991. But the exemption remains in India, Sri Lanka and Bangladesh.

In India, growing clamour to criminalise rape within marriage

Marital rape: Delhi high court gives split verdict

India government says criminalising marital rape 'excessively harsh'

Pakistan removed the marital rape exception in 2006, but no higher court has confirmed that rape laws apply within marriage. A recent high court ruling, meanwhile, granted husbands broad immunity for non-consensual sex.

Since returning to power in 2021, the Taliban have steadily dismantled women's protections in Afghanistan.

A new penal code introduced in January made matters worse, Silwal says. Under it, a husband can be prosecuted for violence against his wife only if he causes a broken bone, open wound or severe bruising. Even then, he faces just 15 days in prison if convicted.

Unsurprisingly, the code makes no mention of marital rape. But its narrow definition of punishable violence effectively denies women protection against it.

Intimate partner violence is much higher than the global average in South Asian countries

"The idea comes from the belief that consent for sex is implied in marriage, that marriage is a threshold where consent ends," Silwal told the BBC.

"But it shows a lack of awareness, it shows we are still treating women as property, that we are still very patriarchal."

The report comes amid renewed debate over marital rape across the region.

In India, 10 petitions challenging the law are pending before the Supreme Court, which is due to begin final hearings on 9 December.

Courts have delivered contradictory rulings over the years, with some judges acknowledging the law's archaic nature and urging parliament to criminalise marital rape.

There was outrage last year when a court let go a man whose wife had died after he brutally raped her.

But the Indian government, religious organisations and men's rights activists have opposed petitions in the top court, saying criminalisation of marital rape would be "excessively harsh".

India refuses to criminalise marital rape. This new series shines a light on it

India anger as judge frees man accused of raping wife who then died

Rights groups in Bangladesh have also challenged the marital rape exception, arguing it violates constitutional rights to life, liberty and protection from torture and cruel treatment, the report says.

"Those who oppose criminalising marital rape say it would undermine the family unit and break them apart. And family is very important in our countries," Silwal says.

"They say it will affect our religious and cultural values and worry that there would be false accusations against men."

Also a change in law, she says, may not necessarily mean justice for survivors.

Silwal points to Bhutan where marital rape is a criminal offence but it's treated as a petty misdemeanour and is punishable by one month to one year.

In Nepal, the punishment for rape is between six and 20 years, but it's only up to five years for a husband who rapes his wife.

#64. Divisions deepen between EU member states after tense budget summit

An EU leaders summit on the bloc's seven-year budget fell apart amid divisions over how to fund Ukraine. The budget proposal also includes a 3% tax on US tech companies, in a bid for the EU to generate additional funds.

EU leaders arrived in Brussels braced for a difficult showdown over the bloc's multitrillion-euro seven-year budget. But few expected the summit to unravel so quickly, with talks breaking down late on Thursday night.

Squabbles and thinly veiled public barbs meant they left without a deal and didn't even attempt to resume discussions on the planned second day.

There is a self-imposed deadline to find agreement before the end of 2026 on the budget which covers the 2028-2034 period. But experts say there is no cause for alarm, yet.

"Every time there are long-term EU budget negotiations, there are breakdowns, chaos, recrimination and a lot of political theater and drama," said Jacob Funk Kirkegaard, an EU economist and expert at the Bruegel think tank in Brussels.

The center of that political theater this time was the prime ministers of Denmark, Sweden, Belgium and Austria — countries wanting a reduced budget — who staged a walkout during smaller, group meetings, claiming the atmosphere had not been constructive to getting a deal.

Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar called the whole summit "completely unnecessary," pointing the blame at the man who had decided to call it and whose job it is to meditate the talks, European Council President Antonio Costa.

Costa was diplomatic in defending his decision to bring the leaders to Brussels, telling the press: "For me it was very useful to figure out the parameters of a possible landing zone in December."

Long before the summit started, Germany had laid down an ultimatum — Berlin would only sign off on the budget if all the other EU countries greenlit an additional €45 billion ($49 billion) support loan for Ukraine to fund the country in its war efforts through 2027.

Hungary, Czechia, Bulgaria and Slovakia have dug their heels in against the proposal, insisting that giving Ukraine money to buy weapons will prolong the war. Slovak Prime Minister Robert Fico even accused Germany of "blackmail."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

As Europe's biggest economy, Germany's contribution amounts to around 25% of the whole budget.

In a press conference after the summit, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said "hundreds of billions" still need to be slashed. The European Commission's original proposal of approximately €1.786 trillion had already been reduced by €204 billion (down 10.2%).

"Europe needs to exercise a sense of proportion," said Merz. "The budget cannot grow indefinitely, and the burden of financing it must be distributed fairly."

Merz also aimed a comment toward Costa, who has been renewing the details in the budget proposal at every stage.

"The proposals that have been on the table so far are not a basis for an agreement," said Merz.

Costa did tell leaders on Thursday that he is planning to cut between €220 billion to €230 billion out of the next proposal.

Berlin's budget reduction push is backed by six other countries who together call themselves the "Sensible Seven." All pay more into the collective EU financial pot than they receive out of it.

They are opposed by 17 EU member states who insist the total budget should grow, led by France and Italy. These countries call themselves the "Friends of Cohesion" — a nod to the EU cohesion funds which are used to share wealth from the EU's richer regions and nations to its poorer ones.

In the current proposal, agriculture, fisheries and cohesion spending falls from 59.1% to 42.4%, while money for economic competitiveness, security, and defense rises from 17.7% to 27.7%. This rebalancing is a key German demand.

The EU is also searching for a way to add money into the budget that doesn't come from national contributions. The current proposal includes a controversial EU-wide digital tax which could chart a course for a major battle with the US.

The idea, originally put forward by French President Emmanuel Macron, is for a 3% tax on revenues of online advertising, online marketplaces and on the monetization of user data. The money, to be paid directly into the EU's budget, is estimated to bring in €5 billion per year.

But the proposal is ringing alarm bells in Ireland, where US tech giants like Google, Meta, Apple, Microsoft and LinkedIn have their European headquarters.

"A digital tax is sending out all the wrong messages," said Irish Prime Minister Micheal Martin, who could still veto the proposal.

There is a fear in some European capitals that without being able to generate its own funds, future EU loans could downgrade the European Commission's unblemished standing on international markets.

The EU has around €860 billion in outstanding debts it took out for its pandemic recovery funding and to support Ukraine. Its triple-A credit rating could be downgraded if Marine Le Pen, the far-right front-runner in France's presidential election, wins in the spring and follows through on her promise to reduce French contributions to the EU budget.

EU member states that favor a financing model through national contributions rather than through tax revenue "understand very well what will happen if the EU loses its triple-A rating," said Bruegel's Kirkegaard. "Interest rate costs will go up even more. Who has to pay for that? Net contributing countries, like Germany, the Nordics, the Dutch, which is why they are much more open to this than in the past."

Other European tax experts believe that prior examples of these digital taxes show the costs outweigh the benefits.

"We analyzed the states which have a digital services tax [DST], and we've seen that economic cost exceeds the revenue generated," said Cristina Enache, an economist at the Brussels-based nonprofit Tax Foundation Europe. "Spain has a DST and the expected revenue was twice or three times larger than what they actually are collecting."

Enache called the tax proposal "politically impossible."

EU leaders know that the clock on their end-of-2026 deadline is ticking, and will return to the same conference rooms in late December hoping for a more conducive atmosphere to strike a deal.
