#63. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#60. Italy's former police chief rejected mafia inquiry allegations before death

Gianni De Gennaro worked in the anti-mafia directorate and became police chief before he headed Italian defence company Finmeccanica, later renamed Leonardo

Italian ex-police chief Gianni De Gennaro has denied misleading investigations into high-profile Mafia killings, in a letter written shortly before his death.

His body was found at his Tuscan country home on Saturday and Italian authorities believe he took his own life. A post mortem examination was due to take place on Friday.

In his eight-page note, De Gennaro, 78, vehemently denied obstructing the inquiry into the murder of anti-mafia judge Paolo Borsellino in Sicily in July 1992.

The Cosa Nostra Mafia was found to have ordered Borsellino's killing and that of fellow judge Giovanni Falcone two months earlier.

But several fruitless investigations were later launched into potential cover-ups by high-ranking officials.

In a recent inquiry De Gennaro, who in the 1990s worked in the anti-mafia directorate and later became Italy's police chief, was accused of misdirecting the initial investigation.

The allegations shocked many Italians, as De Gennaro had worked closely with Falcone and was seen as being on the front line of the fight against organised crime.

"In a few minutes I will be gone, so you understand well that a 'man condemned to death' cannot lie," he wrote in the letter, published by Italian daily Corriere della Sera. "I have never betrayed my oath of loyalty to the Fatherland."

Gianni De Gennaro (L) and anti-mafia judge Giovanni Falcone in 1992

"I hope that, amid the avalanche of falsehoods now overwhelming me, the truth will ultimately prevail," he added.

The case, and the media furore that has surrounded it, have re-opened the wound of the Mafia killings of the early 1990s, which remain an emotionally charged issue in Italy.

The story has dominated Italian headlines since De Gennaro's death, with several of the country's most senior political figures publicly commenting on the case.

On Thursday Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni said she had been "deeply shocked" by the case.

"His words struck us deeply, and I think the most important think we can do now is to help prosecutors who are working to clear things up," she added.

The letter was found next to a hard drive which he said contained notes from his 40-year career.

According to Italian media, investigators seized two hard drives, one from the home of De Gennaro's son and one from a former member of his security detail, and two USB sticks held by a close collaborator, who is also under investigation.

The sticks are reportedly labelled "Stato-Mafia", a likely reference to the alleged relationship between the Italian state and the Mafia, and "Massacres".

Forensic specialists are now expected to examine whether any files on the hard drives or USB sticks were altered or deleted, reports say.

#58. Rare turtles found inside baggage at Amsterdam airport

An official holds one of the seized Vallarta mud turtles - they are now receiving care at a specialised sanctuary

Twenty-six "very rare" turtles have been seized from a traveller's checked baggage at Amsterdam's Schiphol airport, a Dutch government agency has said.

The Netherlands Food and Consumer Product Safety Authority (NVWA) said a man flying from Mexico to Hong Kong via the airport was arrested on Saturday after the Vallarta mud turtles were found by customs officers.

The endangered species, thought to be the smallest freshwater turtles in the world with a maximum length of about 10cm (4in), were wrapped in duct tape to prevent them from moving.

The NVWA said they were "presumably stolen" from the wild in their native Mexico.

It said most of the turtles were found in a box with a toy car, while the others were in a closed sock inside a suitcase.

The arrested man remains in custody and an investigation is under way.

Meanwhile, the turtles are receiving care at a "specialised sanctuary", the NVWA said.

In 2025, the US NGO Turtle Island estimated that no more than 300 Vallarta mud turtles existed in the wild.

They are listed as critically endangered on a list published by the International Union for Conservation of Nature.

According to environmental news outlet Mongabay, a Vallarta mud turtle can sell for $10,000 (Â£7,500) or more on the black market in Asia.

Other cases of live turtles being smuggled to Hong Kong have been reported this year.

In October, customs authorities in Hong Kong were reported to have seized eight turtles, external that were thought to be endangered. The reptiles had an estimated market value of about $54,000.

Three months prior, Hong Kong customs detected two separate smuggling cases, external. Some 42 live turtles, with a total estimated market value of $210,000 were seized.

#57. France seeks German man in Channel migrant boat probe

A German businessman is accused of supplying equipment to smugglers organizing migrant crossings from France to the United Kingdom. However, he can't face formal charges until he is extradited to France.

France has issued an arrest warrant for a German businessman as part of an investigation regarding the migrant deaths in the English Channel.

Joint investigation by several media outlets including French daily Le Monde and the BBC showed that Marvin R.* of MaRe Boote, managing director of a small firm in western German town Werne, is suspected of being one of the main suppliers of inflatable boats smugglers use to journey across the Channel.

According to the media reports, the 35-year-old businessman is accused of supplying smuggling gangs with inflatable boats, engines and life jackets worth hundreds of thousands of euros.

UK authorities estimated that boats or engines linked to MaRe Boote were responsible for three in every 10 detected crossings during the first four months of this year, the BBC and Le Monde reported.

The prosecutor in the northern French city of Lille, Samuel Finielz, confirmed late on Thursday that "five people had been arrested and a German national was briefly detained in his country."

According to Finielz, this is part of the investigation about Channel crossings and sinkings since May last year.

The five others are now held in pre-trial detention and were suspected of offenses including "assisting illegal entry and residence as part of an organized criminal group, involuntary manslaughter, involuntary injury and endangering the lives of others," Finielz told the media.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Lille authorities in May issued an arrest warrant against "the German national implicated in this case," he said, on charges of manslaughter and endangering the lives of others.

Marvin R. was arrested and released by German authorities and now required to appear before a French court before January, the prosecutor added.

The BBC wrote that Marvin R. is contesting extradition to France. He can not be formally charged until he is extradited to France, it noted.

Migrants frequently cross the English Channel from France to the United Kingdom in small rubber dinghy boats. It is a dangerous journey as the Channel is one of the world's busiest shipping lanes.

More than 41,000 people crossed the Channel in small boats last year and there have been many deaths.

#56. Ukraine: How much have Europeans spent on aid?

Since Russia's full-scale invasion in 2022, EU member states have provided around €200 billion in aid to Ukraine, making them its largest contributors ahead of the United States. Germany leads in total financial contributions, with around €54 billion, roughly twice France's €27 billion. But the picture changes when aid is measured against GDP or per capita, with Denmark and other Nordic countries ranking among the biggest contributors.

#52. Clashes mar European games as Macron condemns violence

Fan violence marred the tie at the Stade Velodrome

Violent clashes marred a number of European football matches on Thursday night, with French President Emmanuel Macron condemning trouble at Marseille's home game against Levski Sofia.

There were also incidents at games between Turkish side Besiktas and Israel's Hapoel Be'er-Sheva in Georgia and Sunderland's 1-1 home draw with Poland's Jagiellonia Bialystok.

Uefa says it has appointed ethics and disciplinary inspectors "to conduct investigations into the incidents and examine all available evidence" from the Marseille-Levski Sofia and Besiktas-Hapoel Be'er-Sheva games.

European football's governing body said it "strongly condemns the serious incidents of violence and disorder" at those ties.

It added: "Violence, aggression, hatred and any form of provocative, discriminatory or extremist behaviour have no place in football.

"Uefa competitions are founded on the principles of respect, fair play, integrity and safety."

In Marseille, the build-up to the Europa League match at the Stade Velodrome was affected by incidents in the stands, including the throwing of projectiles and flares between the two sets of supporters.

With 10 minutes to go until kick-off, several Marseille fans ran from a home stand, appearing to pursue a group of Levski Sofia supporters.

They breached the stand designated for away fans, which housed around 500 supporters of the Bulgarian domestic champions.

"Five people were taken to hospital with eye injuries, head trauma and a dislocated shoulder," said the Marseille police prefecture. "Six others were treated on site for various wounds."

Macron spoke to Bulgarian Prime Minister Rumen Radev about the incidents, with the French president condemning "with the utmost force these acts of violence, which have no place in sport or in public space", and reassured him that "these acts would not go unpunished".

According to news agency Reuters, Radev thanked Macron for his personal involvement following the "attack on Bulgarian citizens" and for the medical assistance provided to the victims.

Bulgarian President Iliana Iotova said she found video footage of the incidents "shocking" in a statement posted on social media.

"The attacks against the Bulgarian fans desecrate one of the great games that brings millions of people together around the world," she added.

"Everyone should be able to support their team without being afraid for their safety."

Fan violence mars Marseille-Levski Sofia match

Police response to Besiktas fans' pro-Palestine banner 'unacceptable'

Sunderland held by Polish side Jagiellonia

Ligue 1 club Marseille said it was "working to understand the precise circumstances of these incidents".

The police source said all the people injured were Bulgarian and there was "no information at this stage regarding any arrests".

Security personnel managed to restore calm, allowing the game to kick-off on time, although there were a few further flare-ups between the fans, notably during the half-time break in a match Marseille won 1-0.

"It's a very regrettable episode. These are incidents which are unacceptable for football," said Levski Sofia coach Julio Velazquez

Marseille boss Bruno Genesio added: "We are living through violent times in our society, so this is very, very regrettable."

Besiktas fans clash with Georgian police at their game against Israeli side Hapoel Be'er-Sheva

Meanwhile, the Turkish Football Federation has strongly condemned the "unacceptable" actions of Georgian police towards Besiktas fans who unfurled a pro-Palestinian banner during their Europa League match against Israeli side Hapoel Be'er-Sheva.

Besiktas say they have lodged a complaint with European football's governing body Uefa over "disproportionate" use of force by Georgian police.

The match had been moved from Istanbul to Batumi, Georgia for safety and security reasons but had to be suspended in the 25th minute after clashes involving Besiktas supporters and local police.

Both the officiating team and the Besiktas players left the field, but play was able to resume 30 minutes later.

"We strongly condemn the unacceptable attitudes, behaviour and actions directed at Besiktas supporters inside and outside the stadium before and during the Uefa Europa League match," said a statement from the Turkish federation.

Turkish outlet NTV reported that Georgian police had gone into the stands and used pepper spray on the Besiktas fans.

The report added that there were anti-Israeli chants from the Turkish fans and that Besiktas players and director of football Onder Ozen spoke to supporters to try to calm matters before play restarted.

Hakan Fidan, Turkey's minister of foreign affairs, spoke to his Georgian counterpart Maka Botchorishvili following the incident.

"All necessary steps are being taken to ensure the safety of our citizens and protect their rights," a Turkish foreign ministry source said.

At Sunderland, there were crowd disturbances after Jagiellonia Bialystok fans were found in areas of the Stadium of Light with home fans, despite Uefa having imposed an away supporter ban for the Europa League game.

Police and stewards attended some disruption early on during the match.

"In the build-up to the match, the club had put measures in place in line with this sanction and maintained regular communication with our supporters, Jagiellonia Bialystok and Uefa," said a Sunderland statement.

"Unfortunately, Jagiellonia Bialystok failed to engage and cooperate on this matter.

"We are disappointed that these measures and communications were disregarded by some visiting supporters and we will review the circumstances surrounding their entry into the stadium."

#49. Back to Black Friday: 'We are now bombarded with offers throughout the year'

Black Friday was once a single day of holiday sales, held the day after Thanksgiving. Then retailers began kicking things off a day early, on Thanksgiving itself. “We are now bombarded with offers throughout the year,” says Hilary Ingham, Professor of Economics and Department Head at Lancaster University, who has no plans to shop this Black Friday. Ingham acknowledges a growing sense of “retail fatigue” but still sees the potential for a peak in sales. Meanwhile, an investigation by The Telegraph suggests that six in ten of the most popular products were actually more expensive than earlier in the year, underscoring the need for consumers to remain vigilant and track prices over time.

#48. Ex-media executive admits saying Australian child sex abuse survivor 'looking to be raped again'

Tame says her "whole body was prickling with distress" over what Handbury said.

An Australian former media executive has admitted he said a child sex abuse survivor and prominent advocate "must be looking to be raped again" because of the way she was dressed at an event.

Grace Tame, 31, was groomed and abused by a teacher and has been honoured as Australian of the Year in 2021 for her advocacy work on child safety.

In an op-ed for independent media outlet Crikey, Tame said she overheard Matt Handbury speaking to a friend, and was left distressed by the remark and the failure of others who heard it to intervene.

Handbury, nephew of Rupert Murdoch and a former executive at his firms, has not responded to a BBC request for comment, but in an email to Tame said he regretted the "highly objectionable" comment as soon as he said it.

Matt Handbury is the nephew of Rupert Murdoch and a former executive at his firms

Tame wrote in the Crikey article that she confronted Handbury - who she hadn't met before - immediately, and again later in the evening to check she had heard him correctly.

She said the encounter at the University of New South Wales event last week triggered flashbacks to her abuse and left her feeling like she would vomit.

"My whole body was prickling in distress," she wrote for Crikey.

"The many men and women who'd seen the confrontation were incredulous, and quick to express their anger at Matt's behaviour... Ironically though, in a room full of seasoned activists, journalists, lawyers and actual whistleblowers, not one person besides me took Matt to task in the heat of the moment."

The next day, in an email seen by the BBC, Handbury, 77, apologised for the "attempt at humour in less than poor taste".

"I did make reference to your strikingly attractive get-up... I also said: 'She must be looking to be raped again.'

He thanked her for "calling me out" and "opening my eyes more fully to this flaw in my nature which I will certainly be discussing with my very non-misogynist shrink".

"But no-one can deny you looked absolutely fabulous last night," he said, before signing off.

Handbury ran Murdoch Magazines for 13 years after buying the business from his uncle in 1991. He sold it to Seven Network's subsidiary Pacific Publications in 2004.

Tame said Handbury's remarks were "especially egregious" because it referenced a "very real, criminally tested traumatic experience, which he knew about" rather than an "imagined harm".

"It swiftly reawakened deep-seated physical wounds and haunting memories. How is that funny?"

From the age of 15, Tame was groomed and raped by her 58-year-old maths teacher Nicolaas Ockert Bester. He was later found guilty and served one year and nine months in jail for sexually abusing Tame when she was his student in 2011.

"They're not always as extreme, public or evidenced, but these kinds of experiences are part of my everyday life," Tame wrote on Instagram of Handbury's remarks.

"I'm tired of the maltreatment; and the gaslighting that follows. Tired of being provoked, then asked why I'm angry.

"I'm tired of copping more flak for fighting injustice than those who mete it out."

If you have been affected by any of the issues raised in this story, information and support can be found at the BBC's Action Line.

#47. Spanish rider Marc Marquez announces MotoGP retirement, days after clinching record 8th world title

Marc Marquez took fans and his team by surprise when announcing this weekend's race in Valencia will be his last. The 33-year-old won a record eight MotoGP titles, including his rookie and retirement seasons.

Marc Marquez, one of the most successful motorcycle racers of all time, announced on Friday that he would retire from MotoGP world championship after this weekend's season finale in Valencia.

The 33-year-old Spaniard's decision comes just a few days after he clinched his eighth top-tier motorcycle racing championship, equaling the record of Italian great Giacomo Agostini set in 1975 and surpassing the seven career titles of Marquez's own childhood hero and later rival Valentino Rossi.

"Thank you to everyone who's been part of the journey. I hope you've enjoyed it as much as I have. This will be my last race in MotoGP. We made history," Marquez said in a video recorded in the Valencia garage and shown during Friday's free practice.

He did not issue any firm comments about what he planned next, saying: "I first need to understand what I need in my next life."

The move came as a surprise, not least given that earlier this year Marquez had signed a contract extension with his Ducati team that was set to run until 2028.

The 33-year-old, who has battled with repeated injuries and surgeries in the latter half of his career, said he made the decision after last month's Japanese Grand Prix in light of the physical and mental toll of his battles for fitness.

"Sometimes you need to understand the situation, but it's true that the situation this year was on the limit," he said. "When I signed the contract in March, as always, I was super optimistic and I didn't know that I would have another surgery on my right shoulder [in May]."

Marquez said that at times this season, chasing a massive deficit after his early-season injury to come back and win the championship, he had begun to "feel the fear" in the saddle, and even noticed brief recurrences of the double vision he struggled with a few years ago.

"It was one of the main reasons why I controlled the risk and why I crashed less this second part of the season," he said.

Marquez burst onto the scene in MotoGP in 2013, highly touted after winning two world titles in junior series before graduating to compete in the top tier.

Aged 20, he won the championship in his rookie season, and went on to win the title in six of his first seven seasons with Honda, establishing himself as motorcycle racing's dominant force by 2019.

But Marquez suffered a serious crash at the 2020 season-opener in Jerez, broke his arm and endured an extremely rocky road to recovery.

He underwent four operations, was forced to abandon several comeback attempts and was battling with blurred vision, all while his Honda bike was no longer the class of the field, forcing him to push it too hard to try to stay at the front.

Instead of retiring during this bleak spell, something Marquez said he considered, he switched to the smaller Gresini team where he slowly rediscovered his belief and his race-winning pace by ending a drought of more than 3 calendar years with a win in Aragon in 2024.

He then secured a factory weat with Ducati for the 2025 season, and went on to claim a seventh MotoGP title — matching his childhood idol Valentino Rossi's modern-era record.

This season's campaign, culminating in an eighth championship, was arguably unlikelier still. After his early season crash in France, leading to surgery on his shoulder and a foot injury, Marquez returned trailing his championship rivals by 102 points.

But he won six of the subsequent nine races, sealing the championship last weekend in Portimao in Portugal. When he went up to collect his trophy, organizers superimposed a silhouette of a goat (as in "Greatest Of All Time") behind him as he climbed onto the podium.

Marquez himself has however shied away from that title, saying instead that he hoped to be in the discussion as "one of the greatest" in the sport's long history.

#42. Canada's top court largely upholds Quebec ban on religious symbols for public servants

Canada's top court has largely upheld a Quebec law banning public sector workers from wearing religious symbols, including the hijab and the cross.

The law, known as Bill 21, faced legal challenges from a range of groups, including female Muslim, Sikh and Catholic teachers, who argued it violated their fundamental rights.

Quebec passed the law in 2019 under a unique fixture of Canada's constitution called the "notwithstanding clause" - a legal loophole that allows governments to override certain rights, like freedom of religion.

The Supreme Court's decision is one of the most closely watched in Canadian history, with an impact that will be felt both in Quebec and across the country.

Reacting to the ruling on CBC, Prime Minister Mark Carney said his government would study its implications, adding it was important for all legislatures "to respect the rights and liberties of all Canadians, including those in the minority."

The Supreme Court was asked to weigh in on a number of questions, including on the constitutionality of Bill 21, whether Quebec's use of the notwithstanding clause was appropriate, and whether there are limits to its use.

On Friday, the top court confirmed the validity of Bill 21, but ruled that the law infringes on democratic rights by banning Quebec's elected officials from wearing a face covering for religious reasons.

The court also ruled that a Canadian court has the power to declare whether a law is consistent with the Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms even if the notwithstanding clause is invoked, but that they would not do so in this case.

While the majority of the seven justices found that Quebec's law is largely valid, some dissented to parts of the decision.

Justices Andromache Karakatsanis, Michelle O'Bonsawin and Nicholas Kasirer wrote that the notwithstanding clause "cannot be used to authorize laws that are incompatible with the fundamental character of Canada as a free and democratic country".

Specifically, they said they believe the law infringes on the charter's guarantee to freedom of religion, freedom of expression and the right to equality.

What to know about the Supreme Court decision on Quebec's secularism bill

Civil liberty groups in Quebec have criticised Bill 21, saying it disproportionately affects Muslim women who wear the hijab, arguing that it has excluded them from key areas of public life in the province.

Stephen Brown, CEO of the National Council of Canadian Muslims, called the law "discriminatory" and criticised the court's decision.

"Entire categories of people are now barred from entire sectors of employment, not because of what they have done, but because of who they are," he said.

The chair of the English Montreal School Board, one of the main groups challenging the law, said the board was "obviously very disappointed" with the ruling.

"It wasn't the result we were looking for," Ortona told reporters. "But we do take solace from a lot of the declarations that were made by the court."

Quebec has argued that the law is necessary to uphold the province's state secularism - or laÃ¯citÃ© - as a core component of its identity.

Earlier this year, the province passed another bill expanding its secularism rules to subsidised daycares and post-secondary institutions. It also restricted public prayer with fines for non-compliance.

The leader of the Bloc QuÃ©bÃ©cois, a federal party that advocates for Quebec sovereignty, said Friday's ruling upheld the province's right to protect its values.

"We are now more than ever in a position to move forward with the preservation of the very elements that profoundly define the distinct nature of Quebec society," Yves-FranÃ§ois Blanchet told reporters in French.

#39. Kenyan doomsday cult leader convicted over further 123 deaths

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves

A court in Kenya has convicted the leader of a doomsday Christian cult of the manslaughter of 123 of his followers.

Paul Mackenzie was found guilty in Mombasa of ordering his followers, all adults, to fast to death in Shakahola forest near the coast between January 2021 and September 2023.

Earlier this month Mackenzie was jailed for 200 years for murdering 191 people, all but one of them children, in the same mass killing.

Mackenzie had pleaded not guilty to manslaughter and one of his lawyers said he would appeal against the conviction after sentencing, Reuters news agency reports.

Warning: This article contains details which some people might find distressing.

The court also convicted 29 others including Mackenzie's wife, Rhoda Mumbua Maweu, of their part in the manslaughter.

Sixty defendants were acquitted. The Director of Public Prosecutions indicated that an appeal would be launched against their acquittal.

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves in the Shakahola forest, near the Indian Ocean coast.

The court found Mackenzie had "systematically persuaded his followers to fast until they died", Kenya's Office of The Director of Public Prosecutions said in a statement on Friday's verdict, which it described as "the final conviction" in the cases against him.

"Children were to go first, then women and then men, with Mackenzie himself meant to die last," the office said.

The prosecution argued that Mackenzie and his accomplices had acted together as part of a suicide pact to fast to death in order to "meet Jesus".

Witnesses testified that he had preached that education, medicine and ornaments were sinful.

Those who opposed his teachings were beaten to death, according to the verdict in his trial for the murder of 191 people.

The defendants had claimed that the victims had fasted out of their own free will.

Earlier this week, Mackenzie was also convicted of:

assault causing actual bodily harm and five counts of cruelty to a child

facilitating the commission of a terrorist act

being in possession of an article connected with an offence under Kenya's Prevention of Terrorism Act, 2012.

Kenya cult leader who told parents to starve children guilty of 191 murders

Mackenzie established Good News International Ministries in 2003. Over the next 16 years, the church grew in popularity to draw thousands of followers in branches across Kenya.

A television station called Times TV would broadcast his sermons from his church in the resort town of Malindi.

His End Days sermons included conspiracy theories that described education as "evil", healthcare as "against the doctrine" and digital identity cards as "the mark of the devil".

After the main church shut down in 2019, Mackenzie moved to Shakahola forest, encouraging his devotees to follow him.

The community was described as mud houses scattered across the vast forest.

"We lived a quiet normal life, fasting for a few days then having a small meal," said survivor Peter Shukrani, who was rescued after falling ill.

Stephen Mwiti, who lost six children to the cult, welcomed the conviction of Mackenzie for murder last month

When the shallow graves were discovered, local men were hired for the horrifying task of exhuming the remains.

"Every grave we found had at least three bodies," James, one of the gravediggers, told the BBC. "Some of the remains were of children as young as a few weeks old."

"We once found 12 bodies of children in a line inside a grave. It was massive, the bodies were arranged like clothes. One on top of another."

Gravediggers described some of the bodies of children as having had their "hands and legs tied and bodies wrapped in bedsheets".

Post-mortem records showed many of the victims had died of starvation while the remains of others were mostly skeletal, making the establishment of their cause of death impossible.

Stephen Mwiti, 48, said he had lost all six of his children to the cult. His former wife was one of those convicted of murder alongside Mackenzie.

"I'm a happy man, that all persons have been convicted," he told the BBC outside court last month.

"All six of my children died in Shakahola forest. I only found and buried the remains of my son Samuel."

The deaths have shone a spotlight on the lack of oversight of rogue religious activities and how they could have carried on for so long.

The gravity of the case prompted President William Ruto to set up a commission of inquiry, although this was later declared unconstitutional.

#35. Germany news: Lawmakers approve debt-laden 2027 budget

German lawmakers voted on a federal budget which foresees €556 billion in spending. Elsewhere, the Left Party is hosting a summit on femicide and the BSW party conference starts in Dresden. DW has more.

Welcome to DW's coverage of what Germany is talking about on Friday, 27 November. Catch up on yesterday's news here.

The checkered or arctic skipper, carterocephalus palaemon, has been named the 2027 butterfly of the year by BUND, the German Federation for the Environment and Nature Conservation.

As so often, the animal has a more literal and descriptive moniker in German, where it's known as the gelbwürfelige Dickkopffalter, or "yellow-checked thick-headed butterfly" — an homage to two of its most distinguishing features, its coloration and its large head.

BUND said that with its choice for 2027 it wanted to "raise awareness about the loss of valuable water-based ecosystems." It said that the loss of transitional areas between woods and open land with long grasses were crucial to both the butterfly and the caterpillars and were being lost.

"Forest and path edges are often mowed short. With this, the butterflies' feeding and wintering locations are lost. The climate crisis is also weighing on the checkered skipper: for the caterpillars' development, carterocephalus palaemon needs damp living spaces or at least a damp microclimate."

The species is not classified as endangered in Germany at present, but its numbers are in decline.

One of the two Iraqi nationals arrested yesterday on suspicion of plotting an attack on Cologne synagogue had been placed on a list of people who should leave the country in 2024, according to North Rhine-Westphalia's Refugee Ministry.

The 34-year-old Ameer J. appears to have been placed on a list of people from Iraq who were nominally supposed to leave the country but who were granted a dispensation to stay known in German as a "Duldung," stemming from the word for tolerate.

"The first information available to us indicates that one of the two suspects was included on the so-called Unna List that our ministry compiled in the autumn of 2024," NRW's Refugee Minister Verena Schäfer told regional broadcaster WDR.

This followed similar reporting in German media, including from the Kölner Stadt-Anzeiger newspaper and Der Spiegel.

WDR cited authorities in the city of Cologne as saying the decision to tolerate the man staying was based on him having regular employment at the time, and because he was not in need of replacement passport documentation given his valid Iraqi passport.

The regional broadcaster also reported, citing security sources, that the alleged Islamist had been under observation for several months, partly after he was noticed by investigators looking into an attack on a Jewish site in Munich in April of 2026.

The two men arrested on Thrusday were accused of plotting attacks against the synagogue in Cologne and one other, unnamed, European city. They are thought to have been working for or in conjunction with a pro-Iranian terrorist group. Prosecutors said that, for reasons that were not yet clear, the planned attacks were not ultimately carried out.

French hard-left presidential hopeful Jean-Luc Melenchon visited Berlin on Friday, and said he sees "a need for a thorough overhaul of the Franco-German relationship."

Melenchon, who is hoping the radical economic platform he's championed for decades in France might finally resonate with voters in the vacuum left by Emmanuel Macron as he hits term limit in April's presidential elections, said he opposed the German government's spending and defense policies.

"I am hostile to the liberal [economic] policy of German governments that dominates the European Union," the 75-year-old said.

Melenchon advocates drastically increasing public spending in France, already the much more debt-laden of the EU's top two economies, and canceling French debt held by the European Central Bank in order to do so — a step that critics argue would risk damaging both France and Europe's economies more generally.

The 75-year-old said he also rejected Chancellor Friedrich Merz's plan to increase German defense spending.

"We have the duty to ask: to make war against whom," Melenchon said, warning that a stronger Germany could make nuclear-armed France and the rest of Europe a "target" for other countries.

"They need to tone it down, because this one-upmanship leads nowhere," he said.

Melenchon has enshrined leaving NATO and nationalizing defense companies in his party's election platform, prompting allegations of him cosying up to Russian President Vladimir Putin.

He rejected the notion that he was "Germanophobic," citing his political or musical affection for Karl Marx and Beethoven.

He touted his party's ties with Germany's Left Party (die Linke) and met with party chair Ines Schwerdtner in the capital on Friday while plugging the German translation of his book.

The Left, too, has criticized Germany's defense spending plans. It's increasingly vague on the question of NATO, no longer explicitly calling for an immediate exit as it once did, but still advocating "replacing" NATO with some other kind of collective European security architecture.

European military backing for Ukraine in its fight against the ongoing Russian invasion continues to enjoy widespread support in Germany, according to the results of a new survey published on Friday.

The representative "Politbarometer" poll published by public broadcaster ZDF found that 37% of Germans consider the current level of European support for Ukraine to be just right, while another 31% think it should be increased. Fewer than 30% were in favor of a reduction in military support.

The survey also found that almost two thirds of Germans (64%) think Russia poses a large (39%) or very large (25%) threat to Germany, while the rest consider the threat posed by Moscow to be not so big (23%) or non-existent (11%).

When broken down politically, the survey found that supporters of almost all parties consider the danger posed by Russia to be big or very big. Only far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) voters bucked the trend with 75% believing that Moscow poses either no threat at all or only a minor one.

The ZDF survey results differ slightly from those published by the Körber foundation on Tuesday, which suggested for the first time that German military backing for Ukraine no longer enjoyed majority support among the population.

The ZDF survey, however, asked respondents about "European" support for Ukraine rather than about Germany specifically.

A former German spy chief who is accused of acquiring confidential information supplied to him by a former bureau chief has lost his appeal against pre-trial detention.

August Hanning, the former head of Germany's federal intelligence service (Bundesnachrichtendienst or BND) from 1998-2005, was arrested in October on suspicion of purchased highly sensitive documents from his former colleague and using them as part of intelligence analyses in his role as a private consultant.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Hanning is suspected of having prepared one such analysis for Azerbaijan's domestic intelligence service, although it's unclear whether or not classified information ever actually reached Baku.

At the start of November, Hanning's lawyer appealed against what he called a "disproportionate" pre-trial detention, telling Der Spiegel news magazine that his client had not given away any state secrets nor had the intention of doing so. He also told the dpa news agency that Hanning, 80, did not pose a flight risk.

However, the Federal Court of Justice (Bundesgerichtshof or BGH) has rejected the appeal.

German Labor Minister Alexander Schweitzer (SPD) submitted his draft pension reform legislation on Friday in which he reportedly intends to maintain the option of retiring two years early without benefit deductions for those with 45 years of pension contributions – a policy often referred to as the "pension at 63."

At the same time, however, eligibility requirements are set to become more stringent and more closely tied to an individual's actual lifetime work contribution.

In practical terms, this means that individuals with a long working life will still be able to retire two years early without deductions.

From 2029 onwards, however, only periods of employment, child-rearing, and the care of relatives will count toward the 45-year contribution requirement.

Periods during which an insured person received unemployment benefits, sickness benefits or transitional allowances – or held only a low-wage, part-time "mini-job" – will no longer be credited.

For weeks, Germany's coalition government partners, the conservative Christian Democrats (CDU) and Schweitzer's center-left Social Democrats have been struggling to reach a compromise on this issue.

A pension commission had proposed abolishing this popular type of pension, a proposal opposed by the SPD.

A group of leading coalition politicians, including Schweitzer, reportedly held final discussions regarding the plans as recently as Wednesday evening.

Germany's ‌lower ⁠house ⁠of parliament, the Bundestag, ​voted in favor of ​a €556 ​billion ($610 ‌billion) 2027 ‌federal budget on ‌Friday, taking on additional debt to boost ​defense ​and infrastructure spending.

Total new borrowing will reach €210 ​billion, just short of the record €215.4 billion recorded in ​2021 ‌during the COVID-19 pandemic, according to budget figures.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz's government has vowed a push to strengthen Germany's core civilian infrastructure and rebuild ​its military after decades of underinvestment.

"It is right that we invest in the railways and ​in climate protection," said Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil in parliament on Friday, defending his budget. "It is right... that we invest in our security."

German core defense spending will increase from €82 billion to almost €110 billion. When additional spending from the special defense fund is also taken into account, total defense spending will pass €150 billion.

"We are putting up the ⁠money for ​defense so that Ukraine can defend its freedom," Klingbeil said. "No child born in Germany today should ever have to experience war again."

But there was criticism of the budget from the opposition Left Party, with party leader Ines Schwerdtner accusing the government only ever talking about emergency and war.

"But for millions of people in this country, the emergency comes every month when money gets tight," she said. "But you're not interested in these emergencies."

317 members of parliament voted in favor of the budget and 260 against it. There were no abstentions.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz will have a private audience with Pope Leo XIV at the Vatican on Monday before meeting Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni in Rome later, a government spokesman confirmed on Friday.

Merz, himself a Catholic, had a brief encounter with Pope Leo at the pontiff's inauguration in May 2025, but this will be the pair's first private meeting.

According to Politico, who first reported on the planned meeting earlier this week, officials expect the discussion to focus on "human dignity, artificial intelligence and efforts to bring peace to Ukraine."

Afterwards, Merz will be received with military honors by Italian Prime Minister Meloni, with whom he will hold talks on Russia's ongoing invasion of Ukraine, economic cooperation and, "with certainty," the European Union budget for the period 2028-2034, known as the multiannual financial framework (MFF).

Negotiations over the MFF in Brussels this week ended without agreement, with Germany leading a group of more fiscally conservative member states which also includes Austria, Denmark, Sweden, Finland, Belgium and the Netherlands.

Italy, on the other hand, along with countries like Poland and Spain, would like to see a more ambitious EU budget.

German arms manufacturer Rheinmetall has revised its medium-term revenue forecast for 2030 down from around €45 billion to between €33 billion and €37 billion, with an expected profit margin of around 20%.

The announcement, made on Friday at a market event in Hamburg, comes after the Düsseldorf-based company also lowered its forecast for the current year following delays in major orders  notably from the German government.

In July, the German defense ministry canceled a multi-billion-euro contract to produce six new type-F126 naval frigates and there are now reportedly delays in an expected large-scale order of Boxer armored fighting vehicles (AFV).

Rheinmetall chief executive Armin Papperger said the government was negotiating hard on price and that he would only sign off on the deal once "sufficient profitability" was guaranteed.

Meanwhile, Rheinmetall is set to deliver 66 additional Lynx AFVs to Romania, part of an order which now totals 298 such vehicles.

The deliveries to the European Union and NATO member state are set to begin in 2028 and be completed by 2030.

Rheinmetall is also manufacturing four battleships for the Romanian navy and has increased its production capacities in the eastern European country which borders Ukraine.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

German arms manufacturer Rheinmetall has confirmed that two of its employees had been killed in a drone attack in Kuwait.

"Two highly valued employees lost their lives while carrying out their professional duties, providing technical support for air-defense systems," the Düsseldorf-based company said in a statement.

"Protecting the people of that country was a matter of deep personal importance to them. Both the company's leadership and the entire workforce are devastated by the loss of our colleagues. We share in the grief of those they have left behind."

The two victims were Italian citizens who worked as technicians for subsidiary Rheinmetall Italia, Italian Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani confirmed in Rome.

Tajani said the pair had been at an air surveillance station in Kuwait when it was attacked by two Iranian drones. Two other people, a Pakistani national and Kuwaiti army captain, were also injured.

The Iranian foreign ministry has denied any involvement in the deadly attack.

Rheinmetall secured a contract to supply the Kuwaiti navy with defense systems in July.

Germany's left-wing populist BSW party is holding its annual conference on Friday and Saturday as it looks to remain relevant following a series of electoral defeats and internal splits.

The BSW was founded in 2024 by former Left Party politician Sahra Wagenknecht who leant her initials to the party's original name, the Sahra Wagenknecht Alliance or Bündnis Sahra Wagenknecht (BSW). The founder herself stepped down as party leader in 2025 and the party officially changed its name last month to "Alliance for Social Justice and Economic Reason" – also still abbreviated as BSW.

After initial successes in European and eastern German state elections in 2024, the BSW failed to reach the minimum 5% required to enter parliament in the 2025 federal election. In September, the BSW crept into the state parliament in Saxony-Anhalt with 5.3% of the vote but fell short in Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania and Berlin.

Ahead of its party conference in Dresden this weekend, the BSW has been split over its approach to the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) — with which it shares some economic and geopolitical policies, such as support for trading relations with Russia and opposition to military support for Ukraine.

Like most other German political parties, the BSW has ruled out entering into any coalition with the AfD but, in Saxony-Anhalt, has already voted with the far-right on certain policies and has held talks over support for certain issues which an AfD minority government would want to implement.

The BSW intends to abstain from the vote for the AfD's Ulrich Siegmund as state premier for Saxony-Anhalt on December 8 – but ultimately cannot force its five lawmakers to do so in what will be a secret ballot.

The softening of the BSW's position on the AfD has the backing of founder Wagenknecht and the party's lead candidate in Saxony-Anhalt, Claudia Wittig, who has slammed the policy of refusing to work with the AfD as "firewall idiocy."

"We're not cozying up to the AfD," she insisted, but said: "Let's not get tied up in knots over lies in the mainstream media."

Wagenknecht added: "Vibrant parties should have vibrant discussions about their future course, but we shouldn't accuse ourselves of things which aren't true. The BSW is not team AfD. Rather, we take voters seriously and want to implement political change to improve the lives of the majority."

But other founder members are resisting, writing in the Zeit newspaper this week that a "clear political distancing from the AfD" was a "foundational consensus" for the BSW.

"Just as it was right to reject the firewall, which only served the AfD anyway, so it is also wrong to make common cause with the AfD," they wrote, warning that the approach could morph into "political cooperation with AfD" which would "compromise the BSW and make it irrelevant in the long term."

They demanded: "No tolerance of an AfD government, no cooperation agreements, no abstentions from the vote for the state premier."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Germany's socialist Left Party (Die Linke) is on Friday hosting an international summit on femicide and violence against women.

Under the title "Voices Against Femicide," the summit in Berlin brings together activists, politicians and researchers from around the world, including Kenya, Nigeria, Kurdistan, India, Afghanistan, Syria, Mexico, Argentina, Spain and Germany.

Across three panel discussions, the speakers will highlight women's rights, feminist struggles and legal perspectives and debate political measures which are urgently needed in Germany to effectively combat femicides and patriarchal violence.

The summit comes just two days after Germany's parliament, the Bundestag, marked Orange Day, the United Nations' officially named day for the elimination of violence against women, with a minute's silence and a series of speeches.

Germany's Federal Criminal Police Office reported a total of 1,176 female victims of attempted and completed homicide in 2025, up from 859 in 2024.

The number of female victims of domestic violence in Germany in 2025 rose by 2.8% to 197,667.

German lawmakers will on Friday vote on a federal budget for 2027 which foresees €556 billion ($610 billion) in government spending, around €31 billion more than in 2026.

The largest chunk of expenditure (just over €200 billion) will go on federal contributions to the statutory pension system, while €110 billion is to be allocated to defense – a 32.7% increase.

The plans call for around €210 billion in fresh borrowing, a figure which is only expected to increase between now and 2030, while €2.7 billion are also expected to be shifted away from Germany's climate protection and transformation fund.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz's conservative Christian Democrats (CDU) and their Social Democrat (SPD) coalition partners have spent an unusually long time wrangling over the budget, with Merz finally clinching a deal last week after more than eight hours of talks at the Chancellery.

#34. Capsized tanker carrying 7,000 tonnes of diesel to be towed away from Ibiza

An official inspects the partially submerged tanker - the rescue operation is expected to be challenging

A tanker carrying up to 7,000 tonnes of diesel which capsized off the holiday island of Ibiza on Thursday will be towed to the port of Cartagena, officials in Spain say.

All 17 of the Malta-registered Med Emre's crew members were rescued but the chief engineer later died in hospital. Six people were treated for minor injuries.

There had been concerns of a major oil spillage in the western Mediterranean but an inspection showed that the ship's fuel tanks are intact. An investigation is under way into what happened.

The vessel had been travelling from Algeciras near Gibraltar to Barcelona and is expected to arrive in Cartagena on Monday in what is being seen as a challenging rescue operation.

In a statement on Friday, the Spanish transport ministry said the tanker would be towed to Cartagena "as it is the only one [port] that meets the technical requirements to carry out cargo extraction and repair work".

Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez told Cadena SER radio network he hoped the tanker could be taken to port "in the coming hours, in the coming days".

"The information suggests that, indeed... this leak is not happening", he added.

The Spanish authorities provided no details about how the diesel would be extracted.

Meanwhile, pictures have emerged showing emergency workers inspecting the tanker's hull.

Any possible environmental damage is being assessed by air and pollution control vessel Clara Campoamor, which is owned by Spain's national maritime rescue service.

Elena Delgado, maritime captain of the port of Valencia, earlier told news agency AFP that a small slick which had formed around the vessel had "disappeared".

"The situation is stabilised," she said, adding that further inspections would be carried out.

Regional emergencies director Pablo GÃ¡rriz said the tanker had not completely sunk as originally reported. The vessel remains partially submerged.

The Med Emre capsized 25 nautical miles (46 km; 28 miles) from Ibiza's coastline and 57 nautical miles from Valencia.

Rescuers in Valencia received a distress call late on Wednesday, according to Spanish media.

Minutes later, the vessel reported a leak and the crew said it was leaning at a 90-degree angle. It capsized in the early hours on Thursday.

Ibiza's marine environment was added to the Unesco World Heritage List in 1999. In particular, it is home to dense prairies of Posidonia oceanic seagrass, which is found only in the Mediterranean basin and plays a vital role in supporting local marine biodiversity.

In 2002, Spain experienced one of its worst environmental disasters when the Prestige oil tanker ran into trouble off Galicia's coastline, in the country's north-west.

More than 60,000 tonnes of heavy fuel oil spilled into the sea.

#29. Brazil pushes back on Macron calling its meat 'doped'

The French president said he was proud to have led efforts to ban Brazilian meat products

Brazil says it "regrets" a statement by French President Emmanuel Macron who called Brazilian meat "doped".

France's leader made the comment while meeting representatives of farming unions on Wednesday, during which he celebrated EU restrictions on imports of Brazilian meat and poultry which took effect in September.

In its statement, the Brazilian government said Macron's statements "spread misinformation and contradict the widely available technical and scientific evidence on animal protein production in Brazil".

Macron's comments come as part of a trade row between the EU and Mercosur - the South American trade bloc. The EU banned Brazilian meat, poultry and other animal products owing to the use of antimicrobials in farming.

In its defence, Brazil said it had supplied animal protein to the EU for decades and that it exported meat to 177 markets. It added the country had a rigorous sanitary control system, corroborated by a recent European audit, and observed stringent environmental requirements.

Macron, in his opening remarks in the meeting with the agricultural sector representatives at ÃlysÃ©e Palace, said he was "proud of the fight we led" against Mercosur.

"And the 'doped' meat, if I may put it that way, or rather meat from Mercosur countries, especially Brazil, that was arriving in our ports and therefore entering our markets without complying with our rules, was ultimately banned," he said.

Brazil said it was concerned about the "clearly political nature" of recent EU restrictions, and urged France to reconsider its position "in light of scientific evidence".

Affirming its partnership with France was important, the country emphasised that "in the face of protectionist pressures, the best interests of the bilateral relationship must prevail, always guided by mutual respect and reciprocity".

Brazil is a massive exporter of meat, especially beef - some $1.84bn (Â£1.4bn) of which went to EU markets alone in 2025, according to Brazil's agricultural ministry.
