#26. 'We couldn't give up that easily': Marching bands scramble to cover costs after Thanksgiving parade rearranged

How New York reacted to postponement of Macy's parade

Since New York City officials postponed the Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade, a precaution ahead of a storm that brought heavy rain, wind and flooding, hundreds of touring performers who traveled from across the US to perform were thrust into chaos.

"It was jarring," said Ryan Demkovich, director of a Texas high school marching band. After learning the news, he had to scramble to make new arrangements for over 500 people.

Demkovich and his Pearland High School band made the quick decision to launch a fundraiser, reaching $350,000 (Â£267,000) in just a few hours.

Still, some performers reportedly had to skip the parade, a televised, traditional Thanksgiving event - now rescheduled for Saturday.

On Wednesday afternoon, a day before the event, organisers announced the postponement, in part due to concerns around the parade's giant balloons in strong winds.

The balloons, inflated on Wednesday, were then temporarily deflated - along with the spirits of those who journeyed to the city to watch the action.

The move represented the first time the event had been postponed in its 100-year history, although it was suspended altogether during World War II.

Each year, a number of marching bands travel from across the country, from high schools and colleges, to perform at the event. This is considered a huge opportunity, as the parade is watched by millions in person and on TV.

Macy's postpones Thanksgiving parade due to severe weather

Hundreds of thousands still without power after storms hit US on Thanksgiving

Demkovich described an emotional and exhausting experience, first learning that the parade would be postponed while watching a Broadway performance of Wicked with his band.

"My initial thought was like, 'Oh, crap. We're not going to be able to go'," he said.

"Where are we going to be able to get all the money for this?"

The delay meant unexpected expenses of additional nights in a hotel - and meals - for hundreds of people in the middle of New York City during the holidays.

There were no vacancies to extend their stay at the New York Marriott Marquis in Times Square, so they booked the nearest hotel with availability - across the Hudson River - in New Jersey.

Demkovich said the Pride of Pearland Marching Band posted a fundraiser online and within hours met its fundraising goal.

"The word just spread like wildfire, truly like wildfire," Demkovich said, adding that donations came from over 2,000 people and local businesses.

He called the outpouring of support "very, very overwhelming to think about and comprehend".

"We have a lot to be thankful for this Thanksgiving."

Some groups were not able to maneuver as easily. The University of Alabama's Million Dollar Band is one of those missing out, due to a schedule conflict on Saturday.

ââThis canât be happeningâ,â Rina Browder, mother of a snare drummer in the band, told local news, describing the bandâs reactions.

"They were devastated. They've worked so hard for this. So many practices."

The Pride of Pearland Marching Band from Texas - pictured rehearsing earlier this week - said it raised hundreds of thousands of dollars

Meanwhile, a group from Hawaii, the NÄ Koa AliÊ»i HawaiÊ»i All-State Marching Band, said it too would still be able to participate in the parade, also because of emergency fundraising by families, friends and other supporters donating more than $500,000.

Speaking to the BBC on Thursday, the group's director Rickey H Badua described the time pressures. "We are racing to get this done," he explained.

The band is made up of hundreds of musicians from dozens of schools across the state. It travelled almost 5,000 miles (8,000km) to get to New York.

"Getting the news that we got the parade postponed was not easy, and emotional for our students," Badua said.

But the musicians sprang into action quickly, he said. "We come from a very far place," Badua said, adding that "to get so close to the finish line and not show up for it, we couldn't give up that easily".

The response from friends and family had been "incredible", he said.

New York City mayor on postponing 100th Macyâs Thanksgiving Day Parade

Badua was hopeful his group would have the same experience he had when he first performed at the parade in 2003, as student drum major.

"The energy is palpable, people are just excited to see a band that is great."

Another musical act, from Illinois, told the BBC on Thursday it needed to raise $175,000 to cover another night in a hotel, additional food, and bus costs back to the city of Champaign.

"Things are going well due to the very generous support of so many amazing human beings," said Professor Barry Houser, director of the University of Illinois Marching Illini.

By Friday, 3,500 donors had stepped up to help the 410 students and their travel party. "This is truly phenomenal," said Houser.

While awaiting the parade, musicians and punters alike have sheltered from the heavy wind and rain brought by the storm to eastern areas of the US.

Hundreds of thousands of homes were without power in the storm's aftermath Friday, and coastal areas had flooded. The National Weather Service (NWS) expects conditions to improve gradually on Friday.

#24. Weary of war but with no sign of backing down, Kyiv faces another punishing winter

Weary of war but with no sign of backing down, Kyiv faces another punishing winter

Something weird happens when you reach Kyiv after the eight-hour drive from the Polish border: the clock on your mobile phone goes to midnight. It's not really for phones - it seems to be part of a wider strategy to disorient the missiles and drones the Russians are sending over.

Aside from little things like that, though, Kyiv seems fairly normal at first sight. You become well aware there's a war on when sirens begin to wail and people check their phones for the notifications which tell them there's a red alert and missiles are on their way. You can even buy gadgets that show you which direction the drones are coming from, and what type they are.

The drones and ballistic missiles have been hitting in sizeable numbers over the last couple of weeks. On the morning of Monday 16 November, the battle of the bridges began. Ukraine hit the Kerch bridge which links Russia to illegally occupied Crimea. Then it launched a bigger wave of missiles and drones, damaging the central road-bridge arch. A few hours later, presumably in revenge, Russia fired seven ballistic missiles at Kyiv, disabling two of the nine bridges that cross the River Dnipro.

The war has dragged on for more than four and a half years, killing what some estimate could be 100,000 Ukrainian troops and 18,000 Ukrainian civilians, according to the United Nations, and probably more. Its infrastructure, too, has been battered, as well as its morale. While temperatures drop to freezing at night, and with Ukrainians regularly suffering from no light, heat or water thanks to Russian strikes, can the country survive another difficult winter under bombardment? And what would a peace deal, if one could be struck, look like?

Kyiv is mostly dark at night, thanks to Russia's campaign of targeting Ukraine's electricity generating capability. In the blackness last week, the air raid sirens started wailing across the city, and our hotel's warning announcements came over the loudspeakers. Within a few minutes, we heard the first explosions. I jumped out of bed and stared out of the window into the darkness. After a few seconds two more missiles landed, lighting up the entire sky momentarily in a blaze of white and red. They were uncomfortably close.

Many people prefer to go to the shelters when there's a red alert. Others just stay in bed and hope the law of averages will protect them. It usually does. During one of the heaviest attacks last week, the number of fatalities was in single figures.

I went to the shelter in our hotel one night. The clearly audible thumps and crashes above ground, the permanent lighting, the snores and grunts of other people, and the attentions of the little biting flies that seem to thrive in the warm, crowded shelters made sleep hard.

Kyiv is entering its fourth winter following Russia's full-scale invasion

To the people who live here in Kyiv, it's frightening, certainly, if a drone or a missile hits close by, but enraging too. Over the years I've been in several cities that were being bombed by high-tech weaponry and the reaction always seems the same, whether it's Lebanon, Iraq, Serbia, or Ukraine: a combination of weariness and fury. In Kyiv the attacks come by night and day, making normal life almost impossible. An uninterrupted night's sleep is a rarity. The morning after the bridges were hit, people in the streets looked tired and worn.

Yet although they long for it to stop, there's no sign they want Ukraine to surrender. On the contrary, just about everyone I spoke to felt they had to keep fighting. That's borne out by an opinion poll last month by the KIIS organisation, a Ukrainian market research company, which found that 62% of people were prepared to put up with the war for as long as it took. Of those polled, 79% believed that Ukraine would win the war in the long run, and only 7% thought Russia would win. These aren't the reactions of a country which is being beaten to its knees.

I don't have any doubt that Ukraine can survive the winter. Food is plentiful, though prices are high. Water is a problem, because the regular attacks on the power grid mean that it can't be pumped with any regularity. Kyiv underwent a large transformation under the Soviets, so there are large numbers of high-rise blocks of flats everywhere. Labouring up and down the stairs when there's no power is daunting, especially when you've got to carry quantities of water home.

We came across a small crowd of people in their 20s who had gathered outside a nightclub for a rave at three in the afternoon, because of the 11pm curfew. "It's a way of getting a bit of respite from everything," said one young woman. "It makes me feel I can put up with everything," said a man.

Do people still support President Volodymyr Zelensky? My team and I met an elderly woman wandering around Independence Square in the centre of Kyiv holding a photo of her son, who was missing on the Eastern front. She blamed Zelensky; if he didn't insist on carrying on the war, she believed, her son would still be alive. It isn't a response most people seem to share. The same polling organisation, KIIS, found that Zelensky's trust ratings hover around 54% to 57% â way below the 90% he was registering in the early days of the war in 2022, but not too bad for a man who's been in power now for more than seven years.

Zelensky's ratings have dipped considerably

What comes across all the time in Kyiv is an absolute loathing of Russia. Putin reportedly launched his invasion of Ukraine in February 2022 because his intelligence chiefs assured him that Ukrainians longed to rejoin the Motherland.

Russian missiles have hit hospitals, clinics and schools. In many well-attested cases the Russians have used the "double-tap" method â sending in one missile, then waiting till the medics and rescuers gather before sending in another. At least 20,000 Ukrainian children have been abducted and taken to Russia, according to Ukraine's Ministry of Justice. In late March 2022, when the Ukrainians recaptured the satellite town of Bucha, on the outskirts of Kyiv, they found shocking evidence of torture and murder. Ukrainian soldiers call their Russian enemies Orcs, after the sub-human brutes in The Lord of the Rings. A country that has endured this kind of thing doesn't quickly forgive and forget. Russia denies that it targets civilians or civilian infrastructure.

Ukraine has never lost its own identity, even during the Soviet period. Some of Ukraine's culture was absorbed by Russia â "They call borsch [a famously delicious soup made of beetroot] theirs, but they got it from us," a waiter in a cafÃ© here told me proudly. After the Soviet Union collapsed in 1991, Ukraine overwhelmingly voted for independence. The war has brought a fierce renewal of this desire to be completely separated from Russia.

It's not my usual beat, but my team and I went to film on the set of MasterChef Ukraine, currently one of the country's favourite television programmes. The studio was borrowed; the original one had been bombed. The executive producer had his phone in his hand the whole time, and was constantly glancing at it. The moment a red alert showed, it was his duty to shepherd everyone off the set to a nearby shelter. "People see our cuisine as a way of expressing their national identity," he said. It's one of the reasons the programme is so popular.

There are other ways in which people express their Ukrainian national identity under the bombs. There has been a boom for books being written and published about Ukraine and Ukrainian identity. Ukrainian and Russian are close as languages, though vocabulary and pronunciation differ. Nowadays the differences are emphasised. "Woe betide a writer who uses Russian spellings," a Ukrainian novelist told me.

The war will end at some point; wars always do. But there has to be a clear winner before anyone backs down, and a lasting peace can be negotiated. US President Donald Trump and his advisers, people like Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, with their business backgrounds, have approached the situation as though it's a form of mergers and acquisitions: make a deal attractive enough, and the other side will accept it.

So, last week, Washington was rumoured to be offering a very Trumpian deal, that Ukraine should accept the loss of the land in the east which the Russians now control, and in return it was rumoured it could join Nato. It was a non-starter, because Russia would never accept it. The sweetener, in this US plan, was that the West's sanctions against Russia would be lifted, and both sides would benefit from unrestricted trading once again.

But neither side sees this as being about trade and profit. For Russia, it's about the dangers of being humiliated if it doesn't win. President Vladimir Putin has made it his personal war. By illegally taking Crimea from Ukraine in 2014, he made himself very popular in Russia. If he doesn't win this second stage of a 12-year campaign against Ukraine, the consequences for him personally could be disastrous. He's already had to face down one attempt against his rule: his former associate Yevgeny Prigozhin's march on Moscow in June 2023. In a much-quoted remark, reported last August, Putin is said to have confided to his associates that if he didn't win the war "they'll hang me". My sense is he's not concerned about trade with the West and the profit that will bring, so much as about his personal survival.

Similarly, Ukrainians aren't interested in the precise details that have leaked out of the various negotiations between the Americans and Russians. Their contempt for the US is immense; "they just failed us," said one Ukrainian I spoke to.

And they don't believe Russia will stick to any future peace agreement anyway. "They signed a treaty to accept our borders, and Putin broke it," a young man told me as he sat hunched over his laptop in a bookshop. "The only solution is to defeat them."

Tens of thousands of soldiers have died since the outbreak of war

Other people talked about the need to get huge reparations from Russia for the terrible destruction it has visited on their country. According to one estimate, reconstruction and recovery will cost more than $500bn (Â£383bn), an unthinkably huge amount for a country whose GDP was $200bn before the war. President Trump and his advisers don't seem to have addressed any of these issues in their negotiations.

Here in Kyiv, peace doesn't seem at all close. Russia's efforts to make life intolerable for Ukraine's citizens are certainly having a major effect. The war is often frightening for ordinary people, and it can be appallingly destructive of property and lives. But short of hugely escalating by attacking Ukraine with nuclear weapons, which is something China and the US are both thought to have warned him not to do, Putin can't seem to strike the knock-out blow against Ukraine that he needs.

At present it seems to people in Ukraine that the war will never come to an end. Yet it will, at some point: and then the crucial requirement will be to work out ways to stop Russia coming back for another round. One way would be if Ukraine joins Nato, but that's something Russia would only accept if it had been comprehensively defeated. Ukrainians might think that can happen, but most Western onlookers are more sceptical.

The daily and nightly sirens, the explosions of missiles and drones, the shortages and the fear and the darkness are all terrible. But they aren't enough to destroy Ukraine's will to continue fighting.

#21. Canada: Supreme Court largely upholds Quebec secularism bill

Quebec's government passed Bill 21 to ban religious symbols for public servants, arguing it was necessary to protect its secularist society. Critics argue the bill weakens legal protections for fundamental rights.

Canada's Supreme Court on Friday upheld Quebec's ban on religious symbols for public servants.

However, it ruled that provincial lawmakers should be allowed to wear religious symbols in the legislature, Quebec's National Assembly.

Quebec's government passed the bill, known as Bill 21, in 2019, arguing it was necessary to protect its secular society.

Under the law, anyone receiving public services like teachers and police officers has the right to do so without being exposed to overt religious symbols, including hijabs, turbans and yarmulkes.

Chief Justice Richard Wagner said that the law "is rooted in Quebec's legitimate and historical commitment to ensuring the (secularism) of the state in a free and democratic society."

Mathieu Levesque, the interim the right-wing Quebecois nationalist Future Quebec Coalition (CAQ) that passed the bill, hailed it as a "victory" for the French-speaking province.

"This is a victory for the Quebec nation and for [CAQ], whose actions have today been fully validated," he said.

CAQ ruled Quebec from in two parliamentary terms from 2018 to 2026, after which it failed to enter parliament and the pro-independence center-left Parti Quebecois (PQ), which also supported Bill 21, formed government.

The case was seen as a major test of how far provinces can go in defying provisions of the federal constitution by invoking the "notwithstanding clause," which effectively allows provincial governments to pass laws that may breach Canada's constitution.

Quebec invoked the clause when passing the secularism bill, known as Bill 21, expecting it to be challenged over constitutional provisions guaranteeing freedom of religion.

Critics have argued that the bill unfairly targets Muslim women and weakens legal protections for fundamental rights.

"This decision is an unrestricted license for every government in this country to pass legislation taking away the fundamental freedoms and the legal rights of every Canadian ," Stephen Brown, CEO of the National Council of Canadian Muslims, was cited by Reuters news agency as saying.

He said the law forced many Muslim women to stop working as teachers or even prevented them from volunteering in schools.

Emmett Macfarlane, head of political science at the University of Waterloo, was cited by Reuters as saying that the law amounted to "Islamophobia as a state policy."

#20. Zelensky criticises 'strange' Trump-Putin friendship in BBC interview

Watch: Zelensky questions "strange" Trump-Putin friendship in BBC interview

Volodymyr Zelensky has voiced deep frustration with the US, criticising Donald Trump for calling Vladimir Putin "my friend" and saying delays in delivering vital anti-ballistic missiles were costing Ukrainian lives.

In an interview with the BBC at his central Kyiv office, Ukraine's president complained the US was not pushing Russia hard enough in peace talks, and called it "strange" that Trump posted a photo of himself embracing Putin.

"Of course it's up to the president of the United States, but... it's strange," Zelensky said, speaking in English.

"He's an aggressor, and a killer... He can't be a friend. Just cannot be," he said, adding it was like saying you were friends with Adolf Hitler during World War Two.

Since a bruising encounter with Trump in the Oval Office in 2025, Zelensky has been careful how he handles relations with the US.

Trump's two envoys, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, have been trying to negotiate a peace deal between Russia and Ukraine - to end the huge loss of life. But they often seem more in tune with Moscow's position, than that of Kyiv.

Kushner, Trump's son-in-law, said on Wednesday that Putin had shown "restraint" by not using nuclear weapons in Ukraine. He also described Putin as a "grand master of politics".

The latest round of peace talks in Abu Dhabi earlier this month made no obvious progress: "The more we know, the more problems", is how Zelensky described it.

He said talks were expected to resume in December.

On Thursday, Trump posted on his Truth Social platform that at his own summit with Putin in China last week he had urged Russia to "pull back all military aggression against territories throughout Europe" and said he believed a peace deal with Ukraine was "a strong possibility".

But from its public statements, Russia appears to have regressed to its initial, hardline negotiating positions â on everything.

And Zelensky revealed that it was not clear whether European negotiators would be at the table, as the US envoys had announced.

Europe ready for talks with Russia on ending war in Ukraine, leaders say

Zelensky says Putin is a 'killer' after Trump calls Russian leader a friend

Ukraine urgently needs the talks to move forward and bring five exhausting years of Russia's full-scale invasion to an end.

Russian strikes across the country have been growing more frequent and more deadly: the number of civilians being killed is at an all-time high.

So Zelensky called for "real helpful allies, who can push Russia" to achieve results and urged the US to live up to its reputation as a strong country with "power and respect".

"When you try to be very neutral with the aggressor... it means you support victims and you support also aggressor. It's not right," he said.

The US argues that it is neutral: in the complex search for a peace deal between the two sides that can hold.

Questioned again on Thursday about calling Putin a friend, Trump told reporters: "We want to stop killing. I could say he's not a friend... I want to make a deal."

He added: "I could walk in and say, I don't like you, and then we have the same problem that we have with Zelensky and Putin.

"You know... the biggest problem to that settlement is the fact that you have two leaders that really hate each other."

In the meantime, Kyiv is struggling to defend its skies.

The air alerts, day and night, are now a draining feature of everyday life. As Russian ballistic missiles target power stations and bridges, jet-powered drones are launched daily at central Kyiv and other big cities.

Ukraine launches hundreds of drones at targets in Russia in response.

Zelensky said the military here are now able to destroy about 70% of Russia's fastest drones, a statistic he said was improving.

But there is a "very, very big" deficit of interceptors to stop ballistic missiles and he said the Americans were delaying deliveries of its Patriot systems.

"It's a pity they made a focus on Middle East and they help them moreâ¦ It's a pity. We have huge questions with this," Zelensky said.

"The deficit of these missiles - that is people's lives."

He also confirmed that the US satellite communications system, Starlink â and its military version, Starshield â were still being restricted in occupied areas of Ukraine.

Ukrainian drone units have described their problems to the BBC, as their interceptor drones can no longer catch up with Russian Shaheds. It means those weapons then fly deeper into Ukraine, unhindered.

"Technically SpaceX, politically - I don't know, I really don't know," Zelensky replied. "It's like... ping pong. We are asking one side, they say it's the other."

Ukraine's own long-range strikes against Russian targets have increased in recent weeks, including oil refineries and ships carrying weapons or fuel.

On Thursday, Italy's Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni said an attack that sank a Russian warship docked in eastern Libya this week risked spreading war to the Mediterranean, which was unacceptable.

Zelensky defended the move, calling the warship Merkury an "absolutely fair goal".

He accepted that such strikes were "very dangerous" but said Ukraine's forces "never attack" without co-ordinating with its partners.

But he acknowledged that Ukraine was bracing for a difficult and dangerous winter.

Recent attacks on Kyiv have targeted bridges over the vast river Dnipro, apparently trying to cut the city in two.

Some residents have already left town for the winter and businesses closed, as power cuts and strikes on logistics networks have pushed operating costs unmanageably high.

They have also plunged much of the city into darkness.

"[Russia] wants us to run away, of course," Zelensky said.

"People can go, but we will try to defend our capital in any way. We need our capital. Without a capital, you don't have a country."

#14. Spanish PM and rivals rally supporters in final pitch for votes

Pedro SÃ¡nchez accused his opponents of hurling insults and lies but his conservative rival has promised a new era of politics

Spain's main party leaders have held their final rallies ahead of Sunday's election, with Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez saying he is confident of defying the polls.

"I cannot imagine us losing," he said, posing for selfies with supporters in red T-shirts and accusing conservative rival Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o of planning a coalition with the far right.

FeijÃ³o's People's Party (PP) is narrowly ahead in the polls, and in the final hours of the campaign he travelled first to Barcelona and then Seville, promising voters a new political era of "truth, decency and good governance".

He accused SÃ¡nchez of evading questions on key issues like corruption, rail disasters, immigration and the national debt.

The Socialist leader heads one of Europe's few left-wing governments, and with the French presidential elections five months away, there is a real chance that two of the continent's biggest economies could move to the right in quick succession.

Almost 39 million people have a vote in Spain and while commentators expect a high turnout, most polls have suggested a close finish. Under Spanish electoral law, no polls have been published since Monday.

"This time, yes, we're going to do it," FeijÃ³o told PP supporters, in a nod to his failure to form a government three years ago despite winning most seats.

Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o's PP holds a narrow lead in the opinion polls

If he gets to form a government this time, it will be with the support of anti-immigration party Vox, led by Santiago Abascal, whose final rally took place in a bull-ring in Valencia on Spain's east coast.

Pedro SÃ¡nchez's government "has betrayed us, he has robbed us and he has lied to us", Abascal told an audience earlier in TarancÃ³n, to the south-east of Madrid.

Vox is hoping to almost double its seats from the 33 it won three years ago. It backs policies such as "national priority" - putting Spanish citizens first for welfare - and "remigration" for foreigners not considered to be contributing to the economy.

Leaders clash in final Spain election debate as housing dominates run-in

Spanish election: What you need to know and what's at stake

Clashing political visions reflect a divided Spain ahead of election

The final day of campaigning was very much in the shadow of a four-way TV debate the night before that exposed Spain's sharply polarised politics.

All four leaders had solutions for the housing crisis. FeijÃ³o said the PP would build a million homes over four years, while SÃ¡nchez said housing should be a right not an object of speculation and profit.

Watch: Could housing decide Spain's general election?

While the conservatives tried to turn the spotlight on the series of scandals that have overshadowed the Socialists' term in office, SÃ¡nchez and his left-wing ally Ada Colau, who heads Frente Amplio (The Broad Front), focused on women's rights.

The two leaders seized on a moment in the debate when the PP leader accused Colau and the rest of the left of staying quiet during a flood disaster in Valencia, briefly moving his finger to his lips. The word he used "calladita" is a diminutive and can also be used to tell a woman to "keep quiet" in a condescending manner.

SÃ¡nchez reacted immediately and called on him to show "a little respect", accusing him of sexism.

FeijÃ³o sought to clarify what he meant on Friday, and accused Ada Colau of remaining silent during "these years of corruption". His decision to wind up his campaign in Seville was significant, because the wider southern region of Andalusia has 61 seats in Spain's 350-seat parliament, more than any other.

Andalusia is also important because it is run by a coalition made up of the PP and Abascal's Vox, as are three other regions, Extremadura, AragÃ³n, and Castilla y LeÃ³n.

FeijÃ³o has insisted he wants to govern with a majority, backed up by "parliamentary support agreements" with Vox and says he has never spoken to Abascal about giving Vox ministerial power.

SÃ¡nchez took to the stage in Madrid with his wife BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez, and donned a red T-shirt that read "power of the people".

He then went on to praise Colau and her left-wing coalition's campaign. As he was speaking, Colau told a large audience in the south of Madrid that not only would they "not remain silent, we will win", vowing to form another "progressive government".

#7. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

See more photos of the days leading up to Trump’s indictment:

A small group of supporters chanted as former President Donald Trump arrived at his namesake tower on Monday afternoon, with a handful of people waving banners with messages like “Trump won” and “Arrest Biden.”

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Now that he is inside the building — where he launched his presidential bid nearly eight years ago — Trump is expected to meet with lawyers and political advisers on the eve of his arraignment, an aide told CNN.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Former President Donald Trump has arrived at his midtown Manhattan property Trump Tower.

He will stay at Trump Tower tonight and is expected to depart New York immediately after Tuesday’s arraignment to head back to Florida — where he plans to deliver remarks at his Mar-a-lago home.

President Joe Biden told CNN’s Betsy Klein he isn’t worried about unrest due to former President Donald Trump’s criminal indictment in New York.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

Asked if he had faith in the legal system, Biden replied: “Yes.”

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

“Donald Trump is somebody who’s probably tried to avoid his day in court for a very long time — decades. And now, you know, you have this man who is saying to people who support him, ‘You know I am your retribution.’ And he is going to make his grievance their grievance. And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s Audie Cornish noted how Trump ending up in court seemed unavoidable given the “sheer number of investigations against him.”

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan said he’ll likely issue a decision tonight on whether media outlets can broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

CNN is part of the media coalition that asked the judge for permission to broadcast the hearing.

More on this: Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

The Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request and said that it was deferring to the judge to make a decision on how best to manage his courtroom.

Donald Trump — the first former president in history to face criminal charges — has arrived in New York for an expected arraignment on Tuesday after being indicted last week by a Manhattan grand jury.

The former president will stay at Trump Tower tonight, according to a source familiar with his plans. But even before Trump’s appearance, his presence will be felt in the Manhattan courthouse Tuesday, as all trials and most other court activity is being halted before he is slated to arrive.

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

CNN’s Paula Reid contributed reporting to this post.

Senate Homeland Security Chairman Gary Peters said he has not seen any indication that protests in Washington, DC, over former President Donald Trump’s indictment will become dangerous.

“I think you’re always going to be vigilant, so folks will be vigilant. Security and local law enforcement are certainly monitoring it,” he told reporters Monday.

Peters, who is also a member of Senate Democrat leadership, said it is unclear what effect the indictment will have on the Senate. “We don’t know what’s gonna transpire tomorrow, let alone what’s going to happen in the few weeks and months. I think we’d have to take a look at what the indictment is all about.”

Former President Donald Trump has departed Florida and is en route to New York to face criminal charges.

His personalized Boeing 757 took off from the Palm Beach International Airport this afternoon at about 12:59 p.m. ET ahead of his Tuesday appearance in a Manhattan courtroom to be arraigned.

Trump, the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history, is expected to land at New York’s LaGuardia airport around 3 p.m. ET, according to a source familiar with his plans. He will stay at Trump Tower tonight, the source told CNN.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, according to two sources familiar with the case. A New York grand jury handed down the sealed indictment late last week. The charges have yet to be made public.

The former president is not expected to stay in New York City for very long. Immediately after his Tuesday court appearance, Trump plans to return to Florida, the source said, where he has scheduled an event to speak publicly.

Track Trump’s plane from Florida to New York here.

CNN’s Alta D. Spells and Jack Forrest contributed reporting to this post.

New York City Mayor Eric Adams warned GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene to “be on your best behavior” after the Republican firebrand said she will travel to the city to protest former President Donald Trump’s indictment.

Adams made the statement at a press conference as New York security officials detailed plans ahead of Trump’s arrival in Manhattan for his arraignment scheduled on Tuesday.

“While there may be some rabble rousers thinking about coming to our city tomorrow, our message is clear and simple: Control yourselves. New York City is our home, not a playground for your misplaced anger. We have the safest large city in America because we respect the rule of law in New York City. And although we have no specific threats, people like Marjorie Taylor Greene, who is known to spread misinformation and hate speech — she stated she is coming to town. While you’re in town, be on your best behavior,” Adams said.

“As always, we will not allow violence or vandalism of any kind. And if one is caught participating in any act of violence, they will be arrested and held accountable. No matter who you are,” he added.

#4. 'From bad to catastrophic: Iran's justice system was already unfair for many decades'

Amid war with the US and Israel, Iranian protesters continue to face death sentences following what Amnesty International describes as grossly unfair, torture-tainted trials. “The situation has changed from bad to catastrophic,” says Raha Bahreini, Iranian-Canadian human rights lawyer and Amnesty International's researcher on Iran. Bahreini discusses the aftermath of the January 2026 protests, the authorities' efforts to equate peaceful dissent with treason and espionage, and the extrajudicial killings of peaceful protesters. Amnesty International is calling for an international criminal justice mechanism, potentially a special tribunal. “There needs to be criminal accountability for these crimes against humanity.”

After eight years of socialist-led government, Spain is at a crossroads. Can Pedro Sánchez pull off another electoral comeback, or will the conservative Partido Popular make way for the far right into national government for the first time since the end of Franco's 40-year brutal reign? The youth are divided between the left and the far-right Vox party.
